Abstract: Omni-modal models can handle text, images, and audio in one system, but improving all of these abilities together remains difficult. Training a single model on pooled multimodal data often fails to match models specialized for individual modalities. On-policy distillation (OPD) offers a way to combine such specialists: the student generates a response, and a teacher evaluates that same response, so the student learns directly from behaviors it actually produces. Yet using several teachers can introduce competing guidance and improve one modality at the expense of another. We present On-Policy Omni Distillation (OPOD), which routes each student response to the matching text, image, or audio teacher. OPOD keeps teacher guidance only on tokens where the teacher assigns a higher probability than the student, adjusts the influence of each modality teacher independently during training, and asks the routed teacher to assess both the final answer and whether the reasoning supports the correct answer. Across twelve benchmarks and three backbone sizes, OPOD achieves the best average score at every scale, reaching 70.8, 51.7, and 46.2 and exceeding the strongest comparator by 2.1, 1.8, and 1.7 points. On the 30B model, it outperforms both the base model and a counterpart post-trained jointly on pooled multimodal data on all twelve benchmarks, and ranks first or second on eleven even when the individual specialists are included. The specialists are discarded after training, leaving one deployable omni-modal model. These results show that coordinating modality-specific teachers is an effective way to improve a shared model while maintaining cross-modal balance.

</details>


### [78] [Representing Entity Importance in AI Knowledge Systems: A Dual-Signal Framework of Audience Evaluation and Structural Authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20925)
*Shen Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a dual-signal representation for entity importance with separate audience-evaluation and structural-authority dimensions, showing they are non-redundant and should be preserved separately in AI systems rather than collapsed into a single importance score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI knowledge systems often reduce entity importance to a single score based on either human responses or graph structure, which may discard important distinctions needed when AI systems must choose among entities for different tasks.

Method: The study introduces an interpretable dual-signal representation framework, using movie entities as a validation domain. It combines IMDb ratings (audience ranking), Wikidata for entity alignment, and Wikipedia hyperlinks with PageRank (structural authority). Experiments analyze 482 entities and 13,690 relationships.

Result: The two dimensions show statistically significant but weak association (Spearman rho = 0.2275, p < 0.001). Overlap is minimal: only 10% in top 10 and 34% in top 100 entities. Entity-level divergence occurs in both directions, confirming the signals are non-redundant.

Conclusion: Audience evaluation and structural authority are distinct importance signals that should not be collapsed into a single scalar. The framework supports task-aware AI knowledge systems that preserve these signals for context-specific selection or aggregation rather than pre-aggregating them.

Abstract: AI knowledge systems require representations of entity importance for retrieval, recommendation, evidence selection, and knowledge-intensive reasoning. Yet importance is often reduced to a single score derived from either human response or graph structure. Such compression may discard distinctions that matter when an AI system must choose among entities for different tasks. This study introduces an interpretable dual-signal representation in which each entity is characterized by an audience-evaluation dimension and a structural-authority dimension.
  The framework is evaluated using movie entities as an empirical validation domain. IMDb non-commercial datasets provide a rating-based audience ranking, Wikidata supports entity alignment, and English Wikipedia hyperlinks form the knowledge network on which PageRank estimates structural authority. Experiments on 482 entities and 13,690 directed relationships reveal a statistically significant but weak association between the two dimensions (Spearman rho = 0.2275, p < 0.001). Their overlap is only 10% in the top 10 and 34% in the top 100, while entity-level divergence occurs in both directions.
  The results show that audience evaluation and structural authority are non-redundant signals and should not automatically be collapsed into a single scalar notion of importance. The contribution is not a new ranking algorithm or learned embedding, but a minimal knowledge-representation framework and an empirical test of its dimensional necessity. The findings support task-aware AI knowledge systems that preserve distinct importance signals before applying context-specific selection or aggregation.

</details>


### [79] [SciExplore: Evaluating Autonomous Agents from Scientific Navigation to Information Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20926)
*Yinhao Tang,Youqing Fang,Yanan Sun,Wenran Liu,Weiming Zhang,Bin Liu,Kuikun Liu,Wenwei Zhang,Kai Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciExplore is a new benchmark evaluating scientific information-seeking and reasoning capabilities of LLMs, revealing significant limitations in current models as task complexity increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks focus on general-domain retrieval or static scientific QA, failing to assess key capabilities needed for realistic scientific research workflows involving complex information-seeking and reasoning across heterogeneous sources.

Method: Introduce SciExplore benchmark with four task types covering 103 expert-curated tasks across 10+ scientific disciplines: 1) scientific database navigation, 2) ambiguous literature retrieval, 3) missing reference completion, and 4) cross-source structured knowledge synthesis, probing progressively higher-level abilities.

Result: Evaluation of 10+ state-of-the-art LLMs and autonomous agents shows substantial performance gaps, with performance degrading sharply as task complexity increases and extremely low accuracy on most challenging structured synthesis tasks.

Conclusion: Current models and agents have significant limitations in realistic scientific information-seeking scenarios, highlighting the need for improved capabilities in scientific workflow support.

Abstract: Scientific research involves complex information-seeking and reasoning workflows across heterogeneous sources. However, existing benchmarks primarily emphasize general-domain retrieval or static scientific question answering, and therefore fail to assess key capabilities required in realistic scientific research workflows. We introduce SciExplore, a benchmark designed to evaluate scientific information-seeking and reasoning capabilities of LLMs and agents. SciExplore comprises four task types covering 103 expert-curated tasks across more than ten scientific disciplines: scientific database navigation, ambiguous literature retrieval, missing reference completion, and cross-source structured knowledge synthesis, which probe progressively higher-level abilities from entity-level reasoning and document-level identification to evidence-level grounding and domain-level synthesis. We evaluate over ten state-of-the-art LLMs and autonomous agents on SciExplore, revealing substantial performance gaps with performance degrading sharply as task complexity increases and extremely low accuracy on the most challenging structured synthesis tasks. These results highlight significant limitations of current models and agents in realistic scientific information-seeking scenarios.

</details>


### [80] [Clustered Edge Intelligence: Beyond Just Convergence of Edge Computing and AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20937)
*Chinmaya Kumar Dehury,Boris Sedlak,Alaa Saleh,Ilir Murturi,Lauri Loven,Satish Narayana Srirama,Praveen Kumar Donta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Clustered Edge Intelligence (CEI) proposes treating intelligence as first-class entities that can be shared, discovered, and managed across edge-cloud continuum, addressing limitations of current edge AI approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the shift from information age to intelligence age where derived intelligence becomes more valuable than raw data. Current edge intelligence research lacks an intelligence-centric framework where intelligence can be treated as independently manageable entities across heterogeneous edge devices and applications.

Method: Proposes Clustered Edge Intelligence (CEI) - a visionary intelligence-centric approach with three-layer architecture. Examines enabling technologies including intelligence inventories, semantic knowledge representation, communication mechanisms, discoverability tools, observability systems, lifecycle automation, clustering mechanisms, marketplaces, interoperability, and standardization.

Result: Introduces CEI framework that makes intelligence shareable and reusable as first-class entities that can be independently represented, discovered, observed, exchanged, and managed across distributed edge-cloud continuum.

Conclusion: CEI addresses critical research gaps in edge computing by providing an intelligence-centric approach where derived intelligence becomes a manageable entity that can be dynamically clustered, shared, and reused across heterogeneous edge environments.

Abstract: We are moving from an information age to the age of intelligence. A decade, or possibly less than that, data will not be the gold anymore rather the derived intelligence out of the data and the information we posses from the edge of the network. Existing Edge Intelligence research focuses mainly on two directions: using AI for edge resource management and deploying lightweight AI models on edge devices. However, existing edge computing research lacks an intelligence-centric framework in which derived intelligence is treated as a first-class, independently manageable entity that can be described, discovered, observed, shared, reused, and dynamically clustered across heterogeneous edge devices and applications. To address these research gaps, we introduced Clustered Edge Intelligence, a visionary intelligence-centric approach. The aim of CEI is to make intelligence a shareable and reusable first-class entity that can be independently represented, discovered, observed, exchanged, and managed across the distributed edge-cloud continuum. We present a three layer CEI architecture and examine enabling technologies and research dimensions, including intelligence inventories, semantic knowledge representation, communication, discoverability, observability, lifecycle automation, clustering mechanisms, marketplaces, interoperability, and standardization.

</details>


### [81] [From Scalars to Time Series: Rethinking Implicit Neural Representations for Time-Varying Volumetric Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20970)
*Weihan Zhang,Xuan Zhao,Yenwen Peng,Yuqi Chen,Jun Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Time-varying volumetric data INRs can be trained more efficiently using sequence-level supervision over spatial locations' full time series instead of dense spatiotemporal sampling, improving reconstruction quality while reducing computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current implicit neural representations (INRs) for time-varying volumetric data use dense spatiotemporal sampling requiring extensive coordinate-wise optimization, leading to high computational costs and inefficient temporal structure utilization.

Method: Reformulate the problem by representing data as spatially indexed time series and training INRs using sequence-level supervision over each spatial location's full temporal evolution, eliminating dense spatiotemporal sampling. Compatible with existing INR architectures and can be combined with mixture-of-experts architectures.

Result: Consistently improves reconstruction quality while significantly reducing training cost. MoE instantiation further improves reconstruction compared to both the base reformulation and existing MoE-based INR methods, providing better capacity allocation for heterogeneous temporal dynamics.

Conclusion: Dense spatiotemporal sampling is unnecessary for learning time-varying fields; sequence-level supervision over spatial locations' time series offers more efficient and higher-quality INR training with reduced computational requirements.

Abstract: Implicit neural representations (INRs) for time-varying volumetric data are typically trained using dense sampling over spatiotemporal coordinates, where each observation corresponds to a single point in space and time. This coordinate-wise formulation requires extensive sampling during optimization, leading to high computational cost and inefficient use of temporal structure. In this work, we revisit this design choice and show that dense spatiotemporal sampling is not necessary for learning time-varying fields. Instead, we represent the data as a collection of spatially indexed time series and train INRs using sequence-level supervision over each spatial location, rather than coordinate-wise scalar samples. This reformulation eliminates the need for dense spatiotemporal sampling and instead learns each spatial location from its full temporal evolution in a structured manner. We demonstrate that this representation is compatible with a range of existing INR architectures and consistently improves reconstruction quality, while significantly reducing training cost. Furthermore, we show that this formulation can be combined with mixture-of-experts architectures, and that our MoE instantiation further improves reconstruction quality compared to both the base reformulation and existing MoE-based INR methods, providing a stronger capacity allocation under heterogeneous temporal dynamics.

</details>


### [82] [Delivery, Not Storage: Cue-Anchored Working Memory as a Harness Property for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20972)
*Swapnanil Saha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a two-tier memory system for coding agents where the second tier provides automatic, cue-triggered memory delivery without requiring agent choice, unlike current document-based memory systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current coding agents only use voluntary document-based memory where agents must deliberately write and read memories. Human expertise relies on automatic, situationally-cued memory retrieval that happens involuntarily. The paper argues that for long-running agents, automatic cue-based memory is essential and must be handled by the harness, not left to agent choice.

Method: 1) Two-tier design theory based on cognitive literature about memory offloading, incidental encoding, and event-based prospective memory. 2) Cue-anchored memory model with first-class trigger conditions (path, symbol, semantic, event, temporal) evaluated deterministically by the harness. 3) Controlled evaluation on real coding tasks measuring voluntary vs. automatic memory use. 4) Repeated-compaction decay probe testing memory persistence.

Result: Voluntary memory use is near zero even with pre-seeded stores (0 memory operations in 114 turns). Deterministic memory injection works in every seeded run with zero false alarms. 39% of intra-session re-reads recover content from before compaction boundaries. Memories held only in conversation vanish at first summary, while harness-injected memories persist through all compact-resumes.

Conclusion: Delivery, not storage, is the critical factor for agent memory systems. Reliable memory for agents requires automatic, cue-triggered delivery that the agent never has to consciously think about, making it a harness property rather than an agent choice.

Abstract: Coding agents ship with one kind of memory: documents. Instruction files, plan artifacts, and auto-written memory directories are deliberately authored and deliberately retrieved: the agent must choose to write them and choose to read them back. Human expertise runs on a second tier that never gets written down: situationally-bound operational facts (gotchas, locations, local conventions) encoded as a side effect of the work and retrieved involuntarily when the situation cues them. We argue this second tier is the load-bearing one for long-running agents and must be a harness property, not an agent choice. We contribute: (1) a two-tier design theory grounded in the cognitive literature on memory offloading, incidental encoding, and event-based prospective memory, each mapped to an architectural requirement; (2) a cue-anchored memory model where memories carry first-class trigger conditions over a composable vocabulary (path, symbol, semantic, event, temporal), evaluated deterministically by the harness, a composition no surveyed academic or shipped system provides; (3) a controlled evaluation on a real coding task showing that voluntary memory use is near zero even with a pre-seeded store (0 memory operations in 114 turns), that deterministic injection delivered in every seeded run with zero false alarms, and that 39% of intra-session re-reads re-buy content paid for before a compaction boundary; (4) a repeated-compaction decay probe: ten facts held only in conversation vanish at the first summary and stay absent from 106 of 108 compactions, and the deprived agent greps the harness's own session files to rebuild them, while the same facts injected from a harness-owned store arrive intact through all 138 compact-resumes as the final summary carries none. Delivery, not storage, is the product: the reliable memory channel for agents is the one the agent never has to think about.

</details>


### [83] [Beyond Independent Optimization: Compression, MoE Routing, and Quantization Interactions in Multimodal Edge Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20981)
*Jay Gor,Karm Dave,Akshita Abrol,Rajesh Gupta,Sudeep Tanwar,Zhengkui Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reviews recent advances in efficient multimodal models, focusing on how various optimization techniques (visual token compression, KV-cache management, MoE routing, quantization) interact with each other rather than working independently, and discusses hardware-aware design trade-offs for edge deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal inference faces growing constraints not just from model quality or computational cost (FLOPs), but increasingly from the practical costs of handling multimodal representations - including preservation, movement, routing, caching, quantization - under real-world latency, memory, and energy constraints, especially for edge deployment.

Method: The paper conducts a comprehensive literature review of efficient vision-language and multimodal large language models, organizing recent advances around key interacting optimization techniques: visual token compression, video token management, KV-cache optimization, Mixture-of-Experts routing, low-bit quantization, and edge deployment strategies.

Result: The analysis reveals that these optimization techniques cannot be treated independently - they interact in complex ways: visual compression affects downstream features and MoE routing, routing impacts expert utilization and quantization sensitivity, quantization affects router decisions, KV-cache policies determine retained multimodal evidence, and hardware constraints often shift bottlenecks between computation, memory, and communication.

Conclusion: The paper concludes by identifying key design trade-offs (accuracy vs. token budget, static vs. adaptive compression, routing efficiency vs. expert collapse) and introduces Temporal Routing Consistency as a diagnostic for video MoE models. It highlights open research directions including routing-aware compression, cross-modal cache management, hardware-aware co-design, and unified benchmarking for multimodal edge intelligence.

Abstract: Efficient multimodal inference is increasingly constrained not only by model quality or FLOP count, but also by the cost of preserving, moving, routing, caching, and quantizing multimodal representations under latency, memory, and energy constraints. This paper reviews recent advances in efficient vision-language and multimodal large language models, covering visual token compression, video token management, KV-cache optimization, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing, low-bit quantization, edge deployment, and hardware-aware benchmarking. We argue that these techniques cannot be treated as independent optimizations. Visual token compression alters downstream feature distributions and MoE routing decisions, routing behavior affects expert utilization and quantization sensitivity, quantized router logits influence expert assignment, KV-cache policies determine retained multimodal evidence, and hardware constraints often transform computational savings into memory and communication bottlenecks. We organize the literature around these interactions and identify key design trade-offs, including accuracy versus token budget, static versus adaptive compression, sparse routing efficiency versus expert collapse, and low-bit inference versus modality-specific degradation. Finally, we introduce Temporal Routing Consistency as a diagnostic for video MoE models and highlight open research directions in routing-aware compression, cross-modal cache management, hardware-aware co-design, and unified benchmarking for multimodal edge intelligence.

</details>


### [84] [GuardianAgentBench: Where Agents Fail and How to Guard Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20982)
*Vishal Ishwar Naik,Chenyu Xu,Donna Dong,Hussein Hassan,Abhishek Pradhan,Ofer Mendelevitch,Tallat Shafat,Humayun Irshad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GuardianAgentBench (GABench) is a benchmark for evaluating language model agent safety across 580 scenarios in 6 domains, tested on 3 production frameworks. It reveals significant safety issues, with even top models achieving only 74.8% accuracy, and shows that guardrail interventions outperform system-prompt defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As large language model agents increasingly operate autonomously with access to tools and external environments, ensuring their safe and reliable behavior becomes critical. There is a need for comprehensive benchmarking to evaluate and improve agent safety in real-world scenarios.

Method: Created GuardianAgentBench (GABench) with 580 scenarios across six domains, evaluated on three production-ready frameworks (LangChain, LlamaIndex, Vectara). Used rigorous multi-stage validation and five adversarial attack modes. Tested six state-of-the-art models and implemented execution-time guardrail interventions.

Result: Even the strongest configuration achieved only 74.8% overall accuracy. Identified two failure regimes: stronger models under-call required tools, while weaker models mis-select and over-call tools. Performance degrades with tool-set size and sequential turn depth. Guardrail implementation consistently outperformed system-prompt-based defenses, recovering 19.9% of failures at a false positive rate of just 0.5%.

Conclusion: Execution-time structural intervention improves safety without disrupting correct agent behavior, demonstrating that current agent systems have significant safety vulnerabilities that need to be addressed through proper benchmarking and guardrail implementations.

Abstract: As large language model agents increasingly operate autonomously with access to tools and external environments, ensuring their safe and reliable behavior becomes critical. We present GuardianAgentBench (GABench), a benchmark of 580 scenarios across six domains evaluated on three production-ready frameworks: LangChain, LlamaIndex, and Vectara. The benchmark incorporates rigorous multi-stage validation and five adversarial attack modes. Experiments with six state-of-the-art models reveal that even the strongest configuration achieves only 74.8% overall accuracy and expose two distinct failure regimes: stronger models under-call required tools, while weaker models mis-select and over-call tools. Performance degrades monotonically with both tool-set size and sequential turn depth, with long-horizon planning proving the steeper bottleneck. Our guardrail implementation consistently outperforms system-prompt-based defenses across all models, recovering 19.9% of failures at a false positive rate of just 0.5%. These results demonstrate that execution-time structural intervention improves safety without disrupting correct agent behavior.

</details>


### [85] [Workflow-Localized Mechanism Learning: Attribution-Guided Repair and Knowledge Reuse for Structured Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20999)
*Zibin Lin,Shengli Zhang,Taotao Wang,Yihan Xia,Deen Ma,Guofu Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WML introduces workflow-localized mechanism learning to identify failures in workflow nodes, attribute them to specific mechanisms, and optimize by selectively reusing third-party knowledge through a six-module optimization loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing optimizers for agent Skills fail to jointly resolve where failures occur in workflows, which mechanisms cause them, and how relevant knowledge from third-party Skills should be effectively reused locally.

Method: Node-Mechanism Attribution identifies failed workflow nodes, implicated mechanisms, and smallest valid edit targets. Workflow-Guided Skill Optimization (WGSO) is a six-module loop that selects provenance- and scope-aware third-party knowledge, applies bounded patches, evaluates candidates, and stores verified outcomes.

Result: On SpreadsheetBench: 90.33±1.53 and 74.67±3.51 Hard Accuracy with DeepSeek and Qwen3.6-Flash. On WikiTableQuestions: 84.00±2.00 and 83.00±2.00 Denotation Accuracy with transferred Skills. On Compiler-Supported50: highest hard-PASS rate and lowest cost per successful task; compiled execution reduces tokens and calls while retaining most successful tasks.

Conclusion: WML effectively localizes workflow failures, attributes them to mechanisms, and optimizes skill reuse, demonstrating strong performance across multiple benchmarks while enabling efficient knowledge transfer and compiled execution.

Abstract: Agent Skills package reusable procedural knowledge as external artifacts for frozen language-model agents, yet existing optimizers do not jointly resolve where a failure occurs in a workflow, which mechanism caused it, and how relevant knowledge from third-party Skills should be reused locally. We introduce Workflow-Localized Mechanism Learning (WML). Its Node--Mechanism Attribution identifies the failed workflow node, implicated mechanisms, and smallest valid edit target, routing single-mechanism defects to L3 resources and relational defects across mechanisms to L2 composition protocols. A six-module Workflow-Guided Skill Optimization (WGSO) loop then selects provenance- and scope-aware third-party knowledge, applies bounded patches, evaluates candidates, and stores verified outcomes in optimizer-side memory. On SpreadsheetBench, WML reaches 90.33 +/- 1.53 and 74.67 +/- 3.51 Hard Accuracy with DeepSeek and Qwen3.6-Flash, respectively; without additional optimization, the learned Skills transfer to WikiTableQuestions with 84.00 +/- 2.00 and 83.00 +/- 2.00 Denotation Accuracy. On Compiler-Supported50, WML attains both the highest hard-PASS rate and the lowest cost per successful task; compiled execution sharply reduces tokens and calls relative to a direct SkillAgent while retaining most of its successful tasks. Code and artifacts are available at https://github.com/xiaolin9595/workflow-localized-mechanism-learning.

</details>


### [86] [Naju: A Native Discrete State-Space Model with Independent Retention and Writing for Long-Sequence Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21000)
*Hyuk Lim,Seunghyun Yoon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Naju (Native Adaptive Junction Unit) is a novel recurrent architecture that factorizes memory updates to decouple retention from overwriting, solving the fundamental trade-off in long-sequence tracking while maintaining efficiency and outperforming state-space model baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the fundamental trade-off in long-sequence memory tracking between two opposing demands: near-lossless retention of stored bindings over long horizons, and active overwriting of stale ones. Existing efficient baselines tend to solve only one side of this problem well, creating a need for a solution that handles both requirements simultaneously.

Method: Naju introduces a factorization approach that decomposes the recurrent update into three components: an explicit discrete pole (learned forget gate f_n), an independent write gain i_n, and input-dependent write/read maps. This contrasts with traditional state-space models that use zero-order-hold discretization from continuous-time systems. The sigmoid activation on the forget gate ensures stability by construction.

Result: Empirical results show Naju is the only evaluated model that remains strong on both retention and overwriting at 4x training length. It outperforms Mamba baselines in principal comparisons while remaining competitive with Transformers. Naju demonstrates strong performance on WikiText-103 language modeling, Long Range Arena, and multi-query associative recall benchmarks, all while preserving linear-time and linear-memory scaling.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that Naju successfully addresses the fundamental trade-off between retention and overwriting in recurrent neural networks. By decoupling these two mechanisms through its factorization approach, Naju enables both near-lossless retention over long horizons and effective overwriting of stale information. The model demonstrates superior performance across various benchmarks while maintaining computational efficiency properties comparable to other efficient SSMs.

Abstract: Long-sequence memory tracking places two opposing demands on a recurrent state: near-lossless retention of stored bindings over long horizons, and active overwriting of stale ones. In our diagnostic suite, the strongest efficient baselines tend to solve only one side well. Continuous-time-parameterized state-space models (SSMs) such as Mamba obtain their discrete recurrence by zero-order-hold discretization of a continuous-time system; we argue that this detour is unnecessary for memory tracking and parameterize the discrete transition directly. Naju (Native Adaptive Junction Unit) factorizes the recurrent update, schematically $x_n = f_n\odot x_{n-1} + i_n\odot(B_n u_n)$, into an explicit discrete pole (a learned forget gate $f_n$), an independent write gain $i_n$, and input-dependent write/read maps. Since the sigmoid pole satisfies $0<f_n<1$, each frozen local coordinate is Schur-stable by construction, and the full time-varying recurrence satisfies a fading-memory/BIBO bound under uniform boundedness assumptions, with no stability regularizer. We formalize the key structural limitation of coupled designs: any non-expansive complementary single-gate recurrence ties the effective retention $r$ and write gain $w$ through $|r|+w\le 1$, so near-complete retention forces weak writing; decoupling $f_n$ from $i_n$ removes this constraint. Empirically, Naju is the only evaluated model that remains strong on both retention and overwriting at 4x the training length. Beyond the diagnostic suite, we evaluate Naju on WikiText-103 language modeling, Long Range Arena, and multi-query associative recall. Across these settings, Naju consistently combines strong long-range memory with competitive or superior performance, outperforming the Mamba baselines in the principal comparisons while remaining competitive with the Transformer and preserving linear-time, linear-memory scaling.

</details>


### [87] [Reexamining zero-shot summarization: Empirical investigation of trustworthiness of LLM-summarizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21010)
*Vasudha Bhatnagar,Purnima Bindal,Vikas Kumar,Raj Kumari Bahl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-level diagnostic protocol for benchmarking LLM-based summarizers by measuring the stability of generated summaries, revealing significant variability in LLM summarization quality across different models and document genres.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The stochastic nature of large language models raises concerns about the stability and trustworthiness of LLM-generated summaries, especially as these summaries proliferate in educational settings where students and researchers use them to summarize complex academic materials.

Method: A novel two-level diagnostic protocol: 1) Document-level stability analysis involving multiple LLM-generated summaries under controlled conditions with computed stability coefficients, scoring for semantic and factual alignment with original documents; 2) Corpus-level analysis consolidating observations from a stratified document sample to estimate an overall stability index as a proxy for trustworthiness.

Result: Empirical investigation of three LLM-summarizers across three document genres revealed statistically significant differences in generation-level variability among LLMs across summary evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: The study advances LLM-summarization research by providing evidential recognition of the stability problem in LLM-generated summaries and motivates further research toward developing more robust, reliable, and trustworthy LLM-summarizers.

Abstract: Zero-shot summarization using Large Language Models (LLMs) has significantly advanced the abstractive summarization task by producing coherent and fluent summaries. However, underlying stochasticity of the large language models raises concerns about the stability and trustworthiness of the LLM-generated summaries. This issue has become increasingly important due to proliferation of LLM-generated summaries in educational settings, where students and researchers summarize complex academic materials in zero-shot manner.
  We propose a novel two-level diagnostic protocol for benchmarking LLM-summarizers based on the stability of the generated summaries. At the lower level, document-level stability analysis is performed over multiple LLM-summaries generated under controlled environment, and the stability coefficient is computed. Each generated summary is scored for semantic and factual alignment with the original document, enabling estimation of stability along more than one dimensions. At the next level, observations from a stratified sample of documents drawn from the corpus are consolidated to estimate the stability index of the LLM-summarizer, which is the proxy for its trustworthiness.
  Our empirical investigation of three LLM-summarizers across three genres of documents reveals statistically significant differences in the generation-level variability among LLMs across summary evaluation metrics. This study advances the LLM-summarization research by evidential recognition of the stability problem in LLM-summaries and motivates further research towards development of robust, reliable and trustworthy LLM-summarizers.

</details>


### [88] [EmoAgent-R1: Towards Multimodal Emotion Understanding with Reinforcement Learning-based Dynamic Agent Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21013)
*Lihuang Fang,Yuchen Zou,kebin Jin,Jinghui Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EmoAgent-R1: A reinforcement learning framework with dynamic agent specialization for multimodal emotion recognition that improves emotion reasoning and generalization through adaptive prompt selection and optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLLM-based multimodal emotion recognition methods use fixed prompts, which fail to capture the dynamic and complex nature of emotional cues in multimodal inputs, limiting their ability to adapt to varying emotional sources and contexts.

Method: A two-phase approach: 1) Cold start training with synthetic chain-of-thought and agent routing data to build preliminary emotion recognition and reasoning abilities; 2) Reinforcement learning-based optimization with dynamic agent specialization, featuring agent selection and specialization in a two-step workflow, using novel Progressive Group-Relative Policy Optimization (P-GRPO) for fine-grained learning signals.

Result: The method demonstrates superior performance on multimodal emotion recognition benchmarks, showing stronger emotion reasoning capabilities and improved optimization stability compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: The EmoAgent-R1 framework successfully addresses the limitations of fixed-prompt MLLMs for emotion recognition by enabling dynamic adaptation to complex emotional contexts through reinforcement learning and specialized agents, advancing the field of multimodal emotion understanding.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved impressive performance in multimodal emotion recognition (MER) tasks and lifted MER to a new level that is complex emotion understanding with advanced video understanding abilities and natural language description. However, existing MLLM-based methods often use a fixed prompt to perceive the emotions, ignoring the dynamicity and complexity of the emotion source in the multimodal inputs. To address these issues, we propose a novel Reinforcement Learning-based Dynamic Agent Specialization framework (\textbf{EmoAgent-R1}) to optimize the emotion recognition, reasoning, and generalization abilities of an MLLM with dynamic agent specialization based on reinforcement learning. Specifically, we first adopt a cold start strategy to endow an MLLM with preliminary emotion recognition, reasoning, and agent routing ability by training with synthetic answer-conditioned chain-of-thought data and agent routing data. Then, we further train the MLLM with reinforcement learning to perceive emotions in a two-step agentic workflow with agent selection and agent specialization. To effectively train EmoAgent-R1, we propose a novel Progressive Group-Relative Policy Optimization (P-GRPO) to combine group-based relative advantages with a PMI-inspired progressive token-level modulation to transform sparse rewards into fine-grained learning signals, mitigating the coarse-grained uniform credit assignment issue in GRPO. Extensive experiments on MER benchmarks demonstrate the superiority of our EmoAgent-R1 in stronger emotion reasoning performance and improved optimization stability.

</details>


### [89] [Faster IndexTTS-2: Accelerating and Streaming Autoregressive Zero-Shot Text-to-Speech Synthesis on GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21042)
*Muyang Du,Shuang Yu,Junjie Lai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Faster IndexTTS-2 accelerates the state-of-the-art IndexTTS-2 TTS model using NVIDIA TensorRT optimizations, achieving up to 5× speedup on GPT component and 3.6× end-to-end while maintaining quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive text-to-speech models like IndexTTS-2 achieve high naturalness but suffer from slow inference speeds due to sequential token generation, limiting their deployment in production applications requiring low latency.

Method: Accelerates all neural network components of IndexTTS-2 using NVIDIA TensorRT and TensorRT-LLM for GPU deployment, adds streaming synthesis support for low-latency applications, and enables batched inference across all components to maximize GPU utilization.

Result: Achieves up to 5.0× speedup on the autoregressive GPT component and 3.6× end-to-end acceleration on the Seed-TTS benchmark for both English and Chinese, with minimal degradation in word error rate, speaker similarity, and naturalness.

Conclusion: The methodology provides a practical reference for efficiently accelerating similar autoregressive speech models on GPUs, making high-quality TTS more suitable for production deployment with improved inference speed.

Abstract: Autoregressive text-to-speech models achieve strong naturalness but suffer from slow inference due to sequential token generation, limiting their deployment in production applications that require low latency. IndexTTS-2 is a state-of-the-art autoregressive TTS model consisting of a GPT, a flow-matching Diffusion Transformer, and a vocoder. Despite its high synthesis quality, its inference speed barely reaches real-time without streaming or batching support. We present Faster IndexTTS-2, which accelerates all neural network components of IndexTTS-2 for production deployment on GPUs using NVIDIA TensorRT and TensorRT-LLM. Faster IndexTTS-2 also enables streaming synthesis for latency-sensitive interactive applications, and batched inference across all components to maximize GPU utilization. Experiments on the Seed-TTS benchmark for both English and Chinese demonstrate up to 5.0$\times$ speedup on the autoregressive GPT and 3.6$\times$ end-to-end, with minimal degradation in word error rate, speaker similarity, and naturalness. Our methodology provides a practical reference for efficiently accelerating similar autoregressive speech models on GPUs.

</details>


### [90] [Can Generative Recommendation Reach Cold Items? A Temporal Perspective on Semantic-ID Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21101)
*Jie Peng,Yanping Zheng,Zhewei Zhe,Bin Tong,Guan Wang,Bo Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation struggles with temporal cold-start scenarios involving unseen tokens and unsupported semantic paths, despite enabling token recombination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Semantic-ID (SID) based generative recommendation systems can recombine shared semantic tokens, but it's unclear if this capability extends to temporal open-token cold-start scenarios where new items enter with unseen tokens or weakly supported SID paths.

Method: The study uses an absolute-time temporal protocol separating seen and unseen targets, and conducts seen/unseen-hit analysis, coldness taxonomy analysis, and oracle-prefix probing to diagnose cold item reachability at the token level in SID-based models.

Result: Current SID-based models can sometimes reach future items supported by observed tokens and prefixes, but struggle with unseen atomic tokens and unsupported SID paths. SID generation functions as hierarchical semantic bucketing where early tokens select coarse semantic regions and later tokens refine item-specific paths.

Conclusion: SID generation is compositional but not fully open-ended, suggesting future directions in more independent SID spaces, scoring-based interfaces, and dynamic textual context for better cold-start performance.

Abstract: Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation represents items as sequences of shared semantic tokens, enabling token recombination beyond isolated item IDs. However, closed-world recombination does not necessarily imply temporal open-token cold-start induction, where new items enter the item catalog with unseen atomic tokens or weakly supported SID paths. In this work, we revisit SID-based generative recommendation under an absolute-time temporal protocol that separates seen and unseen targets and diagnoses the cold item reachability at the token level. Through seen/unseen-hit analysis, coldness taxonomy, and oracle-prefix probing, we show that current SID-based models can occasionally reach future items supported by observed tokens and prefixes, but struggle with unseen atomic tokens and unsupported SID paths. We further explain this boundary by interpreting SID generation as hierarchical semantic bucketing: early tokens select coarse semantic regions, while later tokens refine item-specific paths. These findings show that SID generation is compositional but not fully open-ended, and suggest future directions in more independent SID spaces, scoring-based interfaces, and dynamic textual context.

</details>


### [91] [AttriMem: Attribution-Guided Process Feedback for Agent Memory Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21106)
*Qinfeng Li,Yuntai Bao,Xinyan Yu,Hongze Chen,Wenqi Zhang,Xuhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AttriMem: An attribution-guided process-feedback framework that uses token-level contributions to learn memory-construction policies for LLM agents, improving performance on long-horizon dialogue QA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory-construction methods for LLM agents have limitations: heuristic methods rely on subjective rules that may misalign with downstream objectives, while RL-based methods use coarse outcome-level rewards that create a fine-grained credit assignment problem, unable to identify which specific memory contents support final answers.

Method: AttriMem is an attribution-guided process-feedback framework for RL-based memory construction. It augments global outcome rewards with local rewards derived from token-level contributions to the final answer, providing fine-grained feedback on memory decisions without requiring ground-truth intermediate targets.

Result: Experiments on long-horizon dialogue question answering show AttriMem outperforms retrieval-based, heuristic, and RL-based baselines. It also demonstrates generalization across benchmarks and answer models, and stabilizes RL optimization.

Conclusion: AttriMem effectively addresses the credit assignment bottleneck in RL-based memory construction by using token-level attribution to provide fine-grained process feedback, leading to better memory policies that improve LLM agent performance on complex dialogue tasks.

Abstract: Effective memory is crucial for LLM agents, yet constructing it effectively remains challenging. A memory-construction policy decides what information to extract, store, update, compress, or discard as interactions accumulate. Heuristic memory methods rely on subjective, task-specific rules, which can misalign with downstream objectives and limit cross-task adaptability. RL-based methods, by contrast, learn from task feedback but mainly use outcome- or module-level rewards. These coarse signals indicate task success but cannot identify which intermediate memory contents support the final answer, creating a fine-grained credit-assignment bottleneck. However, constructing such process feedback is prohibitively difficult because intermediate memory decisions lack unique ground-truth targets, while the appropriate credit varies with the agent's uncertain reasoning trajectory and therefore cannot be specified in advance. We propose AttriMem, an attribution-guided process-feedback framework for learning memory-construction policies with RL. AttriMem augments the global outcome reward with local rewards derived from token-level contributions to the final answer. Experiments on long-horizon dialogue question answering show that AttriMem outperforms retrieval-based, heuristic, and RL-based baselines, generalizes across benchmarks and answer models, stabilizes RL optimization.

</details>


### [92] [V-DEAL: Diagnosing Video Safety De-Calibration as an Understanding-Refusal Coupling Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21151)
*Zhetong Zhang,Honghao Fu,Miao Xu,Yiwei Wang,Yujun Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Video LLMs are vulnerable to attacks where harmful videos with benign queries bypass safety filters more effectively than harmful videos with harmful queries, requiring new diagnostic and intervention methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video Large Language Models are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, making safety alignment critical. The authors discovered a counterintuitive vulnerability where harmful videos paired with benign queries achieve higher attack success rates than harmful videos with explicitly harmful queries.

Method: The authors developed V-DEAL, a three-level diagnostic framework that analyzes model failures across behavior, understanding, and internal representations. They tested six Video LLMs on three public benchmarks, used hidden-state analysis to examine refusal tendencies, and introduced a prompt injection intervention method to reduce attack success rates.

Result: Models correctly recognize harmful video content with over 81% accuracy, yet the average attack success rate still reaches 48.33% when harmful videos are paired with benign queries. Visual understanding activates weaker refusal tendency than textual understanding. The prompt injection intervention method reduces attack success rates by an average of 48.24 percentage points, achieving performance comparable to prior fine-tuning methods.

Conclusion: Video LLMs have significant safety vulnerabilities where visual understanding fails to trigger adequate refusal mechanisms. The V-DEAL framework provides diagnostic insights, and prompt injection interventions offer an effective, practical solution to address these safety risks in deployed Video LLMs.

Abstract: As Video Large Language Models are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, ensuring their safety alignment has become critical. Counterintuitively, we find that harmful videos paired with benign queries achieve higher attack success rates than the same videos paired with explicitly harmful queries. To understand the underlying mechanism of this vulnerability, we present V-DEAL, a three-level diagnostic framework that jointly analyzes this failure across model behaviour, understanding, and internal representations. By progressively ruling out perception failure and quantifying the model's internal refusal tendency, V-DEAL provides a new diagnostic perspective for analyzing the underlying mechanism of the observed vulnerability. We tested six Video LLMs on three public benchmarks and observed that models correctly recognize harmful video content with over 81\% accuracy, yet the average attack success rate still reaches 48.33\% under the condition pairing harmful videos with benign queries. Hidden-state analysis further shows that visual understanding activates a weaker refusal tendency than textual understanding. Furthermore, we introduce a prompt injection intervention method that reduces attack success rates by an average of 48.24 percentage points and achieves performance comparable to prior fine-tuning-based methods, providing an effective and practical means to address such safety risks in Video LLMs.

</details>


### [93] [Safeguards for Speech2Speech LLM-Assistants: A Case Study in Automotive Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21180)
*Gregor Endler,Sebastian Kraus,Lukas Stappen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates two implementation approaches for speech-to-speech guardrails (transcript-based and tool-based) in automotive contexts, finding both insufficient for industrial deployment due to latency issues and technical impediments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech-to-speech conversational assistants enable natural, humanlike in-car dialogue experiences but lack programmable domain-specific safeguards, creating a need for effective guardrails in automotive applications.

Method: The paper discusses two implementation approaches for S2S guardrails: transcript-based (analyzing text transcripts) and tool-based (using specialized tools/interfaces), followed by empirical evaluation of both strategies.

Result: Both transcript-based and tool-based guardrail strategies are insufficient for industrial deployment due to prohibitive latency (0 to 1.4 seconds delay per answer even for cheap checks) and technical impediments like potentially non-deterministic tool call behavior.

Conclusion: Current S2S guardrail approaches are inadequate for automotive deployment, highlighting the need for further research to address latency, determinism, and other open challenges in this domain.

Abstract: Recent advances have introduced speech-to-speech (S2S) conversational assistants capable of producing natural-sounding interactions, including non-verbal cues like tonality and mood. In the automotive domain, this enables intuitive and humanlike in-car dialogue experiences. However, integrating these end-to-end assistants limits architectural options for programmable domain-specific safeguards. This paper discusses two implementation approaches for S2S guardrails: transcript-based and tool-based. Through an empirical evaluation, we demonstrate that both strategies are insufficient for industrial deployment in most cases due to prohibitive latency (delaying each answer by 0 to 1.4 seconds even for computationally cheap checks) and technical impediments (like potentially non-deterministic tool call behavior). Finally, we outline open challenges for S2S guardrails in the automotive context.

</details>


### [94] [Explaining Weather Bulletins via ILP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21184)
*Enrico Santi,Alessandro Dal Palù,Agostino Dovier,Talissa Dreossi,Andrea Formisano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Using inductive logic programming (FastLAS2) to automatically generate interpretable hypotheses that explain expert meteorologists' weather forecast decisions from raw meteorological data and bulletin ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create an interpretable system that can explain the rationale behind human experts' weather forecast decisions, specifically why certain symbols are chosen in weather bulletin pictograms, helping to clarify and make transparent the decision-making process of meteorologists at OSMER FVG.

Method: A pipeline that starts with simulated meteorological raw data and expert bulletins (ground truth), extracts data as ASP (Answer Set Programming) facts, generates ILP examples, and uses FastLAS2 (an inductive logic programming framework) to infer explanatory hypotheses. The hypotheses are then translated into natural language.

Result: The approach successfully generates simple, interpretable hypotheses that explain the weather forecasts issued by human experts, particularly the rationale behind their choices of specific symbols in weather bulletin pictograms. The system produces natural language explanations of meteorologists' decision-making.

Conclusion: The proposed approach is general and applicable to weather bulletins from various sources and regions, demonstrating how modern ILP frameworks can create transparent, explainable systems for complex expert decision-making in meteorology.

Abstract: Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) originated within the Logic Programming community in the Nineties as a framework for combining symbolic learning with declarative knowledge representation. Nowadays, mature ILP frameworks exist and they are capable of learning complex, non-monotonic hypotheses, thus broadening both the modeling capabilities and the scope of real-world applications of ILP. This work is primarily based on the FastLAS2 framework and aims to generate simple, interpretable hypotheses to help clarify the weather bulletins issued by OSMER FVG, the Regional Meteorological Observatory of the Italian region of Friuli Venezia-Giulia. In this paper we present a pipeline that, starting from simulated meteorological raw data and from OSMERs' bulletins (used as ground truth), extracts data as ASP facts and generates ILP examples. From such examples an explanatory hypothesis is then inferred via FastLAS2. Such a hypothesis (translated into natural language) explains the weather forecast issued by human experts, and in particular the rationale behind experts' choices of specific symbols in the bulletin pictogram (the symbol-annotated meteorological map of the forecast). The proposed approach is general, not specific to any particular region and it can equally be applied to bulletins from other sources and to different regions.

</details>


### [95] [Differentiable Logic Programming to Mitigate Reasoning Shortcuts in Neurosymbolic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21185)
*Akihiro Takemura,Katsumi Inoue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeSy systems often suffer from shortcut reasoning; this paper proposes matrix-based differentiable logic programming to mitigate constraint satisfaction and cognition shortcuts through one-to-one grounding of neural outputs to logical atoms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neurosymbolic systems combine neural networks with logical reasoning for generalization and interpretability, but they are vulnerable to shortcut reasoning behaviors: constraint satisfaction shortcuts (where constraints are satisfied without achieving intended tasks) and cognition shortcuts (where biased data leads to semantically incorrect concept mappings despite logically sound inference).

Method: The authors propose a novel method using matrix-based differentiable logic programming. They build on recent matrix-based logic programming semantics, introduce design elements to mitigate shortcuts (including unified encoding of rules and constraints in a single matrix), and explore connections to fuzzy logic t-norms while comparing their gradient flow properties.

Result: Experiments on MNIST variants show that one-to-one grounding of neural outputs to logical atoms significantly reduces both types of reasoning shortcuts compared to previous methods that rely on soft probability distributions. Architectural choices in coupling symbolic knowledge with neural learning play a critical role in shortcut mitigation.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that proper architectural design in neurosymbolic systems, specifically using matrix-based differentiable logic programming with one-to-one grounding, effectively mitigates reasoning shortcuts and highlights the importance of how symbolic knowledge is coupled with neural learning.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic (NeSy) systems integrate neural networks with logical reasoning to achieve both generalization and interpretability, but recent work has shown they are susceptible to shortcut reasoning behaviors.  We propose a novel method using matrix-based differentiable logic programming to mitigate reasoning shortcuts in two phenomena: constraint satisfaction shortcuts, where constraints are satisfied without achieving the intended task, and cognition shortcuts, where biased data leads to semantically incorrect concept mappings despite logically sound inference. Building on recent matrix-based logic programming semantics, we introduce design elements to mitigate shortcuts, including a unified encoding of rules and constraints in a single matrix.  We also identify connections to fuzzy logic t-norms and empirically compare their gradient flow properties. Through carefully designed experiments on MNIST variants, we show that one-to-one grounding of neural outputs to logical atoms significantly reduces both shortcut types compared to previous methods that rely on soft probability distributions.  We then confirm that architectural choices in coupling symbolic knowledge with neural learning play a critical role in shortcut mitigation.

</details>


### [96] [Identifying Good Rules for Efficient SAT Encodings of Single-Constant Multiplication Using Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21188)
*Chufeng Jiang,Neng-Fa Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic framework that accelerates SAT encoding for Single Constant Multiplication by using graph neural networks to predict promising operator types, achieving significant speedups and memory reductions while preserving near-optimal encoding quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Single Constant Multiplication problem is NP-hard and essential for hardware design. While dynamic programming methods can produce near-optimal SAT encodings, they have high encoding costs and scalability issues for large constants.

Method: The authors propose a neuro-symbolic framework that combines a graph neural network model with symbolic search. The GNN predicts promising operator types from constant decompositions, and the resulting confidence scores are used to prune no-good choices during the symbolic search process.

Result: On unseen 17-32 bit constants, the approach achieves: 1-2 orders of magnitude reduction in encoding time, over 97% reduction in memory usage, order-of-magnitude decrease in branching, while maintaining near-optimal encoding quality in terms of additions.

Conclusion: Learning-guided symbolic strategies can significantly improve the scalability and efficiency of SCM encoding, demonstrating that neuro-symbolic approaches can effectively combine machine learning with symbolic optimization for hardware design problems.

Abstract: The Single Constant Multiplication problem is a fundamental NP-hard optimization task in hardware design, which seeks to decompose a fixed constant using only additions, subtractions, and bit-shifts. Although dynamic programming methods can produce near-optimal SAT encodings for SCM, their encoding cost remains high for large constants. We propose a neuro-symbolic framework that accelerates SCM SAT encoding by identifying good rules for guiding operator selection during decomposition. Our approach employs a graph neural network model to predict promising operator types from constant decompositions, and exploits the resulting confidence scores to prune no-good choices in the symbolic search. Experimental results on unseen 17-32 bit constants demonstrate one to two orders of magnitude reductions in encoding time, over 97% reduction in memory usage, and an order-of-magnitude decrease in branching, while preserving near-optimal encoding quality in terms of additions. These results show that learning-guided symbolic strategies can significantly improve the scalability and efficiency of SCM encoding. Our code and data are publicly available at: https://github.com/Chufeng-Jiang/SCM_MLDP

</details>


### [97] [Bound-Founded Semantics for Answer Set Programming with Difference Constraints: Preliminary Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21201)
*Pedro Cabalar,Jorge Fandinno,Nicolas Rühling,Torsten Schaub,Sebastian Schellhorn,Philipp Wanko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified logical framework based on many-sorted HTb for characterizing equilibrium models in ASP with linear constraints, particularly applied to difference constraints and formalizing foundedness for numeric variables to understand hybrid solvers like clingo[DL].


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hybrid ASP solvers with linear constraints rely on disparate semantic underpinnings without a unified logical foundation, creating a gap in understanding the semantic roots of different systems' behaviors.

Method: Introduce a many-sorted variant of the Bound-founded Logic of Here-and-There (HTb) to create a versatile framework for characterizing equilibrium models across various ASP extensions with linear constraints. Formalize foundedness for numeric variables and analyze how different hybrid systems justify constraint atoms.

Result: Uncover the semantic roots of varying behaviors in systems like clingo[DL], clingcon, and flingo. Develop a single, consistent framework that formalizes the foundations of current systems and enables rigorous study of program simplifications.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a unified logical foundation for ASP with linear constraints, not only explaining current systems but also facilitating future integration of diverse semantic principles and program analysis.

Abstract: While the integration of linear constraints has significantly expanded the reach of Answer Set Programming (ASP), existing hybrid solvers often rely on disparate semantic underpinnings that lack a unified logical foundation. We address this gap by introducing a many-sorted variant of the Bound-founded Logic of Here-and-There (HTb), providing a versatile framework capable of characterizing equilibrium models across a wide spectrum of alternative semantics for extensions of ASP with linear constraints. We apply this framework to the setting of difference constraints, focusing on the semantic characterization of clingo[DL]. Central to our approach is the formalization of foundedness for numeric variables. By investigating how different hybrid systems - such as clingo[DL], clingcon, and flingo - justify constraint atoms, we uncover the semantic roots of their varying behaviors. This investigation results in a single, consistent framework that not only formalizes the foundations of current systems like clingo[DL] but also facilitates the rigorous study of program simplifications and the future integration of diverse semantic principles.

</details>


### [98] [A New Well-Supported Semantics for Description Logic Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21203)
*Spencer Killen,Jia-Huai You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new semantics for description logic programs that maintains well-supportedness while reducing computational complexity from Σ₂ᴾ to NP-complete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The current well-supported semantics for description logic programs has two limitations: increased computational complexity (Σ₂ᴾ) for consistency checking and lack of a reduct transformation characterization, motivating a new approach.

Method: Proposes a new semantics that evaluates ontological atoms more strictly than current semantics, characterized using a fixpoint operator and reduct-based transformation.

Result: New semantics keeps consistency problem NP-complete (not Σ₂ᴾ), identifies syntactic class where new semantics equals current semantics, maintains well-supportedness with stricter notion.

Conclusion: The new semantics is preferable due to its computational advantages (NP-complete complexity), reduct characterization, and similarity to traditional logic programming while preserving well-supportedness.

Abstract: Description logic programs are a powerful formalism for combining rules with ontologies. The well-supported semantics for description logic programs ensures that no answer sets rely on cyclic dependencies. Most popular semantics for logic programming have this property of well-supportedness. We recognize two limitations of the current well-supported semantics for DL programs: its increased computational complexity for the consistency problem and its lack of a reduct transformation characterization.  In this work, we present a new semantics which evaluates ontological atoms more strictly than the current semantics. This keeps the complexity of its consistency problem NP-complete, rather than increasing it to the second level of the polynomial hierarchy. Additionally, we identify a syntactic class of description logic programs for which our new semantics is equivalent to the current semantics. We characterize our semantics using a fixpoint operator and a reduct-based transformation. Our new semantics is a strict subset of the current well-supported semantics, so it maintains the prior notion  of well-supportedness while inducing its own stricter notion. We prefer our new notion of well-supportedness due to its similarities with logic programming.

</details>


### [99] [How Rules Represent Causal Knowledge: Causal Modeling with Probabilistic Logic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21208)
*Kilian Rueckschloss,Felix Weitkaemper*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper translates Pearl's causal reasoning approach into probabilistic logic programming (PLP), proposing a formal causal semantics with interventions that aligns with P-log for stratified ProbLog programs but may differ for non-stratified cases and other PLP formalisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pearl's causal theory is limited to Bayesian networks/causal models and acyclic relationships, making it difficult to apply to other formalisms like probabilistic logic programming without misinterpretation or inconsistency.

Method: The paper brings Pearl's causality approach to PLP by aligning programs with philosophical foundations that don't rely on temporal notions (assuming simultaneous events), and proposes a formal causal semantics with notion of intervention and implementation.

Result: The proposed semantics coincides with P-log semantics for stratified ProbLog programs, but the two may differ in non-stratified cases and for other PLP formalisms.

Conclusion: The work successfully translates Pearl's causal reasoning to probabilistic logic programming, providing a formal framework that handles interventions while maintaining consistency with existing approaches in some cases but showing divergence in others.

Abstract: Pearl famously argues that causal knowledge enables the prediction of intervention effects. By contrast, purely descriptive knowledge supports only conclusions drawn from observations. His theory of causality, however, is developed exclusively within Bayesian networks and causal models. Consequently, it is largely restricted to acyclic causal relationships, and transferring its ideas to other formalisms risks misinterpretation or inconsistency.
  This paper brings Pearl's approach to causality into probabilistic logic programming (PLP). To this end, such programs are aligned with philosophical foundations established in prior work that do not rely on temporal notions; that is, all relevant events are assumed to occur simultaneously. A formal causal semantics for these programs, together with a notion of intervention and an implementation, is proposed. It is shown that this semantics coincides with the P-log semantics for stratified ProbLog programs, while the two may differ in the non-stratified case and for other PLP formalisms.

</details>


### [100] [ICAE-Bench: Evaluating Coding Agents as Interactive Project Builders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21217)
*Zhongyuan Peng,Dan Huang,Chuyu Zhang,Caijun Xu,Changyi Xiao,Shibo Hong,David Lo,Lin Qiu,Xuezhi Cao,Jiyuan He,Yixin Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICAE-Bench is a new benchmark for evaluating coding agents in interactive project-building scenarios, moving beyond static tasks to simulate real-world development with fuzzy requirements and automated user interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current coding benchmarks evaluate agents on static, fully specified tasks, but modern "vibe-coding" workflows require agents to transform incomplete product intent into working software through planning, clarification, debugging, and repository-level construction. Existing benchmarks haven't kept up with this shift.

Method: The benchmark starts from fuzzy product requirements and uses an automated User Agent to simulate dynamic development. Three key designs: 1) Tasks derive ambiguity from precise real open-source repositories with executable behavior, 2) Grounded interaction through User Agent Data for reproducible simulation, 3) Standardized black-box tests with multi-dimensional diagnostics for fair evaluation.

Result: ICAE-Bench creates a realistic yet evaluable setting for testing coding agents' abilities to handle incomplete requirements, interact with users, and build complete software projects from vague specifications.

Conclusion: This benchmark addresses the gap between traditional static evaluation and modern coding workflows, providing a comprehensive framework to assess coding agents' performance in real-world interactive development scenarios.

Abstract: The recent emergence of vibe-coding workflows is changing what coding agents are expected to do. Instead of merely completing code under fully specified instructions, agents are increasingly expected to transform incomplete product intent into working software by combining various abilities including planning, requirement clarification, tool use, debugging, and repository-level construction. Yet existing benchmarks have not fully caught up with this shift, evaluating agents on static, fully specified tasks.
  In this paper, we introduce ICAE-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating coding agents under interactive project-building settings. The basic idea is to start from a fuzzy product requirement, simulating the dynamic paradigm with an automated User Agent. To make this setting both realistic and evaluable, ICAE-Bench introduces three key designs. First, to avoid the ambiguity of unconstrained fuzzy requirements, each task derives ambiguity from a precise real open-source repository with executable behavior. Second, to ensure high-quality and reproducible user simulation, ICAE-Bench grounds interaction through User Agent Data, allowing the User Agent to reveal hidden constraints without inventing new requirements or leaking implementation artifacts. Third, to evaluate open-ended repositories fairly, ICAE-Bench uses standardized black-box tests together with multi-dimensional diagnostics, including functional correctness, semantic and API similarity, structural fidelity, design quality, and interaction quality.

</details>


### [101] [Logic Programming Semantics for Causal Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21233)
*Felix Weitkämper*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper explores connections between logic programming semantics and causal processes, showing stable models correspond to eventual states from neutral starting points while supported models represent states reachable from arbitrary starting points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research is motivated by challenging modeling issues in life sciences, seeking to establish relationships between logic programming semantics and causal processes, contributing to the discussion about appropriate semantics for logic programming as a causal rule language.

Method: The authors investigate the relationship between logic programming semantics (specifically stable models and supported models) and the eventual states of causal processes compatible with those logic programs, adding a temporal perspective to existing interpretations.

Result: The paper shows that stable models of positive logic programs correspond to eventual states of processes starting from neutral states and continuing undisturbed indefinitely, while supported models describe eventual states reachable from arbitrary starting points.

Conclusion: This work contributes to understanding logic programming as a causal rule language by adding a temporal perspective to recent interpretations of stable and supported model semantics from an explanatory viewpoint of causality, providing insights relevant to life sciences modeling.

Abstract: Motivated by challenging modelling issues in the life sciences, we investigate the relationship between logic programming semantics and the eventual states of causal processes compatible with those logic programs. More precisely, we show that while stable models of positive logic programs correspond to the eventual states of processes commencing from a neutral state and continuing undisturbed indefinitely, supported models describe the eventual states reachable from arbitrary starting points. This also contributes to the discussion of the appropriate semantics for logic programming as a causal rule language, adding a temporal perspective to recent interpretations of the stable and supported model semantics from an explanatory viewpoint of causality.

</details>


### [102] [BasketEvent: Understanding Who Did What and When in Basketball Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21267)
*Yu Zhang,Jiayuan Rao,Haoning Wu,Weidi Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BasketEvent dataset and PlayNet framework for player-centric basketball event understanding with spatial and temporal grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods treat spatial perception and semantic recognition as isolated tasks, failing to ground events to individual players or pinpoint temporal boundaries within complex basketball dynamics.

Method: Introduces BasketEvent dataset with player-grounded event labels and precise temporal intervals. Proposes PlayNet framework that tracks entities, associates player identities, models player-player, player-ball, and court interactions, and aggregates sparse temporal evidence via gated pooling.

Result: PlayNet significantly outperforms representative video-level and crop-based baselines, demonstrating superiority of player-centric modeling for fine-grained sports video understanding.

Conclusion: Player-centric modeling is essential for comprehensive basketball video understanding that resolves what events occur, who is responsible, and when key evidence appears within complex collective dynamics.

Abstract: Comprehensive basketball video understanding requires resolving not only what event occurs, but also who is responsible and when the key evidence appears. However, exist- ing methods typically treat spatial perception and semantic recognition as isolated tasks, failing to ground events to individual players or pinpoint their temporal boundaries within complex collective dynamics. To bridge this gap, we introduce BasketEvent, a player- centric basketball event understanding dataset curated from real NBA broadcasts. In BasketEvent, event labels are grounded to the responsible players, and a manually an- notated subset of 1,000 samples with precise event intervals is provided to evaluate tem- poral evidence localization. Based on this data, we propose PlayNet, a player-centric reasoning framework that maps basketball videos to player-level event predictions with temporal evidence. Concretely, PlayNet tracks key entities, associates player identities, and reasons about events by modeling player-player, player-ball, and global court inter- actions, while aggregating sparse temporal evidence via gated pooling. Extensive experi- ments demonstrate that PlayNet significantly outperforms representative video-level and crop-based baselines, proving the superiority of player-centric modeling for fine-grained sports video understanding. Our data, code, and models will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [103] [An LLM-Driven Workflow for Automated Process Control Strategy Generation and Tuning from Dynamic Process Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21292)
*Ari Luna Rueda,Eike Cramer,Klaus Hellgardt,Mehmet Mercangöz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-driven automated workflow for multi-variable control design from dynamic process models, demonstrated on a gas-preheater benchmark with PI feedback-feedforward control and Bayesian optimization tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To automate complex multi-variable control design from dynamic process models using structured LLM-driven workflows, reducing manual engineering effort and demonstrating feasibility of automated control synthesis.

Method: Structured LLM workflow decomposing control design into constrained code-generation steps: plant-interface construction, normalization, MV-CV pairing, controller specification, closed-loop simulation, scenario generation, performance evaluation, and BO-based tuning. Includes validation/execution with repair loops for failed artifacts.

Result: Workflow successfully generated physically consistent decentralized PI feedback-feedforward control structure for nonlinear gas-preheater benchmark. Bayesian optimization reduced closed-loop performance objective by 26.5% relative to initial controller, mainly through improved pressure-loop transient performance.
