Motivation: The strong lottery ticket hypothesis suggests that overparameterized random networks contain subnetworks that are accurate before training, but reliable extraction of these subnetworks remains difficult. Current methods like edge-popup have bottlenecks in layerwise sparsity selection.

Method: Introduces double-scoring, an augmented score-space parameterization that replaces layerwise sparsity search with optimization over enlarged score tensors. This preserves access to all original-coordinate masks while enabling interpretation as edge-popup on a zero-augmented network.

Result: Double-scoring substantially improves strong-ticket extraction over fixed-density edge-popup and pruning-at-initialization baselines. It outperforms rewound sparse-training topologies and exhibits markedly lower sensitivity to sparsity hyperparameters.

Conclusion: The improvement comes not just from additional trainable parameters, but from the augmented score-space competition that induces effective original sparsity, providing a more robust method for extracting strong lottery tickets from random networks.

Abstract: The lottery ticket hypothesis proposes that large random neural networks contain sparse subnetworks that can match the performance of dense models after comparable training. A stronger version asserts that sufficiently overparameterized random networks contain subnetworks that are already accurate before any weight training. Existing theory establishes that such strong lottery tickets exist, but reliable extraction remains difficult. We revisit edge-popup, a frozen-weight score-training method for extracting strong tickets, and identify layerwise sparsity selection as a central bottleneck. We introduce double-scoring, an augmented score-space parameterization that replaces a layerwise sparsity search with optimization over enlarged score tensors. We prove that fixed-density masking in an augmented score space preserves access to all original-coordinate masks, and we show that the resulting method can be interpreted as edge-popup on a zero-augmented network. In controlled experiments, double-scoring substantially improves strong-ticket extraction over fixed-density edge-popup and pruning-at-initialization baselines, improves on the performance of rewound sparse-training topologies, and exhibits markedly lower sensitivity to sparsity hyperparameters. Ablations show that the gain is not merely due to additional trainable score parameters, but is tied to the augmented score-space competition that induces the effective original sparsity.

</details>


### [153] [Monkey King Bang: A Unified Scientific Multimodal Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20557)
*Hesen Chen,Xinyu Su,Xiaomeng Yang,Yuetan Lin,Zixiong Yang,Junyi An,Fenglei Cao,Yifeng Jiao,Yunqi Zhang,Yuan Cheng,Zhiyu Tan,Hao Li,Libo Wu,Yuan Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MKB is a unified multimodal AI model that handles six scientific domains (DNA, RNA, proteins, small molecules, earth science, medical images) with both understanding and generation capabilities, using a shared Transformer backbone with modality-specific components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific discovery is shifting to multi-domain reasoning, but existing AI systems are either specialized for individual domains or unify data mainly through text tokenization and prompts, limiting their ability to handle diverse scientific inputs, produce modality-native outputs, and support joint understanding, reasoning, and generation across scientific domains.

Method: MKB uses a shared Transformer backbone with modality-tailored encoders, adapters, and decoders covering six scientific branches. Training follows a two-stage modality-then-language curriculum: Stage 1 aligns modality-specific components with the frozen backbone, and Stage 2 consolidates them with the language backbone using mixed scientific and general corpora.

Result: MKB achieves competitive scientific understanding across biological and molecular benchmarks, produces high-fidelity native outputs for weather forecasting, biological generation, and medical-image segmentation, and largely retains the general capabilities of its Qwen3-VL backbone.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the feasibility of shared-backbone models with modality-tailored components as a promising foundation for future cross-domain scientific multimodal exploration, enabling unified scientific AI systems that can handle diverse inputs and produce native outputs across multiple domains.

Abstract: Scientific discovery is increasingly shifting from isolated disciplines to multi-domain reasoning, and AI for science faces a similar transition. Existing systems are either specialised for individual domains or unify scientific data mainly through text tokenisation and prompt-based interfaces, limiting their ability to handle diverse scientific inputs, produce modality-native outputs, and support joint understanding, reasoning, and generation across scientific domains. We introduce MKB, a unified scientific multimodal model for both understanding and generation, built around a shared Transformer backbone and modality-tailored encoders, adapters, and decoders. MKB covers six scientific branches, including DNA, RNA, proteins, small molecules, earth science, and medical images, and supports native outputs such as biological sequences, molecular strings, meteorological fields, and segmentation masks. Training follows a two-stage modality-then-language curriculum: Stage 1 aligns modality-specific components with the frozen backbone, and Stage 2 consolidates them with the language backbone using mixed scientific and general corpora. Experiments show that MKB achieves competitive scientific understanding across biological and molecular benchmarks, produces high-fidelity native outputs for weather forecasting, biological generation, and medical-image segmentation, and largely retains the general capabilities of its Qwen3-VL backbone. These results demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed paradigm, suggesting that shared-backbone models with modality-tailored components can provide a promising foundation for future cross-domain scientific multimodal exploration. The model and code are publicly available at https://github.com/Shanghai-Academy-of-AI-For-Science/MKB and https://huggingface.co/sais-org/MKB.

</details>


### [154] [StabilityBench: Benchmarking Instability in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20558)
*Emma Kondrup,Zachary Yang,Anne Imouza,Reihaneh Rabbany*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: StabilityBench is a benchmark operator that transforms single-turn evaluations into multi-turn conversational histories with realistic user simulations, revealing significant performance instability in AI assistants across various tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI assistants are deployed in high-stakes settings but their real-world behavior is poorly understood due to context dependence. Current static, single-turn benchmarks fail to capture conversational variability, creating a need for more realistic evaluation methods.

Method: StabilityBench transforms single-turn benchmark queries into multi-turn interaction histories using realistic user simulations via demographic proxies or sycophantic baits while preserving original task intent. Also introduces StabilityBench-Mini, a size-preserving variant that samples across diversification axes.

Result: When applied to four benchmarks (mathematical reasoning, health QA, and safety) and nine LLMs, model performance showed consistent instability with considerable degradation on three out of four benchmarks, highlighting limitations of static evaluations.

Conclusion: Static evaluations have important limitations; more realistic evaluation settings are needed. StabilityBench provides a principled, general, and model-agnostic approach for more realistic AI assistant assessment without significantly increasing evaluation costs.

Abstract: AI Assistants are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings, such as healthcare or government services. Yet their real-world behavior remains poorly understood due to strong context dependence. Current evaluation protocols follow a defense-in-depth paradigm with compounding layers of safeguards, ranging from traditional benchmarks to live or adversarial testing. Such benchmarks remain largely static and single-turn, limiting their ability to capture real-world variability in conversational settings. We propose StabilityBench, a principled, general and model-agnostic benchmark operator that transforms single-turn benchmark queries into multi-turn interaction histories. StabilityBench augments existing benchmarks by injecting realistic user simulations, through demographic proxies or sycophantic baits, while preserving original task intent. We apply StabilityBench to four benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, health question-answering and safety, and evaluate nine large language models under these conditions. Our results show that model performance is consistently unstable under these injections, with considerable performance degradations on three out of four benchmarks studied. These highlight important limitations of static evaluations and motivate more realistic evaluation settings. To this end, we propose StabilityBench-Mini: a size-preserving variant of StabilityBench that samples across diversification axes, enabling more realistic evaluation without increasing costs.

</details>


### [155] [Joint Utilization of Geospatial and census proxies for Autoencoder-Assisted Downscaling (JUGAAD) of socioeconomic indicators in India](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20559)
*Aditya Dutt,Paul Gader,Aditya Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JuGAAD is a deep learning framework that combines Indian census and survey data to predict socioeconomic indicators at fine geographic scales by addressing scale mismatches between data sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a significant challenge in monitoring poverty and food security in developing nations due to scale mismatches: census data provide geographic coverage but surveys that contain socioeconomic indicators are conducted infrequently and at coarse resolutions.

Method: A three-step deep learning approach: 1) Average census and geospatial data into village-cluster-scale tessellations to reduce noise and handle administrative boundary changes, 2) Use an autoencoder to compress high-dimensional NSSO survey data into low-dimensional latent representation, 3) Train a regression model to map upscaled census data to the latent representation, then apply this to fine-grained census data for high-resolution predictions.

Result: The methodology successfully predicts socioeconomic indicators at fine scales with strong accuracy, validated against ground-truth district-level NSSO indicators.

Conclusion: JuGAAD provides an effective solution for generating high-resolution socioeconomic predictions by bridging the scale gap between census and survey data, offering improved monitoring capabilities for poverty and food security in developing nations.

Abstract: Monitoring poverty and food security indicators is imperative for addressing socioeconomic challenges in developing nations. A limitation is mismatches in scale between data sources: census data provide geographic coverage, while socioeconomic indicators are derived from infrequently conducted surveys at coarse resolutions, posing a methodological challenge. This study introduces a deep learning framework, JuGAAD, using Indian census and survey data from 2001 and 2011 as a case study. We employ a three-step process: census and geospatial data are averaged into intermediate village-cluster-scale tessellations to reduce noise and regularize administrative boundary changes; an autoencoder compresses high-dimensional National Sample Survey Office (NSSO) data into a low-dimensional latent representation; and a regression model maps upscaled census and geospatial data to this representation. This function is applied to fine-grained census data to generate high-resolution predictions, validated against ground-truth district-level NSSO indicators. Results confirm the methodology predicts socioeconomic indicators at fine scales with strong accuracy.

</details>


### [156] [Chronofy: A Temporal-Logical Decay Architecture for Information Validity in Time-Aware Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20560)
*Muntaser Syed,Marius Silaghi,Sheikh Abujar,Sharun Akter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Chronofy is a neuro-symbolic RAG framework that embeds temporal validity into representation, retrieval, and reasoning layers using temporal subspaces, learnable decay functions, and temporal logic to combat temporal hallucination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG systems treat all retrieved facts as equally valid regardless of temporal provenance, leading to temporal hallucination where plausible but obsolete facts corrupt outputs. Temporal context is critical for accurate decision-making (e.g., recent clinical lab readings vs. old ones).

Method: Three-layer Temporal-Logical Decay Architecture (TLDA): 1) Temporal subspace within Matryoshka embeddings, 2) Learnable exponential decay functions in graph-based retrieval with decay coefficient grounded in Bayesian decision theory, 3) Signal Temporal Logic (STL) robustness functions with possibilistic weakest-link principle.

Result: Chronofy improves retrieval precision, reduces temporal hallucination, and enables principled data re-acquisition triggers when temporal context is insufficient. Evaluated on temporal knowledge graph forecasting benchmarks, TimE temporal QA benchmark, and domain-specific sensitivity analysis.

Conclusion: Explicit temporal decay modeling in RAG systems is essential for handling time-sensitive information, and the neuro-symbolic Chronofy framework provides a principled approach to address temporal hallucination while maintaining practical applicability across domains.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems retrieve and integrate external knowledge to ground large language model (LLM) outputs. However, current RAG architectures treat all retrieved facts as equally valid regardless of temporal provenance, leading to temporal hallucination, where plausible but obsolete facts corrupt the output. A clinical lab reading from yesterday is actionable; the same reading from six months ago is noise. We present Chronofy, a three-layer neuro-symbolic framework implementing the Temporal-Logical Decay Architecture (TLDA) that embeds temporal validity directly into the representation, retrieval, and reasoning layers of RAG systems. Layer 1 reserves a dedicated temporal subspace within Matryoshka embeddings to make fact age structurally irremovable from the representation. Layer 2 integrates learnable exponential decay functions into graph-based retrieval, where the decay coefficient $β_j$ is grounded in Bayesian decision theory as an approximation of twice the latent process mean-reversion rate. Layer 3 applies Signal Temporal Logic (STL) robustness functions to evaluate the temporal validity of retrieved knowledge, not LLM output confidence, and enforces the possibilistic weakest-link principle to bound output confidence by the most decayed evidence in the reasoning chain. We evaluate Chronofy on temporal knowledge graph forecasting benchmarks, the TimE temporal QA benchmark, and a domain-specific sensitivity analysis, demonstrating that explicit temporal decay modeling improves retrieval precision, reduces temporal hallucination, and enables principled data re-acquisition triggers when temporal context is insufficient.

</details>


### [157] [CT-Merging: Consensus Directions and Task-Level Scaling for LoRA Adapter Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20561)
*Keumseo Ryum,Joonhyuk Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CT-Merging is a new LoRA-aware merging algorithm that combines multiple task adapters by constructing consensus directions from average task subspace projectors and assigning task-level RMS coefficient scales, outperforming previous SVD-based merging methods on CLIP adapter benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying multiple LoRA adapters for different tasks requires storage overhead and task selection at inference time. While model merging addresses this by combining adapters, existing SVD-based methods rely on original task SVD coefficients that don't match the magnitudes induced by actual task updates.

Method: CT-Merging estimates consensus directions from average task subspace projectors and assigns task-level RMS (Root Mean Square) coefficient scales in the final update. It uses repeated support across task SVD subspaces to construct a common basis while reducing reliance on rank-wise SVD magnitudes after direction construction.

Result: On the DC-Merge CLIP adapter benchmark, CT-Merging achieves superior average normalized accuracy compared to state-of-the-art merging methods. It improves over DC-Merge by 2.56 points on ViT-B/32 and 1.51 points on ViT-L/14 KnoTS-trained checkpoints.

Conclusion: CT-Merging effectively addresses the coefficient magnitude mismatch in existing LoRA merging methods by using consensus directions and task-level RMS scales, leading to better multi-task adapter performance than previous approaches.

Abstract: LoRA adapters provide an efficient way to specialize a pretrained model for many downstream tasks, but deploying one adapter per task requires adapter storage and task selection at inference time. Model merging addresses this issue by combining independently trained adapters into one multi-task adapter. Recent SVD-based LoRA merging methods mainly focus on constructing shared or task specific directions, while the coefficients assigned to the final directions are often directly from the original task SVD. On a fixed merged basis, inherited coefficients preserve component order with high rank correlation, yet their magnitudes differ substantially from the coefficients induced by the task updates. To address this mismatch, we propose CT-Merging, a LoRA-aware merging algorithm that estimates consensus directions from average task subspace projectors and assigns task-level RMS coefficient scales in the final update. CT-Merging uses repeated support across task SVD subspaces to construct the common basis, while reducing reliance on rank wise SVD magnitudes after direction construction. On the DC-Merge CLIP adapter benchmark, CT-Merging achieves superior average normalized accuracy compared to state-of-the-art merging methods and further improves over DC-Merge by 2.56 points on ViT-B/32 and 1.51 points on ViT-L/14 KnoTS-trained checkpoints.

</details>


### [158] [AI-Driven Surrogate Models for Predicting Electrode-Scale Discharge Behavior in Lithium-Ion Batteries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20577)
*Mengda Xing,Jean-Marie Lagniez,Alejandro Franco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Swin3D Transformer-based deep learning pipeline with Gaussian Positional Encoding and Temporal Encoding modules accurately predicts spatiotemporal discharge dynamics in lithium-ion batteries while drastically reducing computational costs compared to physics-based simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physics-based simulations for understanding electrode-scale discharge behavior in lithium-ion batteries suffer from prohibitively high computational costs, creating a need for more efficient prediction methods to enable scalable battery design and optimization.

Method: The authors propose a novel deep learning surrogate pipeline based on the Swin3D Transformer that directly predicts spatiotemporal discharge dynamics from volumetric data. Key innovations include Gaussian Positional Encoding (GPE) for enhanced spatial feature representation adapted to complex electrode microstructure geometry, and a specialized Temporal Encoding module to capture non-linear timeseries evolution.

Result: The pipeline significantly outperforms state-of-the-art point cloud baselines in prediction accuracy on an Electrochemical Simulation (ES) dataset. Additionally, it reduces computational overhead by orders of magnitude compared to traditional physics-based simulations.

Conclusion: The proposed deep learning surrogate pipeline provides a scalable and efficient framework for high-throughput battery design and optimization, successfully addressing the computational bottleneck of physics-based simulations while maintaining high prediction accuracy for spatiotemporal discharge dynamics.

Abstract: Physics-based simulations are essential for understanding the electrode-scale discharge behavior of lithium-ion batteries (LIBs) but suffer from prohibitive computational costs. To address this, we introduce a novel deep learning surrogate pipeline based on the Swin3D Transformer to predict spatiotemporal discharge dynamics directly from volumetric data. Our approach integrates two key innovations: Gaussian Positional Encoding (GPE), which enhances spatial feature representation by adapting to the complex geometry of electrode microstructures, and a specialized Temporal Encoding module to capture non-linear timeseries evolution. Experimental validation on an Electrochemical Simulation (ES) dataset demonstrates that our pipeline significantly outperforms state-of-the-art point cloud baselines in prediction accuracy. Furthermore, the proposed method reduces the computational overhead by orders of magnitude, providing a scalable and efficient framework for high-throughput battery design and optimization.

</details>


### [159] [Fisher Widths: Local Learning Geometry and Anisotropic Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20578)
*Vu Khac Ky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies Gaussian-width complexity on statistical manifolds using Fisher and inverse-Fisher metrics, showing they have complementary roles in learning and recovery problems with connections to statistical dimension and support-sensitive analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how Fisher information geometry affects complexity in statistical learning and recovery problems, particularly how different metrics (Fisher vs inverse-Fisher) capture different aspects of parameter estimation and sparse recovery.

Method: Uses Gaussian-width analysis on statistical manifolds with Fisher and inverse-Fisher metrics, analyzing functional widths, establishing bounds for Fisher-regular losses, studying sparse recovery with anisotropic Gaussian measurements, and proving relationships between primal and inverse-Fisher widths.

Result: Shows that Fisher width measures local parameter fluctuations in learning, inverse-Fisher width captures anisotropic measurement effects in recovery, obtains two-sided estimates for statistical dimension, support-sensitive recovery estimates, and proves a sharp inequality relating the two widths: w_G(T)w_{G^{-1}}(T) ≥ w(T)².

Conclusion: Fisher and inverse-Fisher widths provide complementary geometric perspectives on statistical complexity, with the Fisher metric capturing learning aspects and inverse-Fisher capturing recovery aspects, linked by a fundamental inequality showing Fisher anisotropy can transfer but not reduce both widths simultaneously relative to Euclidean scale.

Abstract: We study Gaussian-width complexity on statistical manifolds through a pair of functionals: the primal Fisher width $w_G(T) = w(G^{1/2}T)$, induced by the Fisher metric, and the inverse-Fisher width $w_{G^{-1}}(T) = w(G^{-1/2}T)$, induced by the inverse Fisher metric. The two widths play complementary statistical roles.
  On the learning side, the Fisher width measures the size of local parameter fluctuations in the geometry induced by the Fisher information. For Fisher-regular losses, we prove that the scale \(w_G(H_r)/\sqrt n\) is attained on sufficiently small Fisher balls.
  On the recovery side, the inverse-Fisher width captures the effect of anisotropic Gaussian measurements whose covariance is determined by the inverse Fisher information. For sparse recovery, the resulting geometry depends not only on sparsity but also on the position of the active coordinates in the Fisher spectrum. We obtain a two-sided estimate for the corresponding statistical dimension, together with support-sensitive recovery estimates and a natural ordering of supports with different curvature profiles.
  Finally, we establish a sharp relation between the primal and inverse-Fisher widths. On any common compact coordinate set $T$, they satisfy \[ w_G(T)w_{G^{-1}}(T)\geq w(T)^2. \] Thus, Fisher anisotropy may transfer complexity from one geometry to the other, but cannot reduce both widths relative to the Euclidean scale.

</details>


### [160] [Exact ReLU realization of affine one-dimensional refinement iterates via residual memory and offset frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20586)
*Boldsaikhan Bolorkhuu,Tsogtgerel Gantumur*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vector-valued affine refinement operators with matrix masks and piecewise linear data can be exactly realized by fixed-width ReLU networks with linear depth for M≥3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how affine refinement operators with forcing terms can be exactly represented by ReLU neural networks, extending previous results for homogeneous (forcing-free) cases to more general affine settings.

Method: Introduce a residual memory controller to handle noninvertible residual dynamics by creating an injective skew-product system, allowing exact backward replay of residual states. Use offset frames to align forcing atoms away from residual seams, enabling complementary loop readouts for exact value recovery. The approach works for M≥3 with arbitrary compactly supported piecewise linear forcing terms.

Result: For M≥3, every finite affine iterate Wⁿγ admits an exact fixed-width ReLU realization with O(n) depth. For M=2, the same construction works for ordinary-frame seam-separated forcing. Also provides stage-dependent extensions for finite-dimensional piecewise linear spans and linear-depth upgrades for various recursive constructions.

Conclusion: Affine refinement operators with forcing terms can be exactly realized by fixed-width ReLU networks with linear depth complexity, extending the applicability of neural network representations from homogeneous to affine operators and establishing concrete realizability results for various parameter settings and forcing types.

Abstract: We study vector-valued affine refinement operators of the form [
(Wγ)(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}} A_jγ(Mt-j)+B(t), ]
with finitely supported matrix mask and compactly supported continuous piecewise linear input and forcing data. Building on the homogeneous realization theorem for (B\equiv 0), we prove that, for (M\ge 3), every finite affine iterate (W^nγ) admits an exact fixed-width ReLU realization whose depth is (O(n)).
  The main new ingredient is a residual memory controller. It replaces the noninvertible residual dynamics by an injective skew-product and permits exact backward replay of the residual states required by a Horner-type evaluation of the affine forcing sum. Offset frames align the forcing atoms away from residual seams, allowing complementary loop readouts to recover their values exactly. The remaining branch-selection ambiguity occurs only where the accumulated affine state has already vanished.
  For (M\ge 3), the result applies to arbitrary compactly supported continuous piecewise linear forcing terms. For (M=2), the same construction applies to ordinary-frame seam-separated forcing. We also prove a stage-dependent extension for forcing terms in a fixed finite-dimensional continuous piecewise linear span and record the resulting linear-depth upgrade for open-curve, finite-state, and Hilbert- and Morton-type recursive constructions.

</details>


### [161] [Detecting Neural Network Failures through Spectral Analysis of Internal Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20590)
*Arunan J*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural network failures have characteristic spectral instability in internal activations that gets masked at the output layer. SDNN monitors these spectral dynamics across network depth using spectral analysis to detect failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural network misclassifications have spectral instability in internal activations that is invisible at the output layer, explaining why confidence-based detection methods struggle. This spectral signature emerges during internal processing but becomes masked in final outputs.

Method: SDNN monitors spectral dynamics across network depth using Short-Time Fourier Transform, wavelet decomposition, and statistical moments to capture multi-scale spectral features. A lightweight detector network (5% parameter overhead) learns to identify failure-indicative patterns via curriculum learning on natural misclassifications, distribution shifts, and adversarial perturbations.

Result: Failures exhibit significantly higher Spectral Drift than correct predictions (1.9% increase, p<0.001). On CIFAR-10, SDNN achieves 79.0 +/- 25.3% AUROC across three seeds, outperforming confidence-based baselines by approximately 25-30 percentage points (MaxSoftmax: 50.5%, Energy Score: 52.9%).

Conclusion: Spectral analysis of internal activations is a promising direction for neural network reliability, revealing diagnostic information inaccessible to output-based approaches. The spectral signature of failures in internal processing provides new opportunities for failure detection.

Abstract: Neural network misclassifications exhibit characteristic spectral instability in internal activations that is invisible at the output layer. This phenomenon is identified and formalized as Spectral Drift -- the frequency-domain distance between consecutive layer activations -- with empirical validation showing that failures exhibit significantly higher drift than correct predictions (1.9% increase, p<0.001). This spectral signature emerges during internal processing but becomes masked in final outputs, explaining why confidence-based detection methods struggle.
  This work introduces Self-Detecting Neural Networks (SDNN), a framework that monitors spectral dynamics across network depth using Short-Time Fourier Transform, wavelet decomposition, and statistical moments to capture multi-scale spectral features. A lightweight detector network (5% parameter overhead) learns to identify failure-indicative patterns via curriculum learning on progressively challenging distributions: natural misclassifications, distribution shifts, and adversarial perturbations.
  Experiments on CIFAR-10 demonstrate that SDNN achieves 79.0 +/- 25.3% AUROC across three seeds, substantially outperforming confidence-based baselines including MaxSoftmax (50.5%) and Energy Score (52.9%) by approximately 25-30 percentage points. Ablation studies reveal that wavelet decomposition and statistical features make consistent contributions, while STFT's role remains unclear. This work establishes spectral analysis of internal activations as a promising direction for neural network reliability, revealing diagnostic information inaccessible to output-based approaches.

</details>


### [162] [STeMP: Spatio-Temporal Modelling Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20592)
*Jan Linnenbrink,Jakub Nowosad,Marvin Ludwig,Anna Frederike Jablotschkin,Fabian Schumacher,Teja Kattenborn,Hanna Meyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STeMP is a standardized reporting protocol for spatio-temporal machine learning models in environmental research, with an R package implementation to facilitate transparent reporting and guide modeling decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatio-temporal machine learning models are crucial in environmental research but highly sensitive to training data characteristics and methodological choices. Current lack of standardized reporting protocols hinders trust, transparency, and comparability of models that have significant scientific and practical applications.

Method: The authors developed STeMP (Spatio-Temporal Modelling Protocol) with three main sections: Overview (metadata), Model (predictors, evaluation), and Prediction. They created an R package with a web application that helps users fill the protocol manually or semi-automatically from modeling objects, including warning systems for common pitfalls.

Result: STeMP provides a comprehensive framework for standardized reporting of spatio-temporal models, available as an open-source protocol on GitHub with an accompanying R package. The tool supports authors through the modeling process and assists reviewers in assessing modeling studies.

Conclusion: STeMP addresses the critical need for standardized reporting in spatio-temporal machine learning for environmental research, promoting transparency, trust, and comparability while providing practical guidance through the modeling workflow.

Abstract: Spatio-temporal machine-learning modelling is an important tool in environmental research. However, machine-learning models are highly sensitive to both the characteristics of the training data, such as its distribution, and methodological choices, including the cross-validation strategy. Each decision has impact and implications on the model itself as well as the estimation of the model quality and applicability for certain purposes. Taking into account the large role of machine-learning based maps of the environment in science and their transfer into practice, transparent reporting of spatio-temporal models, ideally using standardized model protocols, is essential to enable trust, transparency and comparability. However, such protocols are currently lacking for spatio-temporal modelling.
  We propose STeMP (Spatio-Temporal Modelling Protocol) to fill this gap by serving two purposes: standardized reporting to understand the model functioning as well as providing guidance during the modelling process by pointing at critical decisions and parameters. The protocol is structured in three sections: Overview, Model and Prediction. The Overview section contains metadata, while the Model and Prediction sections go into detail, describing predictors, evaluation and software, and further relevant elements of the modelling workflow.
  The protocol definition is hosted on GitHub and accompanied by an R-package (https://github.com/LOEK-RS/STeMP). The R-package contains a web application that can be used to fill the protocol either manually or in a semi-automated way from provided modelling objects. Warnings are returned from the protocol when common pitfalls are encountered, which may help authors as a guide through the modelling process but also support reviewers in the assessment of modelling studies. Via GitHub, incorporation of contributions and feedback from the community is encouraged.

</details>


### [163] [When Does Recurrence Become an Algorithm? Convergence Selection in Weight-Tied Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20594)
*Tong Zhang,Junhao Hu,Yun Peng,Tao Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weight-tied looped transformers learn algorithmic computation frontiers with speed-vs-budget tradeoffs; architecture priors (not expressivity) determine algorithm selection, with surprising generalization patterns across group word problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when weight-tied looped transformers implement actual algorithms rather than just performing pattern matching, using group word problems as a controlled testbed to study computational capabilities and generalization.

Method: Using controlled populations on group word problems with weight-tied looped transformers (one block applied T times). Introduced convergence-time scaling τ(n,i) as a head instrument to measure algorithmic behavior, validated via damage cones. Compared standard-depth transformers vs weight-tied architectures at matched depth/parameters.

Result: Found four key results: 1) Budget law establishing linear computation frontier v~n_train/T_train; 2) Architecture prior determines algorithm selection (serial vs parallel); 3) Generalization barriers relate to group order not circuit complexity; 4) Mechanisms are portable across budget contracts. Weight tying enables serial algorithms where untied models fail, especially on harder problems like A5.

Conclusion: Weight-tied looped transformers implement real algorithms with predictable budget laws, where architectural priors (not just expressivity) drive algorithm selection. The introduced convergence-time scaling instrument reveals algorithmic mechanisms invisible to standard metrics, providing principled halting rules and predicting out-of-distribution performance.

Abstract: When does a weight-tied looped transformer -- one block applied T times -- implement an actual algorithm? We answer with four findings from controlled populations on group word problems. (1) The budget law: free training installs a linear computation frontier, a mechanism that solves v positions per loop, whose speed is priced by the training contract: v ~ n_train/T_train (exponent 0.98 +/- 0.04, R^2=0.99), exactly unity under T=n training. SGD selects a frontier matching the minimum the contract demands; granting more test-time loops than ever trained rescues late positions at fixed input length, yielding a principled halting rule T* = ceil(n / v-hat). (2) Architecture prior, not expressivity, picks the algorithm: standard-depth transformers learn parallel scans on this family; weight tying flips the selection to the serial frontier, even when positional addressing for a log-depth scan is supplied. At matched depth and parameters, untied models extrapolate worst and fail to learn A5 at all. (3) The walls are not where circuit complexity says: NC1-completeness costs nothing (A5 generalizes fully), while group order does (S5's 120x120 operator deadlocks joint learning) -- and an operator-first curriculum dissolves the wall in every seed. (4) Mechanisms are portable, not mandatable: warm-starting across budget contracts transfers the algorithm in every seed, re-pricing its speed, while imposing seriality through the input schedule fails where free training succeeds. These results are invisible to standard instruments, which provably saturate at the fixed points trained loops converge to. We introduce a head instrument, the convergence-time scaling tau(n,i), validate it causally via damage cones whose slope reproduces v, and show in-distribution head measurements predict out-of-distribution fate where tail metrics do not. Results replicate on the public easy-to-hard benchmark.

</details>


### [164] [Are Single-Token Sparse Autoencoder Features Causally Necessary? Layer-Depth and SAE-Family Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20596)
*Seonglae Cho,Zekun Wu,Kleyton Da Costa,Rishi Kalra,Ilham Wicaksono,Adriano Koshiyama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAE features' causal stability across different SAE families is tested, showing training methodology matters more than activation functions or scale for cross-family interpretability claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to test whether sparse autoencoder (SAE) features have stable causal roles across different SAE families, addressing concerns about the reliability of SAE-based interpretability and steering methods when features vary between training methodologies.

Method: Analyzed 3.9M features across six models and three SAE families using zero-ablation at full layer depth. Focused on single-token features that activate on one vocabulary item as a diagnostic case where ground truth permits direct comparison. Used Benjamini-Hochberg significance testing and controlled activation-function comparisons.

Result: Single-token features cluster 4.7x tighter in decoder space and concentrate in early layers. Ablating them yields significant logit reductions in 178 of 208 full-layer conditions. Cross-family causal differences exceed within-family scale effects: some SAE families maintain causal anchors while others show local redundancy. The target token's rank recovers to within 2x baseline 96-98% of the time after ablation.

Conclusion: Cross-family interpretability claims are sensitive to training methodology rather than just activation function or scale, highlighting the importance of SAE training recipes for reliable feature-based analysis and steering of language models.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoder (SAE) features are used to interpret and steer large language models, yet whether a feature's causal role is stable across SAE families remains untested. Single-token features that activate on one vocabulary item provide the diagnostic case where ground truth permits direct comparison. We analyze 3.9M features across six models and three SAE families using zero-ablation at full layer depth. Single-token features cluster 4.7x tighter in decoder space and concentrate in early layers (Layer 0 in GPT2-Small; L0-L4 in Gemma). Ablating them yields Benjamini-Hochberg-significant logit reductions in 178 of 208 full-layer conditions, with depth controlling whether damage cascades downstream or shapes the output directly. Cross-family causal differences exceed within-family scale effects: on the same base model, GemmaScope and BatchTopK features remain causally anchored, while LlamaScope features are locally redundant. The target token's rank recovers to within 2x baseline 96-98% of the time after the same ablation, and a controlled activation-function comparison reverses sign within the same model, leaving training recipe as the residual candidate. Cross-family interpretability claims are therefore sensitive to training methodology, not just activation function or scale.

</details>


### [165] [One Round Is All You Need: Analytic Federated Learning for Task-Heterogeneous Multi-Label Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20641)
*Afsaneh Mahanipour,Hana Khamfroush*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An analytic federated learning framework for multi-label medical image classification that handles task heterogeneity (missing class labels across institutions) with closed-form operations requiring only 2 communication rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning in clinical settings faces task heterogeneity where each institution annotates only pathologies within its expertise, leaving missing class labels. Existing gradient-based FL methods fail due to slow convergence (hundreds of rounds) and systematic false-negative bias from missing labels.

Method: Replaces iterative gradient optimization with three closed-form operations: 1) balanced label projection to neutralize class-imbalance bias by normalizing positive/negative contributions, 2) per-class absolute aggregation law to assemble optimal ridge-regression classifiers from sufficient statistics, 3) optional analytic pseudo-label refinement to propagate missing-class knowledge via confidence-filtered teacher classifier.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art FedMLP by up to 18.44 BACC points and 13.24 AUC points on ChestXray14 under four progressively severe missing-class configurations, while reducing communication to at most 2 rounds regardless of task heterogeneity or number of clients.

Conclusion: Proposed analytic FL framework effectively addresses task heterogeneity in multi-label medical image classification, achieving superior performance with dramatically reduced communication overhead compared to gradient-based approaches.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables multiple clinical institutions to collaboratively train a shared disease classifier without centralizing patient data. In practice, however, each institution annotates only the pathologies within its area of expertise, so the federation operates under task heterogeneity: each client holds labels for a strict subset of the target disease categories while the remaining classes are entirely unobserved at that site. Existing gradient-based FL methods fail under this setting because they require hundreds of communication rounds to converge and because missing class labels introduce systematic false-negative bias that the model cannot correct without a principled mechanism. We propose an analytic federated learning framework for multi-label medical image classification under task heterogeneity. The proposed method replaces iterative gradient optimization with three closed-form operations: a balanced label projection that neutralizes class-imbalance bias by normalizing positive and negative contributions to equal total mass; a per-class absolute aggregation law that independently assembles the optimal ridge-regression classifier for each disease category from the sufficient statistics uploaded by its annotating clients; and an optional analytic pseudo-label refinement round that propagates missing-class knowledge from a confidence-filtered teacher classifier to non-annotating clients. The entire procedure requires at most two communication rounds, irrespective of the degree of task heterogeneity or the number of participating clients. Experiments on ChestXray14 under four progressively severe missing-class configurations demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms the state-of-the-art federated multi-label method FedMLP by up to 18.44 BACC points and 13.24 AUC points, while reducing the communication.

</details>


### [166] [Scaling Interpretable Transformers with Parity Bottleneck Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20652)
*Andrew Mack,Kraig Yuheng Tou,Mark Henry,Zhengxun Wu,Lauren Greenspan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The ParityTransformer introduces a novel architecture with built-in interpretability through sparse, wide bottleneck layers that efficiently enforce sparsity using deterministic algebraic dictionaries, eliminating the memory/compute costs of traditional post-hoc sparse autoencoders while maintaining competitive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models exhibit superposition (representing many features in limited dimensions), and while sparse autoencoders (SAEs) can recover these features post-hoc, training models that are interpretable by construction has been impractical due to prohibitive memory and compute costs of per-layer over-complete bottlenecks at scale.

Method: Introduces ParityTransformer with Deep Parity Bottleneck (DPB) layers that replace learned over-complete bases with parameter-free algebraic dictionaries, providing deterministic incoherence guarantees. Uses hierarchical sparse bottleneck with multi-level mixture-of-experts for efficient sparsity enforcement, implemented in hardware-aware way that minimizes the interpretability tax.

Result: ParityTransformers perform at least as well as post-hoc SAEs on sparse probing tasks while outperforming on feature absorption, steering effectiveness, and fine-grained causal interventions. Since computation only acts on features that survive the sparse bottleneck, the features are native to the model's forward pass by construction.

Conclusion: This approach addresses whether SAEs probe features actually used during computation, representing a step toward training models whose internal representations are interpretable by design rather than recovered post-hoc, with practical efficiency that enables interpretability at scale.

Abstract: Language models are thought to exhibit the phenomenon of superposition, representing many more features than dimensions in their residual streams. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are designed to recover such features post-hoc, but training models that are interpretable by construction has remained impractical, as a per-layer over-complete bottleneck is prohibitively expensive in both memory and compute. To overcome this issue, we introduce the ParityTransformer, a GPT-2-scale architecture whose intermediate representations are efficient and wide / sparse by design. At each layer, a Deep Parity Bottleneck (DPB) replaces a learned over-complete basis with a parameter-free algebraic dictionary, providing a deterministic incoherence guarantee and eliminating the memory requirements that have prevented per-layer interpretable bottlenecks at scale. A DPB is a hierarchically structured sparse bottleneck which efficiently enforces sparsity using a multi-level mixture-of-experts approach: a hardware-aware implementation that closes the cost gap between activation sparse and dense training to a manageable interpretability tax. Empirically, ParityTransformers perform at least as well as post-hoc SAEs on sparse probing tasks, while out-performing on measures of feature absorption, steering effectiveness, and fine-grained causal interventions. Because subsequent computation acts only on features that survive the sparse bottleneck, the ParityTransformer's features are native to the model's forwards pass by construction, addressing the question of whether SAEs probe features the model actually uses during computation. We see this as a step toward training models whose internal representations are interpretable by design rather than recovered post hoc.

</details>


### [167] [SalesLoop: Reinforcement Learning from Performance Feedback for Sales Lead Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20655)
*Chenyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SalesLoop is a reinforcement learning framework for CRM lead ranking that addresses offline-online performance gaps through a closed feedback loop, achieving significant improvements in both offline metrics and real-world conversion rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CRM lead ranking models face a persistent challenge where models with high offline accuracy underperform in production due to three fundamental gaps: offline-online metric mismatch, pointwise-listwise objective misalignment, and temporal distribution drift.

Method: SalesLoop establishes a reinforcement learning framework with a closed feedback loop between predictions and business outcomes. It includes: (1) a performance-aware reward encoding conversion outcomes weighted by ranking position and conversion velocity, and (2) Discriminative GRPO, a listwise optimization objective adapting Group Relative Policy Optimization to discriminative ranking models.

Result: SalesLoop improves NDCG@K by +7.9% and P@K by +15.8% over static baselines. A 160-day production A/B test at a New Energy Vehicle manufacturer (16.5M leads, 280 sales specialists) showed statistically significant cumulative lifts of +4.7% (p=0.047) and +8.7% (p=0.002). The ranking backbone achieves 44.1% Top-10% recall and surfaces high-intent leads at 2.3× the conversion rate of specialist baselines.

Conclusion: SalesLoop effectively bridges the offline-online performance gap in CRM lead ranking through a reinforcement learning approach with closed-loop feedback, delivering significant improvements in both offline metrics and real-world business outcomes across large-scale production deployments.

Abstract: Lead ranking in Customer Relationship Management (CRM) systems faces a persistent challenge: models achieving high offline accuracy often underperform in production. We identify three fundamental gaps responsible for this disconnect: offline-online metric mismatch, pointwise-listwise objective misalignment, and temporal distribution drift. To address these gaps, we propose SalesLoop, a reinforcement learning framework that establishes a closed feedback loop between model predictions and real-world business outcomes. Our approach introduces (1) a performance-aware reward that encodes conversion outcomes weighted by ranking position and conversion velocity, and (2) Discriminative GRPO, a listwise optimization objective that adapts Group Relative Policy Optimization to discriminative ranking models.
  SalesLoop improves NDCG@K by +7.9\% and P@K by +15.8\% over the strongest static baseline. A 160-day production A/B test at a New Energy Vehicle manufacturer, spanning 16.5M leads and 280 sales specialists across two provincial markets, validates statistically significant cumulative lift of +4.7\% ($p=0.047$) and +8.7\% ($p=0.002$). In production, the ranking backbone achieves Top-10\% recall of 44.1\% and surfaces high-intent leads at $2.3\times$ the conversion rate of specialist baselines.

</details>


### [168] [Adaptive Multi-Horizon Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20656)
*Manoosh Samiei,Doina Precup,Paul Masset*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes adaptive multi-horizon RL that dynamically selects and combines temporal horizons instead of using fixed discount factors, improving adaptation to changing environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed discount factors in RL impose a single temporal horizon, while biological agents show flexible temporal discounting. Need for adaptive temporal discounting that can handle complex, changing environments without manual tuning.

Method: Multi-horizon approach that adaptively selects and combines temporal horizons, enabling robust adaptation to changes in reward structure without manual discount-factor tuning.

Result: Method identifies effective discount factors across MiniGrid environments, including continual settings with three sequentially changing tasks. Shows improved parameter efficiency and adaptability.

Conclusion: Adaptive temporal discounting can improve both artificial and biologically inspired learning systems by enabling flexible adaptation to environmental changes and continual learning scenarios.

Abstract: Effective decision-making in complex and changing environments requires balancing short-term and long-term consequences. In reinforcement learning (RL), this trade-off is typically controlled through a fixed discount factor, which imposes a single exponentially discounted temporal horizon. However, biological agents exhibit flexible and adaptive temporal discounting, suggesting that effective planning requires multiple timescales. Here, we propose a multi-horizon approach that adaptively selects and combines temporal horizons, enabling robust adaptation to changes in reward structure without manual discount-factor tuning. This flexibility makes the method particularly suitable for continual learning scenarios involving task switches and varying environmental configurations. Empirically, we demonstrate that our approach identifies effective discount factors across a range of MiniGrid environments, including continual settings composed of three sequentially changing tasks. These results suggest that adaptive temporal discounting can improve parameter efficiency and enhance adaptability in both artificial and biologically inspired learning systems.

</details>


### [169] [End-to-End Learning of Safe Optimal Feedback Control in High Dimensions with Control Barrier Function Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20674)
*Xingjian Li,Kelvin Kan,Deepanshu Verma,Krishna Kumar,Stanley Osher,Samy Wu Fung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable method for training high-dimensional safety-critical controllers using operator splitting and Jacobian-Free Backpropagation to overcome computational bottlenecks in control barrier function-based safety filters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior approaches using control barrier functions (CBFs) as safety filters in end-to-end policy training face computational and differentiation bottlenecks, restricting them to low-dimensional systems (typically ≤16 state dimensions). There's a need to scale these methods to handle high-dimensional systems while preserving hard safety guarantees.

Method: Combines operator splitting with Jacobian-Free Backpropagation (JFB) method to enable scalable end-to-end training while embedding quadratic-program-based CBF safety filters as optimization layers. The approach avoids computational bottlenecks through efficient numerical techniques.

Result: Successfully demonstrates the method on high-dimensional multi-agent nonlinear control problems with state dimensions up to 1200 and control dimensions up to 400, significantly exceeding previous dimensionality limitations while maintaining hard safety guarantees.

Conclusion: The proposed approach enables scalable training of safety-critical controllers for high-dimensional systems through operator splitting and JFB, with theoretical justification using nonsmooth analysis and practical validation on challenging multi-agent problems.

Abstract: We consider the problem of learning high-dimensional semi-global feedback controllers under hard safety constraints enforced by control barrier functions (CBFs). Incorporating CBFs into end-to-end policy training requires embedding a quadratic-program-based safety filter as an optimization layer, but computational and differentiation bottlenecks have largely restricted prior approaches to low-dimensional systems, typically with at most 16 state dimensions. We address this limitation by combining operator splitting with the recently developed Jacobian-Free Backpropagation (JFB) method to enable scalable end-to-end training while preserving hard safety guarantees through the CBF safety filter. We justify this training methodology theoretically using nonsmooth analysis techniques and demonstrate its effectiveness on high-dimensional multi-agent nonlinear control problems with state and control dimensions up to 1200 and 400, respectively.

</details>


### [170] [Explanation-Based Runtime Verification for Trustworthy ML-driven Optical Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20675)
*Omran Ayoub,Carlos Natalino,Ali Al Housseini,Felix Foschum,Philipp Morger,Tiziano Leidi,David Hock,Paolo Monti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Explanation-based runtime verification uses ML model explanations to assess decision soundness in optical network automation, catching errors while maintaining high automation rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As ML models are increasingly integrated into optical network automation where incorrect decisions can immediately impact service quality and network stability, ensuring reliability of individual decisions at deployment time has become critical. XAI techniques provide transparency but need to be leveraged for real-time decision validation.

Method: Introduces explanation-based runtime verification that exploits model explanations to assess the soundness of individual ML decisions before execution in the network control loop. The approach evaluates explanation coherence and physics grounding consistency at runtime to flag uncertain decisions.

Result: Experimental results on a lightpath quality of transmission classification use case show that explanation-based verification can intercept a significant fraction of erroneous decisions while preserving high automation rate.

Conclusion: Explanation-based runtime verification provides an effective method for improving the reliability of ML-driven decisions in optical network automation by leveraging XAI insights to validate decisions before execution, balancing safety with automation efficiency.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) models are increasingly integrated into optical network automation frameworks to support tasks such as failure management, performance monitoring and resource allocation. In these environments, ML-driven predictions may be directly coupled with control-plane actions where incorrect decisions can immediately impact service quality, resource efficiency, and network stability. As automation levels increase, ensuring the reliability of individual decisions at deployment time becomes a critical requirement. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) techniques have emerged to improve transparency by highlighting the factors influencing ML predictions. In addition to identifying influential features, they provide insights into the underlying reasoning process of the model, revealing how different input variables contribute to the final outcome and how feature interactions shape the decision boundary. In this work, we introduce explanation-based runtime verification, an approach that exploits model explanations to assess the soundness of individual ML decisions before they are executed in the network control loop. The proposed approach evaluates explanation coherence and physics grounding consistency at runtime, enabling the system to defer or reject decisions flagged as uncertain. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on a representative use case of lightpath quality of transmission classification. Experimental results show that explanation-based verification can intercept a significant fraction of erroneous decisions while preserving high automation rate.

</details>


### [171] [Attribution Markets: A Fisher-Market Formulation for Fractional Credit Assignment Between Planned Tasks and Performed Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20694)
*Salavat Ishbulatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A planning system that formulates the bridge between planned tasks and performed actions as a quasi-linear Fisher market, addressing challenges of budget constraints, affinity noise, and progress discounting with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional planning systems maintain separate records of planned tasks (effort budget) and performed actions (duration/description) that drift apart. Existing systems use exclusive all-or-nothing links that strand genuinely related but unlinked effort and report false stalls on active goals.

Method: Formulates the bridge as a quasi-linear Fisher market where planned tasks are budget-constrained buyers and performed actions are divisible goods. Uses fused text/structural/temporal signals for valuation, includes seller reserve price and buyer cash option for conservation and budget caps. Extends with concave completion utility discounting progress, and addresses noise sensitivity with an entropy-regularized generalization.

Result: Proves conservation, hard budget cap, and junk filter as theorems. Resolves convergence issues with satiation-threshold fixed point with existence and local uniqueness guarantees. Identifies sensitivity to affinity noise and resolves with one-parameter entropy-regularized generalization unifying Fisher market and optimal transport approaches.

Conclusion: Provides a theoretically grounded market-based approach to align planned tasks with performed actions, addressing limitations of current systems with noise-adaptive regularization and comprehensive validation. Relates to multi-touch attribution, optimal transport, and online Fisher-market algorithms.

Abstract: Personal and organizational planning systems maintain two records that drift apart: what was planned (a task's effort budget) and what was done (a logged action's duration and description). Existing systems bridge them with an exclusive, all-or-nothing link that strands genuinely related but unlinked effort and reports false stalls on active goals. We formulate the bridge as a quasi-linear Fisher market: planned tasks are budget-constrained buyers, performed actions are divisible goods, and a fused text/structural/temporal signal sets each buyer's valuation. Two market instruments - a seller reserve price and a buyer cash option - yield conservation, a hard budget cap, and a provable junk filter as theorems. We extend the market with a concave completion utility discounting progress as a task nears its plan; standard convergence theory for the market's algorithm does not transfer here, resolved by a satiation-threshold fixed point with existence (Brouwer) and local uniqueness under an explicit diagonal-dominance condition, validated empirically on random and adversarial instances. A de-circularized, multi-seed benchmark - observed affinity corrupted independently of the scored ground truth - surfaces a genuine weak spot: the market's sharp, zero-entropy equilibrium is more sensitive to affinity noise than entropy-regularized optimal transport's permanently smoothed one. We resolve this with a one-parameter entropy-regularized generalization unifying the two, plus a noise-adaptive rule for its regularization strength. We report full reproducibility parameters, discuss limitations candidly, and relate the result to multi-touch attribution, optimal transport, and online Fisher-market algorithms.

</details>


### [172] [CEDAR: Causal Edge Discovery for Autoregressive Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20696)
*Mohammad Fesanghary*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CEDAR is a constraint-based method for discovering lagged causal edges in sparse autoregressive time series using distance correlation screening and targeted conditional independence tests with edge-level interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for efficient lagged causal edge discovery methods in sparse autoregressive time series, particularly when data is scarce and variables exhibit lag-1 self-dynamics, where existing methods with richer conditioning sets may not be optimal.

Method: CEDAR screens candidate cross-variable lags using AR(1)-residualized, U-centered distance correlation, applies two targeted conditional independence tests per significant candidate, accepts at most one lag per ordered pair, performs stable MCI pruning to remove indirect edges, and optionally uses deterministic C-nodes to adjust for trend-like nonstationarity.

Result: In sparse regimes with few surviving lags after screening, CEDAR requires only O(d²) conditional independence tests while maintaining edge-level interpretability, making it particularly effective for scarce data with lag-1 self-dynamics.

Conclusion: CEDAR is most effective when data is scarce and variables show lag-1 self-dynamics, while methods with richer conditioning sets become preferable as sample size grows or when higher-order autoregressive/multi-lag effects are common.

Abstract: We propose CEDAR (Causal Edge Discovery for Autoregressive Processes), a constraint-based method for lagged causal edge discovery in sparse autoregressive time series. CEDAR screens candidate cross-variable lags using AR(1)-residualized, U-centered distance correlation, then applies two targeted conditional-independence tests per significant cross-variable lag candidate and accepts at most one lag per ordered pair. A stable MCI pruning step removes indirect edges, and optional deterministic C-nodes adjust for specified trend-like nonstationarity. In sparse regimes where few lags survive screening, CEDAR requires $O(d^2)$ CI tests after screening while retaining edge-level interpretability. CEDAR is most effective when data are scarce and variables exhibit lag-1 self-dynamics; methods with richer conditioning sets become preferable as $T$ grows or when higher-order autoregressive or simultaneous multi-lag effects are common.

</details>


### [173] [Perspective Latents as an Architectural Condition for Causal Emergence in Active Inference Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20708)
*Hongju Pae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper examines how causal emergence (measured by Φ_r) manifests in active inference agents, finding that architectural factors dominate overall Φ_r magnitude while learning effects are visible only in compositional patterns like decoupling and downward causation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent work shows Φ_r (causal emergence) increases with training and tracks reward improvement in reinforcement learning agents. For active inference agents (which operate without rewards through prediction), the paper investigates how this predictive organization relates to information-theoretic signatures of emergence.

Method: The study uses an active inference agent with a two-latent architecture: fast perception latent (z) and slow global latent (g). The g latent is driven by prediction error and structurally decoupled from policy gradients. Experiments are conducted in a reward-free environmental regime-switching protocol.

Result: Φ_r concentrates in the g latent; its aggregate magnitude is largely architectural and decreases with training. Learning effects become visible at compositional levels: decoupling flips from negative to positive and becomes regime-invariant, while downward causation carries regime-dependent adjustments.

Conclusion: The g latent is identified as the architectural locus of Φ_r-relevant temporal organization in active inference agents. Scalar Φ_r should not be interpreted as a direct index of learned integration, as architectural factors dominate overall magnitude while learning manifests in compositional patterns.

Abstract: A recent line of work measures causal emergence in reinforcement learning agents through Integrated Information Decomposition, reporting that $Φ_r$ grows with training and tracks reward improvement. For active inference, this raises the question of how reward-free predictive organization relates to such information-theoretic signatures. I test this within an active inference agent whose architecture separates a fast perception latent $z$ from a slow global latent $g$, where $g$ is driven by prediction error and structurally decoupled from policy gradients. In a reward-free environmental regime-switching protocol, $Φ_r$ concentrates in $g$; its aggregate magnitude is largely architectural and decreases with training. The substantive effect of learning becomes legible only at the atom-compositional level: decoupling flips sign from negative to positive and becomes regime-invariant under environmental change, while downward causation carries the regime-dependent adjustment. These results identify $g$ as the architectural locus of $Φ_r$-relevant temporal organization in an active inference agent, and argue against reading scalar $Φ_r$ as a direct index of learned integration.

</details>


### [174] [LLMs Get Lost in Evolving User Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20734)
*Jihoon Tack,Philippe Laban,Jennifer Neville*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs perform well in static, single-turn tasks but fail to track evolving user intent in multi-turn conversations, revealing a critical capability gap invisible to traditional evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation focuses on static, fully-specified tasks, but real-world interactions are dynamic where users reveal, revise, and redirect their intent incrementally during conversations. There's a gap in understanding how well LLMs track evolving user intent.

Method: The authors introduce a framework that transforms static single-turn tasks into dynamic multi-turn conversations where user intent evolves across turns (incrementally revealed, revised, redirected). This approach preserves original evaluation protocols, enabling reuse of existing benchmarks without new annotation.

Result: Across multiple tasks, strong performance in static settings does not transfer to evolving-intent settings, with substantial performance drops across different model families. LLMs struggle to faithfully track and act on users' evolving intent during conversations.

Conclusion: Today's LLMs have a fundamental gap in tracking and acting on evolving user intent - a critical capability for future collaborative agents that remains invisible to static evaluation methods.

Abstract: As LLMs become more capable, they are increasingly deployed as collaborative agents, taking on user-delegated tasks through iterative interaction. Yet genuine interaction is inherently dynamic: users rarely specify their intent upfront, instead disclosing, revising, and reshaping it as the conversation unfolds. Despite this, LLMs are still predominantly evaluated or trained in single-turn, fully-specified settings, leaving open a fundamental question: how well do LLMs track and act on user intent as it evolves over the course of a conversation? To study this, we introduce a framework that transforms static, single-turn tasks into dynamic multi-turn conversations in which the user's intent evolves across turns--incrementally revealed, revised, and at times redirected mid-conversation--while preserving each task's original evaluation protocol, enabling existing benchmarks to be reused as controlled testbeds without new annotation. Across multiple tasks, we surface a consistent phenomenon: strong static-setting performance does not transfer to the evolving-intent setting, with substantial drops across model families. Our findings point to a fundamental gap: today's LLMs do not yet faithfully track and act on the user's evolving intent, a capability invisible to static evaluation yet critical for future collaborative agents.

</details>


### [175] [Cardinality-Decomposed Loss: Matching Training Objectives to Relation Structure in Heterogeneous Recommendation Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20737)
*Parul Maheshwari,Amulya Paruchuri,Yiqing Zou,Alireza Sahami Shirazi,Farhad Farahani,Prakhar Mehrotra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Cardinality-Decomposed Loss (CDL) combining Cross Entropy and BPR to handle heterogeneous bipartite graphs with varying cardinality relations, preventing attribute embedding collapse while maintaining recommendation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional recommendation systems use Bayesian Personalized Ranking (BPR) loss for all graph components, but BPR causes attribute embeddings to collapse to near-random geometry when handling one-to-one relations. This silent failure pollutes user embeddings and hurts downstream tasks like personalization and segmentation, while remaining invisible to standard ranking metrics.

Method: Cardinality-Decomposed Loss (CDL) combines Cross Entropy (CE) for one-to-one relations (attributes) and BPR for one-to-many relations (user-item preferences). Uses a lambda parameter to balance the trade-off between losses. Evaluated on five datasets with different structural configurations and analyzed graph properties like semantic alignment and topology leakage.

Result: CDL consistently improves discriminability in attribute embeddings across all datasets. Ranking (NDCG) improves when attributes carry meaningful preference signal, but conflicts occur when correlation is weak. Lambda-sweep reveals dataset behavior is governed by semantic alignment (whether attribute predicts preferences) and topology leakage (whether graph connectivity already encodes it).

Conclusion: CDL effectively handles heterogeneous relations with varying cardinalities in graph neural networks for recommendation systems. It prevents attribute embedding collapse while optimizing for both types of relations, with the lambda parameter providing flexibility to navigate trade-offs based on dataset characteristics.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks trained on heterogenous bipartite graphs form a common basis in recommendation systems. These graphs often express relations that vary in cardinality, for example, user-item preferences are one-to-many and user-attribute features are one-to-one. Traditionally, a unique loss function is applied for all of the network components which is often Bayesian Personalized Ranking (BPR). While BPR works well for the recommendation task, we find that it causes attribute embeddings to collapse to near-random geometry -- a silent failure that leaves standard ranking metrics largely unaffected and therefore invisible to conventional evaluation. This in turn pollutes user node embeddings, which are shaped by both edge types simultaneously, hurting downstream tasks like personalization, segmentation, etc. Here we propose a Cardinality-Decomposed Loss (CDL) that combines both Cross Entropy (CE) and BPR to enable the model to collectively optimize for relations across cardinalities. We confirm this CE-BPR conflict by showing the two losses compete in the shared encoder's parameter space. We evaluate CDL on five datasets spanning two structural configurations -- one-to-one attributes on user nodes (MovieLens-1M, Last.fm-360K, PayPal Audience Factory, BookCrossing) and on item nodes (Yelp) -- and find that CDL consistently improves discriminability in attribute embeddings. We also show that ranking (NDCG) improves when attributes carry meaningful preference signal, but conflicts with it when the correlation is weak. We use a lambda parameter to navigate this trade-off, and a lambda-sweep reveals that dataset behavior is governed by two graph properties -- semantic alignment and topology leakage. Semantic alignment measures whether the attribute predicts preferences, while topology leakage measures whether the graph's connectivity already encodes it.

</details>


### [176] [Adaptive Confidence-weighted Expansion for Trustworthy Multi-Omics Multimodal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20742)
*Mohammad Raahemi,Ali Sekhavati,Alireza Maleki,Hamid Nasiri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACE (Adaptive Confidence-weighted Expansion) is a new multimodal fusion framework that improves trustworthiness by generating complementary modalities and using dual-level confidence mechanisms to adaptively weigh modalities and provide trust scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal learning models have poor performance with noisy/uninformative data streams and lack robust mechanisms for assessing data quality and providing trustable confidence scores, which prevents their deployment in safety-critical settings like medical prognosis.

Method: ACE enhances multimodal space by generating new complementary modalities from intra-modality correlations, then uses a dual-level confidence mechanism: (1) adaptively reweighs all modalities by their reliability before fusion, and (2) estimates a global trust score over the final decision.

Result: Tested on four multi-omics datasets (BRCA, KIPAN, LGG, ROSMAP), ACE significantly outperforms state-of-the-art algorithms in both classification performance and confidence calibration.

Conclusion: ACE provides a more stable and robust data fusion method that facilitates the use of multimodal learning for high-stakes problems by enhancing trustworthiness through adaptive confidence weighting.

Abstract: Multimodal learning is a robust approach to improve predictive performance in applications such as medical prognosis. However, the clinical applicability of models that use multimodal learning is hampered by their poor performance under noisy or uninformative data streams. Present fusion approaches often lack robust mechanisms for the dynamic assessment of data quality and for the provision of a trustable confidence score on the final prediction. This dissuades their deployment in safety-critical settings. To address these limitations, we introduce Adaptive Confidence-weighted Expansion (ACE), a novel framework to enhance the trustworthiness of multimodal fusion models. ACE first enhances the multimodal space by generating new, complementary modalities from intra-modality correlations. It then employs a dual-level confidence mechanism that (1) adaptively reweighs all modalities by their reliability before fusion and (2) estimates a global trust score over the fused, final decision. To evaluate ACE, we used four challenging multi-omics datasets (BRCA, KIPAN, LGG, and ROSMAP). ACE significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art algorithms in both classification performance and confidence calibration.
