Our framework provides a more stable and robust data fusion method that facilitates the use of multimodal learning in addressing high-stakes problems.

</details>


### [177] [GaugeQuant: Online Learning of Quantization-Optimal Bases from LLM Symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20757)
*Miguel P. Bento,João Seabra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GaugeQuant is an in-training quantization method that leverages transformer symmetries to minimize activation outliers without changing the language modeling objective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers have internal continuous symmetries that leave outputs invariant but affect quantization. Current quantization methods often require calibration data, quantization simulation, and post-processing. There's a need for efficient in-training quantization that doesn't alter the core training objective.

Method: Introduces a LogSumExp term to the loss function that breaks the internal symmetries, selecting a basis that minimizes activation outliers. Uses a stop-gradient operator to ensure only rotation matrices are updated, keeping the language modeling objective unchanged. Works without calibration data or quantization simulation.

Result: With LLaMA-2 7B under W4A4 quantization (group size 128), perplexity drops from 8.22 to 6.73, competing with post-training methods. Under W4A16 quantization, perplexity drops from 11.16 to 5.45. Adds negligible training overhead.

Conclusion: GaugeQuant enables efficient in-training quantization by leveraging transformer symmetries, achieving competitive results without calibration data or quantization simulation while maintaining the original training objective.

Abstract: Transformers are known to have internal continuous symmetries that leave outputs invariant, while modifying quantization. GaugeQuant leverages this in-training by introducing a LogSumExp term to the loss that breaks the symmetries, thus selecting a basis that minimizes activation outliers. A stop-gradient operator ensures that only rotation matrices are updated, yielding the language modeling objective completely unaltered. Our requires no specific calibration data, no quantization simulation, and adds negligible training overhead. With the LLaMA-2 7B model under W4A4 quantization with group size 128, perplexity drops from 8.22 to 6.73, competing with post-training methods that require frozen models and calibration datasets. Under W4A16, perplexity drops from 11.16 to 5.45. Code is available at https://github.com/MPedraBento/gauge-quant.

</details>


### [178] [Memory-Computation Tradeoffs in Semi Amortized Parametric Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20769)
*Shijie Pan,Agustin Castellano,Zeyu Shen,Enrique Mallada*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the tradeoff between offline memory storage and online computation for learning-enabled optimization systems, establishing theoretical bounds on how much memory is needed to achieve desired accuracy under fixed online computation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-enabled decision systems use offline data/computation to reduce online compute costs, but there's limited general understanding of how much offline information is needed to achieve desired accuracy under fixed online computation budgets.

Method: Analyzes amortized parametric optimization framework: offline phase stores finite memory of solved instances, online phase uses nonparametric predictor from stored solutions with K steps of projected gradient descent. Analyzes smooth convex parametric optimization over compact domains.

Result: Establishes matching upper/lower memory bounds for μ-strongly convex objectives to guarantee ε-accuracy under fixed K. For convex objectives with β-growth condition, obtains near-matching bounds with phase transition in K. Provides framework quantifying memory cost of acceleration and identifies key quantities: convergence rate of online optimizer and Lipschitz sensitivity of solution map.

Conclusion: Provides theoretical foundations for understanding memory-computation-accuracy tradeoffs in learning-enabled optimization systems, with experimental validation on ridge regression showing predicted tradeoffs.

Abstract: Learning-enabled decision systems often use offline data or computation to reduce online compute cost. Despite the empirical success of such approaches, there is limited general understanding of how much offline information is needed to achieve a desired accuracy under a fixed online computation budget. We study this question through the lens of amortized parametric optimization: an offline phase stores a finite memory of solved problem instances, and an online phase produces a solution to a new instance by retrieving a warm start and applying $K$ steps of projected gradient descent. We analyze this setup for smooth convex parametric optimization over a compact domain, using a nonparametric predictor built from the stored offline solutions. For $μ$-strongly convex objectives, we establish matching upper and lower bounds on the memory required to guarantee $\varepsilon$-accuracy under a fixed online iteration budget $K$. For convex objectives satisfying a $β$-growth condition ($β>2$), we obtain near-matching bounds and identify a phase transition in $K$ beyond which additional memory provides no benefit. We further provide a general proof framework that (i) explicitly quantifies the memory cost of acceleration---how much offline memory is required to achieve a prescribed speedup over the unaided online optimizer---and (ii) identifies two key quantities driving this cost: the convergence rate of the online optimizer and the Lipschitz sensitivity of the solution map to the problem parameter. Experiments on parameterized ridge regression confirm the predicted memory--computation--accuracy tradeoffs.

</details>


### [179] [Toward Mechanistic Interpretability of an AI Foundation Model Fine-Tuned for Atmospheric Chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20778)
*Jason Y. Hu,Ivan Higuera-Mendieta,Patrick Obin Sturm,Makoto M. Kelp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers analyze Microsoft's Aurora weather foundation model to determine if it actually learns atmospheric chemistry mechanisms or just exploits statistical patterns when fine-tuned for air quality forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weather forecasting foundation models are being fine-tuned for air quality prediction, but it's unclear whether they learn actual chemical/physical mechanisms or just exploit statistical patterns in training data. This gap matters as these models increasingly inform environmental policy decisions.

Method: Researchers examine Microsoft's Aurora model by: 1) imposing controlled chemical perturbations on forecasts, 2) testing against known photochemical relationships, 3) examining internal representations that generate forecasts, and 4) using sparse autoencoders to identify causal components.

Result: Aurora captures first-order ozone-nitrogen responses but fails to enforce chemical constraints like process-based models. It generates chemically inconsistent species combinations and relaxes emission features (like wildfire plumes) toward background. Internal representations remain meteorology-focused with little chemistry-specific structure, and identified causal components don't map cleanly to atmospheric processes.

Conclusion: The study provides a framework for testing AI forecasting systems' understanding of atmospheric chemistry. Models should be judged not just by forecast skill but also by internal mechanisms, especially as they inform policy decisions.

Abstract: Weather forecasting foundation models (FMs) are increasingly fine-tuned to predict air quality, offering fast global pollution forecasts at lower computational cost than conventional chemical transport models. These FMs are typically trained on reanalysis data and generate forecasts through autoregressive rollout. They do not explicitly represent governing physical or chemical processes. Therefore, high forecast skill does not reveal whether a model has learned physical mechanisms or exploits statistical regularities in its training data. Here, we present the first study of what a FM fine-tuned for atmospheric chemistry has learned by examining Microsoft's Aurora model. We impose controlled chemical perturbations on its forecasts and test them against known photochemical relationships. We then examine the internal representations that generate these forecasts. We find that Aurora captures a first-order ozone response to reactive nitrogen but does not enforce the chemical constraints that a process-based model encodes. It generates chemically inconsistent combinations of related species and relaxes localized emission features such as wildfire plumes toward background. Internally, its representations remain largely organized around the meteorology inherited during pretraining, with little structure specific to chemistry. Using sparse autoencoders, we identify internal components that causally control the chemical forecast but do not map cleanly onto individual atmospheric processes. This work provides a framework for testing whether AI forecasting systems learn atmospheric chemistry from reanalysis data. As these models are increasingly positioned to inform environmental policy decisions, we argue that composition forecasts should also be judged by their internal mechanisms rather than by benchmark skill alone.

</details>


### [180] [Synthetic minority data is redundant or invalid: a data-dependent validity theory and a de-biased test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20787)
*Ahmad B. Hassanat,Ahmad S. Tarawneh,Ghada A. Altarawneh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Traditional methods for generating synthetic minority class examples in imbalanced learning have been validated using a biased check that always passes. The authors introduce a de-biased validation approach showing that most synthetic data is invalid, fails to improve classification, and often harms model calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For two decades, class-imbalanced learning has relied on synthetic minority oversampling methods (like SMOTE) that are validated using a circular check - scoring synthetic points against the same data that generated them. This creates a validation method that cannot fail, masking potential issues with synthetic data quality.

Method: The authors propose a de-biased validation approach that treats validity as a population quantity: the probability that a synthetic point truly belongs to the minority class. They develop a consistent estimator that scores synthetic points against withheld real data. They conduct a comprehensive audit across 91 oversampling methods, 3 classifiers, and diverse datasets from medicine and finance, including a custom generator engineered to pass the classical biased check.

Result: The classical biased test underestimates true invalidity in 96-99% of cases, while the de-biased estimator closely tracks actual validity. No method cleared both validity and usefulness bars: improvements over trivial baselines were negligible (median below 0.01 F1), and most methods damaged model calibration. Validity is shown to be a data property rather than a method property - class overlap creates an invalidity floor that no faithful generator can overcome.

Conclusion: The standard practice of synthetic minority oversampling is fundamentally flawed: it produces invalid synthetic data when classes overlap and is redundant when classes separate. The authors release their audit as a pip-installable test and propose flipping the burden of proof - synthetic minority data must now demonstrate both validity and information gain on the actual data being used.

Abstract: For two decades, the standard remedy for class-imbalanced learning has been to fabricate synthetic minority examples, and the standard evidence of their validity has been a check that cannot fail: synthetic points are scored against the very data that generated them. We de-bias the check. Validity becomes a population quantity -- the probability that a synthetic point truly belongs to the minority class -- with a consistent estimator that scores synthetic points against withheld real data. Where held-out ground truth is available, the classical test underestimates true invalidity in 96-99% of method-by-imbalance-ratio cells, while the de-biased estimator tracks it closely. We prove validity is a property of the data, not the method: class overlap sets an invalidity floor no faithful generator escapes, making oversampling redundant where classes separate and invalid where they overlap. Across 91 methods, three classifiers, and datasets spanning medicine and finance -- including a generator engineered to pass the classical check -- none clears both bars: gains over the best trivial baseline are noise-thin (median below 0.01 F1, a decision threshold's reach), and most damage calibration. We release the audit as a pip-installable test and flip the burden of proof: synthetic minority data must now demonstrate, on the data at hand, both validity and information gain.

</details>


### [181] [Memoir: Should a Model Write to Its Memory While It Thinks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20792)
*Jaber Jaber,Osama Jaber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Memoir introduces a memory architecture combining fast per-sample memory with shared slow parameters, but experimental results show that allowing memory rewriting during pondering iterations hurts learning speed compared to read-only pondering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper explores the riskiest coupling in memory architectures where each pondering iteration can rewrite the fast memory tier that the same iteration reads from, testing whether this dynamic memory rewriting is beneficial for learning.

Method: The authors test Memoir on procedural associative recall with key interference, comparing two arms: coupled (allowing memory rewriting) vs read-only pondering (only reading memory). Both use identical architectures (81,738 parameters), compute budgets, data, optimizer, and seeds for fair comparison.

Result: After 240 training steps, read-only recall (0.6557) significantly outperforms coupled recall (0.5203). The read-only advantage has a 95% interval of [0.0431, 0.2277] and wins on 10/12 seeds. After 960 steps, both reach perfect recall, showing the effect is a learning speed penalty, not capability limitation. Kernel optimization also reduced forward time by 61%.

Conclusion: Contrary to predictions, memory rewriting during pondering doesn't corrupt learning signals but does impose a significant learning speed penalty. The read-only pondering approach learns faster, though both can eventually achieve perfect performance given enough training.

Abstract: Memoir combines per-sample fast memory, shared slow parameters, variable-depth latent recurrence, and a future-latent energy objective. We test its riskiest coupling: each pondering iteration may rewrite the fast tier that the same iteration reads. On procedural associative recall with key interference, we compare a coupled arm against an otherwise identical read-only pondering arm. Both arms contain 81,738 parameters, including 76,362 trainable parameters, and use matched declared forward multiply-accumulate counts, data, optimizer, schedule, and seeds. After 240 training steps across 12 seeds, coupled recall is 0.5203 with a 95 percent interval of [0.4522, 0.5883], while read-only recall is 0.6557 with [0.5953, 0.7160]. The arms are paired per seed, and the read-only lead of 0.1354 gives a paired t of 3.23 on 11 degrees of freedom with a 95 percent interval of [0.0431, 0.2277] on the difference, winning on 10 of 12 seeds. After 960 steps across 8 seeds, both arms reach 1.0000, so the measured effect is a learning-speed penalty at a fixed budget, not a demonstrated capability penalty. That longer control is ceiling limited, leaving convergence on a non-saturating task unmeasured. A predicted failure in which memory rewriting corrupts the energy signal did not occur: the energy margin grew and held. Kernel restructuring also reduced delta-rule forward time from 0.907 ms to 0.351 ms on the stated device. Code and evidence are available at https://github.com/RightNow-AI/Memoir

</details>


### [182] [External Clustering Validation by the Homogeneity-Parsimony Trade-off](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20799)
*Andreas Tiffeau-Mayer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes normalized scores for clustering evaluation that balance homogeneity (informative about class labels) and parsimony (avoiding unnecessary fragmentation), based on a modified information bottleneck principle with monotonic properties under cluster refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scalar metrics for evaluating clusterings against known classes obscure a fundamental trade-off: clusterings should be both informative about class labels and avoid unnecessary fragmentation. There's a need for evaluation criteria that properly quantify this trade-off.

Method: The authors develop normalized scores of cluster homogeneity and parsimony based on a modified information bottleneck principle that doesn't reward lossy compression. They mathematically prove these scores vary monotonically under cluster refinement. They also extend this framework beyond Shannon entropies to derive set-matching and pair-based counterparts, unifying common evaluation criteria.

Result: The proposed homogeneity and parsimony scores have intuitive monotonic properties under cluster refinement. The pair-based formulation recovers the receiver operating characteristic of binary classifiers, demonstrating a deep connection between clustering evaluation and classification metrics. The framework provides utility for feature selection and algorithm comparison by identifying Pareto-optimal solutions in the homogeneity-parsimony trade-off space.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a principled approach to evaluating clusterings that properly captures the trade-off between being informative about class labels and avoiding fragmentation. By considering homogeneity and parsimony scores jointly, it enables clearer understanding of clustering operating points and identification of optimal solutions.

Abstract: Scalar metrics are often used to evaluate clusterings against known classes, but they can obscure a fundamental trade-off: clusterings should be informative about class labels while avoiding unnecessary fragmentation. Here we describe normalized scores of cluster homogeneity and parsimony that quantify this trade-off. These scores build on the information bottleneck principle, modified to not reward lossy compression. We show by example and mathematical proof that our definitions of these scores have the intuitive property of varying monotonically under cluster refinement in contrast to related proposals. Extending the information-theoretic framework beyond Shannon entropies, we furthermore derive set-matching and pair-based counterparts of the homogeneity and parsimony scores. These unify commonly used evaluation criteria and show that, in the pair-based setting, the homogeneity-parsimony trade-off recovers the receiver operating characteristic of binary classifiers. We demonstrate the framework's utility for feature selection and algorithm comparison, illustrating how considering scores jointly can clarify clustering operating points and identify Pareto-optimal solutions.

</details>


### [183] [New Complexity-Theoretic Frontiers of Tractability for Neural Network Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20811)
*Cornelius Brand,Robert Ganian,Mathis Rocton*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents novel polynomial-time algorithms for optimally training neural networks with linear and ReLU activation functions, pushing beyond previous tractability boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite neural networks' fundamental role in machine learning, there's incomplete understanding of the computational complexity of optimal training, even for simple activation functions like linear and ReLU. Recent progress has mainly focused on establishing lower bounds rather than identifying tractable network architectures.

Method: The paper obtains algorithmic upper bounds for training linear- and ReLU-activated neural networks to optimality. For ReLU networks, it develops polynomial-time tractability for architectures where hidden neurons have out-degree 1. For linear networks, it introduces algorithms for architectures satisfying a novel data throughput condition.

Result: For ReLU networks: polynomial-time tractability established for all architectures where hidden neurons have out-degree 1, improving upon Arora et al.'s previous algorithm. For linear networks: first non-trivial polynomial-time solvable class identified via algorithms for architectures satisfying a data throughput condition.

Conclusion: The paper successfully pushes the boundaries of tractability for neural network training, providing novel polynomial-time algorithms for both linear and ReLU networks that go beyond previous state-of-the-art results.

Abstract: In spite of the fundamental role of neural networks in contemporary machine learning research, our understanding of the computational complexity of optimally training neural networks remains incomplete even when dealing with the simplest kinds of activation functions. Indeed, while there has been a number of very recent results that establish ever-tighter lower bounds for the problem under linear and ReLU activation functions, less progress has been made towards the identification of novel polynomial-time tractable network architectures. In this article we obtain novel algorithmic upper bounds for training linear- and ReLU-activated neural networks to optimality which push the boundaries of tractability for these problems beyond the previous state of the art. In particular, for ReLU networks we establish the polynomial-time tractability of all architectures where hidden neurons have an out-degree of $1$, improving upon the previous algorithm of Arora, Basu, Mianjy and Mukherjee. On the other hand, for networks with linear activation functions we identify the first non-trivial polynomial-time solvable class of networks by obtaining an algorithm that can optimally train network architectures satisfying a novel data throughput condition.

</details>


### [184] [Robust Asynchronous Q-Learning under Reward and State Corruption via Batching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20822)
*Sreejeet Maity,Aritra Mitra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Novel robust asynchronous Q-learning algorithm (BR-Async-Q) that maintains performance despite adversarial corruption of both rewards and states, with optimal corruption dependence when only rewards are corrupted.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is reinforcement learning in harsh environments where feedback can be adversarially corrupted, requiring robust algorithms that can handle perturbations in both reward and state observations following the Huber contamination model.

Method: BR-Async-Q algorithm: epoch-based robust Q-learning that partitions online data stream into batches to reduce variance and constructs robust estimates of the Bellman optimality operator using batched data to defend against corruption.

Result: Proves high-probability ℓ∞ error bound matching vanilla Q-learning up to small additive term scaling with corruption fraction. First robustness guarantee for asynchronous Q-learning with both reward and state corruption. Minimax optimal corruption dependence when only rewards are corrupted.

Conclusion: BR-Async-Q provides effective defense against adversarial corruption in RL environments, maintaining performance comparable to standard Q-learning while offering robustness guarantees, with optimal corruption handling in reward-only corruption scenarios.

Abstract: Motivated by reinforcement learning in harsh environments, we consider the problem of learning an optimal policy subject to adversarially corrupted feedback. Specifically, at each time-step, an adversary can perturb both the reward and state observations of the learner following the Huber contamination model. To defend against such data corruption, we propose {\texttt{BR-Async-Q}}: a novel, epoch-based, robust \(Q\)-learning algorithm built upon two key ideas: (i) partitioning the online data stream into batches to reduce variance, and (ii) constructing robust estimates of the Bellman optimality operator using such batched data. We prove a high-probability $\ell_\infty$ error bound for {\texttt{BR-Async-Q}} that matches that for vanilla \(Q\)-learning, up to a small additive term that scales with the fraction of corrupted samples. To our knowledge, this provides the first robustness guarantee for asynchronous \(Q\)-learning subject to both reward and state corruption. Furthermore, when only rewards are corrupted, the dependence of our algorithm's bound on the corruption fraction is minimax optimal.

</details>


### [185] [Beyond Heavy Log Curation: Perplexity-Based APT Detection via Unsupervised, Context-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20832)
*Shoya Otsu,Kei Suzuki,Toshiaki Koike-Akino,Jing Liu,Ye Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAPTAIN is a perplexity-based APT detection system using pre-trained language models with minimal preprocessing, achieving competitive performance while reducing development and operational costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: APTs are hard to detect due to the rarity of attack events in large logs, expensive investigations, and scaling challenges. Existing ML approaches require heavily curated training data and sophisticated preprocessing pipelines that demand substantial domain expertise and engineering resources.

Method: CAPTAIN uses pre-trained language models with domain-agnostic preprocessing to score long log entries via perplexity. It encodes recent history with an encoder model and Q-Former-style bridge, injects compact context tokens into decoder input to incorporate temporal context, and applies smoothing filters to the perplexity time series for stability.

Result: CAPTAIN competes with strong existing baselines across APT-oriented benchmarks while remaining robust under substantially less curated inputs, reducing the development and operational costs of advanced log preprocessing.

Conclusion: CAPTAIN provides an effective APT detection approach that leverages pre-trained language models with minimal preprocessing, offering competitive performance while significantly reducing the engineering overhead and domain expertise requirements of traditional detection systems.

Abstract: Advanced Persistent Threats (APTs) remain difficult to detect because only a small fraction of events in large-scale logs are attack-related, and investigation is expensive and hard to scale. Prior machine-learning approaches can reduce analyst workload, but they often rely on heavily curated training data and sophisticated preprocessing pipelines. Building and maintaining such pipelines require substantial domain expertise and engineering cost. Motivated by insights from a study of a strong APT detection baseline, we propose CAPTAIN (Context-Augmented Perplexity-based Threat Activity log detectIoN), a perplexity-based detector that leverages general, pre-trained language models with minimal, domain-agnostic preprocessing, enabling robust scoring of long, minimally processed log entries. CAPTAIN encodes recent history with an encoder model and a Q-Former-style bridge, then injects the compact context tokens into the decoder input so that perplexity reflects temporal context. To improve stability, CAPTAIN additionally applies smoothing filters to the perplexity time series. Across APT-oriented benchmarks, CAPTAIN competes with strong existing baselines and remains robust under substantially less curated inputs, that reduces the development and operational cost of advanced log preprocessing.

</details>


### [186] [Offline RL with Hierarchical Action Chunking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20834)
*Ahad Jawaid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiQC: Offline hierarchical RL method that combines latent planning with action chunking to overcome horizon limitations, achieving superior performance on long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline goal-conditioned RL struggles with long-horizon tasks due to the "curse of horizon" where value estimation errors compound through long chains of Bellman backups. Existing hierarchical approaches help but suffer from myopic low-level execution and biased value estimates.

Method: Hierarchical Implicit Q-Chunking (HiQC) combines high-level latent planning with low-level action chunking. It conditions the low-level critic on temporally extended action sequences, enabling unbiased k-step value backups and compressing the horizon at both planning and execution levels.

Result: HiQC achieves the highest aggregate performance among compared methods on the OGBench suite, with particularly large gains on long-horizon navigation tasks like humanoid-giant. The theoretical analysis shows tighter bounds on value error compared to standard hierarchy or flat chunking alone.

Conclusion: The dual decomposition approach of HiQC effectively addresses the curse of horizon in offline goal-conditioned RL by combining hierarchical planning with action chunking, providing both theoretical guarantees and empirical improvements on challenging long-horizon tasks.

Abstract: Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (RL) holds the promise of learning general-purpose policies from static datasets. However, scaling these methods to long-horizon tasks remains a challenge due to the curse of horizon, where value estimation errors can compound through long chains of bootstrapped Bellman backups. Existing hierarchical approaches mitigate this by decomposing tasks into subgoals, yet they often rely on low-level controllers that suffer from myopic execution and biased value estimates. In this work, we propose Hierarchical Implicit Q-Chunking (HiQC), an offline goal-conditioned RL algorithm that combines high-level latent planning with low-level action chunking. By conditioning the low-level critic on temporally extended action sequences, HiQC enables unbiased k-step value backups, compressing the horizon at both the planning and execution levels. We theoretically demonstrate that this dual decomposition results in a tighter bound on value error under a bounded per-backup error model compared to standard hierarchy or flat chunking alone. Empirically, HiQC achieves the highest aggregate performance among the compared methods on the OGBench suite, with its largest gains on long-horizon navigation tasks such as humanoid-giant.

</details>


### [187] [Multilevel Graph Wavelet Compressed Sensing with Scale-Aware Neural Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20857)
*Amirhossein Nouranizadeh,Sarang Rajendra Patil,Alan John Varghese,Varsha Narayanan,Amit Chakraborty,Mengjia Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GWCS is a learning-based graph signal compression framework that uses spectral graph wavelet transforms and scale-aware GNNs to achieve high compression ratios while maintaining reconstruction fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific ML methods require large simulated datasets, making data preparation and model training expensive. Existing methods lack efficient compression for graph-structured simulation data.

Method: GWCS combines a nonparametric multilevel importance sampler to retain high-energy wavelet coefficients with a scale-aware graph neural network for signal reconstruction from sparse coefficients.

Result: GWCS achieves high reconstruction fidelity and substantial data compression compared to graph signal sampling methods and graph autoencoder baselines across multiple PDE simulation datasets.

Conclusion: The framework provides an effective offline compression solution for graph signals, enabling more efficient data management for scientific ML applications while maintaining interpretability through sparse wavelet representations.

Abstract: Scientific machine learning methods such as neural operators and physics-informed neural networks have advanced engineering applications and inverse problems, but their training typically requires large volumes of simulated data. This makes data preparation and model training expensive. We propose Graph Wavelet Compressed Sensing (GWCS), a learning-based framework for offline compression of graph signals by representing them as sparse, interpretable wavelet-domain representations using the spectral graph wavelet transform. The framework combines a nonparametric multilevel importance sampler, which retains high-energy wavelet coefficients within each scale for a given compression ratio, with a scale-aware graph neural network that reconstructs the signal from the sparse coefficients. We evaluate the proposed framework on synthetic approximately band-limited graph signals over random graphs and four PDE simulation datasets over meshes, which include Turbulent Radiative Layer, Viscoelastic Instability, Kolmogorov Flow, and Dynamic Stall. We compare against graph signal sampling methods and graph autoencoder baselines. Results demonstrate that the framework achieves high reconstruction fidelity and substantial data compression compared to existing benchmarks.

</details>


### [188] [Position Bias is Hidden Behind Ceiling Effects: A Permutation Diagnostic for LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20864)
*Hiroki Tamba*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Position bias in LLM evaluations is only statistically detectable within a "Goldilocks zone" (60-95% accuracy), with two distinct bias mechanisms observed. Standard MMLU places frontier models above this detection band, making bias unmeasurable rather than absent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current measurements of position bias in multiple-choice LLM evaluation rely on single answer-order shuffles, which confound bias signals with content-level noise and sampling stochasticity. There's a need for more rigorous, systematic measurement methods.

Method: Developed inspect_permute, an open-source extension to inspect_ai framework that runs exhaustive answer-order permutations per question. Applied to four LLM vendors (gpt-4o-mini, claude-haiku-4-5, gemini-2.5-flash, grok-3) on five MMLU subjects with 24,000 API calls at temperature-0, using pre-registered predictions with public SHA-256 hash before half the data was observed.

Result: Position bias is only statistically detectable within a 60-95% base-accuracy "Goldilocks zone." Two mechanism types identified: monotone A-to-D decrease (processing_load in low-tier models) and non-monotone D-drop (content_ambiguity in narrow capability band). Frontier-tier models in standard MMLU are above detection band, making bias unmeasurable rather than absent.

Conclusion: This work brackets the detectable region of position-bias measurement and enables verifiable testing of the central question. Standard MMLU's positioning of frontier models above the detection band means absence of bias signal should be interpreted as not measurable rather than unbiased.

Abstract: Position bias in multiple-choice LLM evaluation is widely cited as a confound in capability comparisons, but published measurements rely on single answer-order shuffles whose results confound the bias signal with content-level noise and sampling stochasticity. I introduce inspect_permute, an open-source extension to the inspect_ai evaluation framework that runs exhaustive answer-order permutations per question and reports the chi-squared / Cramer V signature of position bias with bootstrap confidence intervals. I apply the tool across four vendors (gpt-4o-mini, claude-haiku-4-5, gemini-2.5-flash, grok-3) on five MMLU subjects, 24,000 API calls under temperature-0 generation, with falsifier predictions pre-registered via a public SHA-256 hash before half the data was observed. Position bias turns out to be statistically detectable only within a roughly 60-95% base-accuracy Goldilocks zone. Below it, processing-load dominance swamps subject-specific signal; above it, ceiling effects compress the variance below the chi-squared test resolution. Detectable cells separate into two mechanism types: monotone A-to-D decrease (processing_load, in low-tier models) and non-monotone D-drop (content_ambiguity, in a narrow capability band). Standard MMLU places every frontier-tier model above the detection band, so absence of signal there should be read as not measurable, not unbiased. Together with the ceiling-effect characterisation in arXiv:2606.26185, this work brackets the detectable region of position-bias measurement and makes the field central question askable in a verifiable form. Package, data, preregistration under MIT.

</details>


### [189] [TwistedMerge: Certified Higher-Order Diagnostics and Abstention for Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20887)
*Ting Gong,Shitan Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Model merging often fails because pairwise alignments don't guarantee global consistency; TwistedMerge provides a certification framework that detects when merging is impossible and abstains rather than giving bad results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging is challenging because pairwise alignment between checkpoints doesn't guarantee globally consistent alignment across all models, leading to failures when trying to merge multiple models.

Method: Formulate merging as finite descent problem; develop TwistedMerge pipeline with four stages: fixed-chart averaging, synchronization-removable gauge inconsistency, certified central obstruction analysis, and nonabelian holonomy detection.

Result: Shows that nonzero cycle residuals alone don't indicate fundamental obstruction; trained low-rank-adapter audits reveal naive factor averaging depends on GLr representation; on natural checkpoints, no central obstruction certified.

Conclusion: Descent theory provides falsifiable certification framework for model merging - can detect when merging is impossible and abstain rather than produce unreliable results, addressing fundamental alignment consistency problems.

Abstract: Model merging combines independently trained or fine-tuned models, but pairwise alignability does not imply globally consistent alignment. We formulate merging as a finite descent problem in which checkpoints are local objects, alignment maps are transitions, and cycle products are residuals. TwistedMerge is a conservative certification pipeline that separates fixed-chart averaging, synchronization-removable gauge inconsistency, a certified central obstruction on a specified comparison complex, and nonabelian holonomy. A residual is promoted to a cohomology class only after inverse-consistency, coefficient-identification, centrality, and closure tests; otherwise the method abstains and returns an ordinary or synchronized fallback. We prove a constant-edge no-go result, frozen-complex three-way and predeclared-family error-control theorems, and a refinement test for comparison-complex sensitivity. A planted neural alignment defect is removed by cycle-consistent synchronization, showing that a nonzero cycle score alone is not a higher obstruction. Controlled central systems recover the predicted non-coboundary and projective-rank behavior, while noisy estimates move from certification to abstention without false lifts on the tested controls. A trained low-rank-adapter audit shows that naive factor averaging depends on the chosen GLr representative, whereas global factor synchronization and dense-delta SVD are stable. On natural checkpoint collections, cycle residuals do not predict merge degradation and no natural central or period-index class is certified. The results position descent theory as a falsifiable certification and abstention framework.

</details>


### [190] [Information-Theoretically Secure Aggregation for Lightweight Federated Learning: Resilient to Dropouts and Adversaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20890)
*Hyeong-Gun Joo,Songnam Hong,Dong-Joon Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Lightweight secure aggregation framework for sign-based federated learning that ensures information-theoretic security while reducing communication costs by 99.5% and latency by 85.7%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device FL enables privacy-preserving training but sign-based methods like signSGD expose gradient signs to inference attacks, while existing secure aggregation schemes are either incompatible or too computationally/communication-heavy for resource-constrained devices.

Method: Proposes a framework that securely computes majority vote polynomial through single-round secure multiplication. Uses two key techniques: inverse-form exponent reduction (halves MV polynomial degree) and single-round secure multiplication (linear offline complexity with single online communication).

Result: Reduces online communication by up to 99.5% and latency by up to 85.7% compared to conventional approaches. Achieves robustness against dropouts and adversarial behaviors with accuracy gains of up to 20.65% and 10.74% respectively through MDS-code-based decoding.

Conclusion: The framework establishes a practical foundation for large-scale, low-latency, and information-theoretically secure aggregation in sign-based FL, offering both security and efficiency improvements.

Abstract: On-device federated learning (FL) enables privacy-preserving and personalized model training on resource-constrained devices such as smartphones and IoT nodes. To reduce communication cost, sign-based methods (e.g., signSGD) transmit one-bit gradients. However, exposing gradient signs makes them vulnerable to inference attacks, while existing secure aggregation schemes are often incompatible with such methods or incur significant computational and communication overhead. We propose a lightweight and information-theoretically secure aggregation framework tailored for sign-based FL. The framework securely computes the majority vote (MV) polynomial through single-round secure multiplication, ensuring end-to-end information-theoretic security under the honest-majority assumption while revealing only the final aggregated sign to the server. To enhance efficiency and scalability, we introduce two key techniques. First, inverse-form exponent reduction halves the effective MV polynomial degree, reducing both communication and computation costs. Second, we propose single-round secure multiplication, achieving linear offline complexity and storage with only a single online communication. Together, these techniques reduce online communication by up to 99.5% and latency by up to 85.7% compared to conventional approaches. Also, by leveraging inherent MDS-code-based decoding, the framework achieves robustness against both dropouts and adversarial behaviors, yielding accuracy gains of up to 20.65% and 10.74%, respectively. Overall, the proposed framework establishes a practical foundation for large-scale, low-latency, and information-theoretically secure aggregation in sign-based FL.

</details>


### [191] [HierarchicalDAEW: Domain-Aware Edge-Weighted Graph Convolution with Evidential Uncertainty for Multi-Section Spatial Gene Expression Prediction from H&E Histology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20896)
*Kritanu Chattopadhyay,Soumya Chatterjee,Ondrej Krejcar,Debotosh Bhattacharjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HierarchicalDAEW is a dual-graph architecture that predicts spatially resolved gene expression from H&E histology by explicitly modeling tissue heterogeneity through domain-aware edge weighting and fusing protein-protein interaction priors with tissue-specific co-expression, while providing calibrated uncertainty estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial transcriptomics assays are costly and technically demanding, limiting their clinical use. Predicting gene expression from routine H&E histology could bridge this gap, but current methods ignore tissue architecture and lack reliable uncertainty quantification.

Method: HierarchicalDAEW uses a dual-graph architecture: 1) A spot graph with Domain-Aware Edge-Weighted convolutional operator that learns separate projections for inter-domain, intra-domain, and boundary edges from Leiden clustering, explicitly treating tissue heterogeneity as structural signal. 2) A gene-level graph that fuses protein-protein interaction priors from STRING-DB with tissue-specific co-expression through learned attention gating, propagating predictions from landmark genes to broader panels. Uses evidential uncertainty estimation for better calibrated confidence intervals than Monte Carlo dropout.

Result: Outperforms thirteen published baselines across six human Visium sections (breast, colorectal, prostate, cerebellar tissue) with strongest correlation to ground-truth expression. Gains hold under multi-seed reproducibility checks and negative controls ruling out positional shortcuts. Ablations confirm necessity of domain-aware edge typing and hierarchical depth. Calibrated uncertainty estimates effectively identify low-confidence predictions for clinical review.

Conclusion: HierarchicalDAEW provides an effective solution for predicting spatially resolved gene expression from H&E histology by explicitly modeling tissue architecture, integrating biological priors, and offering reliable uncertainty quantification, enabling potential clinical translation where spatial transcriptomics remains inaccessible.

Abstract: Spatial transcriptomics assays remain costly and technically demanding, restricting transcriptome-wide profiling to specialist settings and preventing routine clinical deployment. Predicting spatially resolved gene expression from H&E histology could close this gap, yet current methods largely ignore the underlying tissue architecture and rarely quantify how their predictions can be trusted. We introduce HierarchicalDAEW, a dual-graph architecture that addresses both gaps. On the spot graph, a Domain-Aware Edge-Weighted convolutional operator learns separate projections for inter-domain, intra-domain, and boundary edges derived from Leiden clustering, allowing the model to treat tissue heterogeneity as an explicit structural signal rather than an implicit one. A second gene-level graph then fuses protein-protein interaction priors from STRING-DB with tissue-specific co-expression through learned attention gating, propagating predictions from a landmark gene set to a broader gene panel. Reliability is handled through evidential uncertainty estimation, which produces far better calibrated confidence intervals than Monte Carlo dropout under identical conditions. Across six human Visium sections spanning breast, colorectal, prostate, and cerebellar tissue, and against thirteen published baselines, HierarchicalDAEW achieves the strongest correlation with ground-truth expression, with gains that hold up under multi-seed reproducibility checks and negative controls that rule out positional shortcuts. Ablations further confirm that both the domain-aware edge typing and the hierarchical depth are necessary to this improvement, and calibrated uncertainty estimates identify low-confidence predictions for pathologist review before clinical action.

</details>


### [192] [Multi-turn RL with Structural and Performance Aware Rewards for CUDA Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20908)
*Quazi Ishtiaque Mahmud,Nesreen K. Ahmed,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CudaPerf is a reflective RL framework that combines verifiable execution rewards with structural code-aware rewards for optimized CUDA code generation, outperforming existing methods in both performance and correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR approaches for LLMs focus only on outcome-based signals like correctness and speedup, missing important structural properties of programs that are critical for generating optimized code, particularly for CUDA/parallel programming.

Method: CudaPerf uses a two-stage approach: 1) offline pairwise ranking module learns to distinguish strong/weak program candidates via contrastive comparisons, and 2) online RL training jointly optimizes correctness, performance, and structural efficiency through unified reward signals incorporating parallelization features like memory coalescing, occupancy, etc., with iterative refinement using execution feedback.

Result: CudaPerf significantly outperforms baselines: achieves up to 5X & 3.32X improvements in speedup, and 17% & 7% improvements in correctness for C to CUDA and PyTorch to CUDA transformations respectively, compared to Qwen-3-32B and CUDA Agent.

Conclusion: Incorporating structural code-aware rewards alongside verifiable execution rewards in a reflective RL framework enables superior optimized code generation, demonstrating the importance of considering both execution outcomes and program structure for effective parallel code optimization.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful technique to enhance the reasoning capacity of LLMs for optimized code generation. However, existing RLVR approaches primarily rely on outcome-based signals such as correctness and speedup, overlooking performance-critical structural properties of programs that are essential for generating optimized code. In this work, we propose CudaPerf, a reflective RL framework that incorporates both verifiable execution rewards and structural code-aware rewards derived from parallelization features (e.g., memory coalescing, occupancy, Arithmatic Intensity, and synchronization patterns). CudaPerf operates in two stages: (1) an offline pairwise ranking module that learns to distinguish strong and weak program candidates via contrastive comparisons, and (2) an online RL training phase that jointly optimizes for correctness, performance, and structural efficiency through a unified reward signal. To further enhance learning, CudaPerf utilizes iterative refinement using execution feedback enabling progressive improvement of generated candidates. We also introduce a dataset comprising 2.9k C to CUDA and 1k PyTorch to CUDA programs, each paired with diverse input configurations and multiple CUDA implementations encompassing diverse optimization strategies. CudaPerf is evaluated across multiple benchmarks comprising both C to CUDA and PyTorch to CUDA transformations. Empirical findings suggest that CudaPerf significantly outperforms strong baselines, including Qwen-3-32B (for C to CUDA) and CUDA Agent (for PyTorch to CUDA) by achieving up to 5X & 3.32X improvements in speedup, and 17% & 7% improvements in correctness, respectively.

</details>


### [193] [Three-Pronged Spectral Control for Federated Parameter Efficient Fine Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20914)
*Shiva Raj Pokhrel,Dipsan Bhattarai,Anwar Walid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRISHUL is a spectral-control framework that improves federated PEFT performance under non-IID data by using shared frozen multi-head low-rank bases, nuclear norm shrinkage on small core matrices, and concave water filling budget allocation across layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) like LoRA enables communication-efficient adaptation of large models on decentralized edge data, but it's fragile under non-IID client heterogeneity - different clients learn locally useful but spectrally misaligned update subspaces, causing high-variance aggregation and poor global transfer.

Method: TRISHUL uses: 1) shared frozen multi-head low-rank bases for algebraically exact aggregation of compact core updates, 2) nuclear norm proximal shrinkage to suppress client-specific high-rank spectral components before upload, and 3) non-uniform allocation of adaptation heads across layers using a concave water filling budget rule derived from pretrained layer capacity. The shrinkage is only performed on small core matrices, adding negligible computation and no extra per-round communication.

Result: Across vision (CIFAR-100, SVHN) and language (20 Newsgroups, MRQA, GLUE) benchmarks with LLaMA3.2-1B, TRISHUL improves convergence, stability, and final performance over federated LoRA baselines, with greater gains under stronger heterogeneity.

Conclusion: TRISHUL provides a robust spectral-control framework for federated PEFT that addresses the spectral misalignment problem in non-IID settings through efficient architectural innovations without significant computational or communication overhead, resulting in improved performance across diverse benchmarks.

Abstract: Federated parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) enables communication-efficient adaptation of large pretrained models on decentralized edge data, but it remains fragile under non-IID client heterogeneity. In low-rank adaptation (LoRA), different clients may learn locally useful but spectrally misaligned update subspaces, causing high-variance aggregation and poor global transfer. We propose TRISHUL, a spectral-control framework for robust federated PEFT. TRISHUL follows the FL no-raw-data-sharing setting but does not itself provide formal privacy guarantees. TRISHUL uses shared frozen multi-head low-rank bases to obtain algebraically exact aggregation of compact core updates, applies nuclear norm proximal shrinkage to suppress client-specific high-rank spectral components before upload, and allocates adaptation heads non-uniformly across layers using a concave water filling budget rule derived from pretrained layer capacity. Because shrinkage is performed only on small core matrices, TRISHUL adds negligible computation and no extra per-round communication over the underlying multi-head PEFT protocol. Across vision and language benchmarks, including CIFAR-100, SVHN, 20 Newsgroups, MRQA, and GLUE with LLaMA3.2-1B, TRISHUL improves convergence, stability, and final performance over federated LoRA baselines, with greater gains under stronger heterogeneity.

</details>


### [194] [Best-of-Evidence: Best-of-N Selection under Partial Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20950)
*Cenwei Zhang,Teng Fang,Yuxia Wang,Derek Li,Bryan Dai,Lei You*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BoE extends Best-of-N selection to handle partial verification scenarios where only parts of responses can be verified, using evidence-based selection with limited query budgets rather than requiring complete candidate evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Best-of-N (BoN) methods assume complete candidate evaluation is possible, but many vision-language tasks only allow partial verification (checking specific findings, spans, values, regions, or relations) without reliable whole-response verification. Additionally, the same claim may appear across candidates with different stances, creating opportunities for evidence to support some candidates while contradicting others.

Method: BoE keeps the BoN candidate pool fixed but introduces a signed candidate-factor graph to represent reusable claims across candidates. It allocates a limited budget to evidence collection actions that can influence the final selection, formalizing selection under partial verification constraints with a practical score-based controller. Zero-budget case recovers BoN decisions.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows evidence capacity limits improvements, and shared factor queries can achieve O(log K) vs Θ(K) query separation in a factor-code model. Experiments on four medical VQA settings demonstrate BoE can improve fixed-pool selection and rescue some BoN failures when evidence is reliable, contrastive, and decision-relevant, while also revealing quality and generation limits.

Conclusion: BoE provides a principled framework for inference-time selection when only partial verification is available, showing practical benefits in scenarios with reliable, contrastive evidence but also highlighting fundamental limitations that prevent universal gains across all conditions.

Abstract: BoN improves model outputs by sampling several candidates and selecting one with a proxy score, but it assumes that complete candidates can be evaluated reliably. Many vision-language tasks instead provide only partial verification: a finding, span, value, region, or relation may be checkable even when no dependable whole-response verifier exists. Moreover, the same claim may recur across candidates with opposing stances, allowing one observation to support part of the pool and contradict another. We introduce Best-of-Evidence (BoE), an inference-time selection framework that keeps the BoN candidate pool fixed, represents reusable claims with a signed candidate--factor graph, and allocates a limited budget to evidence actions that can change the final choice. BoE formalizes selection under partial verification and provides a practical score-based controller, with the zero-budget case recovering the underlying BoN decision. Theoretically, we show that residual evidence capacity limits any evidence-driven improvement and that shared factor queries can achieve an O(log K) versus Θ(K) query separation in a factor-code model. Common-ledger experiments on four medical VQA settings show that BoE can improve fixed-pool selection and rescue some BoN failures when evidence is reliable, contrastive, and decision-relevant, while also revealing the channel-quality and candidate-generation limits that prevent universal gains.

</details>


### [195] [The Weight of Silence: A Causal Case for Weights Over the Scratchpad in Latent Chess Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20952)
*Ishan S. Kshirsagar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL-trained chess-playing models with latent reasoning show increased robustness to thought disruption but not increased reliance on thought content, challenging assumptions that latent reasoning functions as an active inference-time scratchpad.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether the common assumption that latent reasoning functions as an internal scratchpad that models actively consult during inference survives reinforcement learning (RL), especially since existing causal analyses are confined to math/logic tasks and compare within single checkpoints rather than before/after RL stages.

Method: Train a chess-playing model through a staged latent-reasoning curriculum followed by RL, then conduct a six-condition causal intervention suite comparing the same model before and after RL. Interventions include substituting/adding noise to latent thought vectors, ablating them, and using exact-zero vectors.

Result: Legality improves from 48% to 61% after RL while checkmate confabulation is eliminated. Causal interventions show robustness: substituting/adding noise leaves performance unchanged, ablation causes mild degradation, and only exact-zero vectors cause collapse. Post-RL model shows much greater robustness to exact-zero corruption (9% vs 1% pre-RL).

Conclusion: RL adds robustness to disruption rather than increasing reliance on thought content, pushing back against the field's default assumption that latent thoughts function as actively consulted inference-time scratchpads. Instead, latent reasoning's principal effect appears to be shaping model parameters during training.

Abstract: Latent, or silent, reasoning lets language models carry out intermediate computation in continuous vector space instead of words, and is widely assumed to function as an internal scratchpad the model actively consults during inference. Whether that assumption survives reinforcement learning has not been tested directly: existing causal analyses of latent reasoning are confined to math and logic tasks, and compare a model's reliance on its thoughts within a single checkpoint, never before and after an RL stage. We train a chess-playing model through a staged latent-reasoning curriculum followed by reinforcement learning, and find legality climbs monotonically to 61% (from a 48% pre-RL baseline) while checkmate confabulation is eliminated entirely. To locate this gain, we run a six-condition causal intervention suite on the same model before and after RL: substituting or adding matched noise to the latent thought vectors leaves performance unchanged, ablating them causes only mild degradation, and only exact-zero vectors cause collapse. This robustness gap is itself the finding: under exact-zero corruption, legality collapses to 1% pre-RL versus 9% post-RL, a gap that survives correction for testing across the full battery; milder conditions trend similarly without independently reaching significance. RL appears to add robustness to disruption, not reliance on thought content. These results push back against the field's default assumption that latent thoughts function as an actively consulted inference-time scratchpad, and instead indicate latent reasoning's principal effect here is shaping the model's parameters during training. We also demonstrate a working RL gain in chess, a domain outside the math and logic settings where multiple groups report the same latent-reasoning-plus-RL recipe failing to improve accuracy over SFT.

</details>


### [196] [ADABORD: a novel AdaBoost approach for ordinal classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21003)
*Rafael Ayllón-Gavilán,Francisco José Martínez-Estudillo,David Guijo-Rubio,César Hervás-Martínez,Pedro A. Gutiérrez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADABORD is a new AdaBoost framework specifically designed for ordinal classification that incorporates ordinal information into both the base estimators and error functions, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art methods on datasets with 5+ classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ordinal classification approaches often fail to fully leverage ordinal information, treating problems as nominal classification and losing performance potential. There's a need for methods that properly incorporate the natural order of classes into the learning process.

Method: ADABORD extends the AdaBoost algorithm with two key modifications: 1) uses decision trees with an ordinal Gini splitting criterion as base estimators, 2) employs an absolute ranked probability score error function that accounts for both ordering and distance between classes when updating sample weights and classifier weights in the ensemble.

Result: The method was extensively compared against 7 state-of-the-art methods on the TOC-UCO repository (largest benchmark for OC). Statistical analysis shows ADABORD significantly outperforms competing methods, particularly on datasets with 5+ classes where ordinal structure is more pronounced.

Conclusion: ADABORD successfully incorporates ordinal information into both base learning and ensemble weighting, demonstrating superior performance over existing methods, especially when ordinal structure is more complex. The publicly available code ensures reproducibility and facilitates future OC research.

Abstract: Ordinal Classification (OC) deals with classification tasks where the classes follow a natural order. Despite the progress in OC, many existing approaches fail to fully leverage the ordinal information, treating the problem as nominal classification and thereby losing performance potential. In this work, ADABORD, an AdaBoost framework specifically designed for ordinal classification problems, is introduced. The ordinal nature of the classes is incorporated into two key components of the well-known AdaBoost algorithm: 1) the base estimator, where decision trees with the ordinal Gini splitting criterion are proposed; 2) the error function used to update sample weights at each stage and the weights of the classifier in the final ensemble model, given by the absolute ranked probability score, a measure that accounts for both the ordering and the distance between classes. ADABORD is extensively compared against seven state-of-the-art methods on the TOC-UCO repository, the largest benchmark collection for OC to date. The experimental results, supported by statistical analysis, show that ADABORD significantly outperforms competing methods, particularly on datasets with five or more classes, where the ordinal structure becomes more pronounced. Source code, along with all experimental protocols, is publicly available to ensure reproducibility and facilitate future research in OC.

</details>


### [197] [Weight-norm Criticality: A Mechanism for Loss Spikes Induced by the Normalization and Weight Decay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21005)
*Xiaolong Li,Zhangchen Zhou,Zhi-Qin John Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "weight-norm criticality" as a new explanation for training instability in deep neural networks, showing how the interaction between normalization and weight decay can drive scale-invariant weights to critically low norms, causing sharpness spikes and loss explosions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanations for training instability focus primarily on learning-rate criticality (Edge of Stability), but practical DNN training experiences instability that cannot be fully explained by this framework. The paper aims to identify and explain an additional overlooked mechanism involving weight decay and normalization layers.

Method: The authors propose a theoretical framework analyzing the interaction between normalization (which creates scale-invariant components) and weight decay (which persistently shrinks parameter norms). They identify a critical boundary where scale-invariant weight norms become dangerously small, leading to sharpness increases and optimization instability. They validate predictions empirically on networks with scale-invariant components.

Result: The paper demonstrates that weight decay can drive scale-invariant weights toward zero, causing rapid increases in loss landscape sharpness and resulting in abrupt loss spikes. This explains why weight penalties improve generalization but cannot be made arbitrarily strong - excessive decay pushes norms past a critical boundary and destabilizes training.

Conclusion: Weight-norm criticality provides a new mechanistic understanding of training instability and loss spikes in DNNs. This perspective explains the delicate balance in using weight decay for regularization while avoiding training collapse, offering testable predictions validated empirically in scale-invariant networks.

Abstract: Most explanations of training instability focus on \emph{learning-rate criticality}, typically characterized by the Edge of Stability, beyond which optimization becomes unstable. We argue that, in practical deep neural network training, there is an additional and often overlooked \emph{weight-norm criticality}. This criticality is induced by the interaction between normalization (which introduces scale-invariant components) and weight decay (which persistently shrinks parameter norms). As the weight decay coefficient increases, the norms of scale-invariant weights are progressively driven toward zero. Meanwhile, the sharpness of the loss landscape increases rapidly, destabilizing the optimization dynamics and resulting in abrupt loss spikes. This perspective provides a rationale for why weight penalties can improve generalization yet cannot be made arbitrarily strong: excessive decay drives scale-invariant weight norms past a critical boundary and destabilizes training. Our work provides a new mechanistic understanding of loss spikes through the lens of \emph{weight-norm criticality}. Moreover, \emph{weight-norm criticality} yields testable predictions that we validate empirically in networks with scale-invariant components, providing empirical support for the proposed mechanism.

</details>


### [198] [Regularized Optimization on Grassmann Manifold: Theory, Algorithm and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21039)
*Zhuan Liang,Zheng Zhai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RPMA is a robust spectral subspace estimation method that regularizes projection matrix approximation to improve community detection and clustering performance in noisy environments through manifold optimization techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional spectral methods for community detection and clustering are sensitive to noise, outliers, and model perturbations, which can substantially degrade their performance. The accurate estimation of spectral subspaces is critical but challenging in real-world noisy environments.

Method: Proposed Regularized Projection Matrix Approximation (RPMA) framework that extends classical spectral projection by adding a regularization term. Formulated as an optimization problem on the manifold of rank-K projection matrices (equivalent to Grassmann manifold), with derived optimality conditions and stability analysis. Developed efficient Riemannian gradient projection and Cayley-SMW gradient algorithms to solve the nonconvex optimization.

Result: RPMA substantially improves recovery accuracy of projection matrices compared to conventional methods. Experimental results on synthetic and real-world datasets show consistent outperformance for community detection and clustering under noisy conditions.

Conclusion: RPMA provides a robust framework for spectral subspace estimation that addresses the sensitivity limitations of classical spectral methods, offering improved performance, sparsity, and interpretability for graph learning tasks in practical noisy scenarios.

Abstract: Spectral methods are among the most widely used techniques for community detection, clustering, and graph learning. Their performance, however, critically depends on the accurate estimation of the underlying spectral subspace and can deteriorate substantially in the presence of noise, outliers, or model perturbations. To address this limitation, we propose a Regularized Projection Matrix Approximation (RPMA) framework for robust estimation of rank-$K$ projection matrices. RPMA extends classical spectral projection by incorporating a regularization term, producing projection estimates that are more robust, sparse, and interpretable. We formulate the proposed model as an optimization problem on the manifold of rank-$K$ projection matrices and exploit its geometric equivalence to the Grassmann manifold. Based on this manifold characterization, we derive the first- and second-order optimality conditions, establish the local stability of the regularized leading eigenspace, and characterize the stability of the critical-point landscape under sufficiently small regularization. To efficiently solve the resulting nonconvex optimization problem, we develop a Riemannian gradient projection algorithm with backtracking line search, together with a more efficient Cayley--Sherman--Morrison--Woodbury (Cayley--SMW) gradient method that avoids repeated eigendecompositions. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that RPMA substantially improves the recovery accuracy of projection matrices and consistently outperforms conventional spectral projection methods for community detection and clustering under noisy environments.

</details>


### [199] [Counterfactual Explainability Framework With CycleGAN And Counterfactual-Classifier Alignnment Score for Retinal Disease Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21068)
*Kritanu Chattopadhyay,Sayanjit Singha Roy,Soumya Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CounterFundus: A CycleGAN-based counterfactual explainability framework that generates healthy counterparts of pathological fundus images to localize disease-associated retinal changes, with alignment metrics showing spatial consistency with classifier evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DL models for retinal disease detection lack explainability, especially when model decisions aren't linked to clinically meaningful retinal regions, creating a bottleneck for clinical adoption.

Method: Combines EfficientNet-B5-based disease detection with CycleGAN-driven counterfactual generation to create healthy fundus counterparts from pathological images. Introduces Counterfactual-Classifier Alignment Score (CCAS) to quantify spatial agreement between counterfactual difference maps and classifier saliency using Spearman correlation, binary IoU, and pointing accuracy.

Result: Demonstrates that counterfactual explanations remain spatially consistent with classifier-relevant retinal evidence across all CCAS dimensions. Ablation studies confirm CCAS-filtered counterfactual augmentation improves downstream classification performance.

Conclusion: CounterFundus establishes a clinically-grounded, explainable AI framework for retinal disease detection by providing visually interpretable counterfactual explanations through disease-to-normal translation, addressing explainability barriers for clinical adoption.

Abstract: Automated detection of vision impairing retina-based ocular conditions from fundus images is important for early screening, timely referral and reducing dependency on specialist-only assessment, for which neural network-based deep learning (DL) models have been widely utilized. However, explainability of the DL frameworks remains a major bottleneck for clinical adoption, particularly when model decisions are not linked to retinal regions that are clinically meaningful. To address this issue, this study presents CounterFundus, a novel CycleGAN-driven counterfactual explainability framework, integrating EfficientNet-B5-based retinal disease detection with visually interpretable disease-to-normal fundus image translation. For each pathological image, the counterfactual yielded by the CycleGAN generator represents an estimated healthy counterpart and the resultant difference map is utilized to localize disease-associated retinal changes. Unlike conventional post-hoc saliency methods, CounterFundus provides counterfactual explanations through visually plausible disease-to-normal retinal translation. Thereafter, to quantify the spatial agreement between counterfactual difference maps and classifier saliency, the Counterfactual-Classifier Alignment Score (CCAS) is introduced, embedding Spearman correlation, binary IoU and pointing accuracy into a single assessment protocol. To this end, EigenCAM-aligned evaluation demonstrates that the generated counterfactual explanations remain spatially consistent with classifier-relevant retinal evidence across all CCAS dimensions. Along with that, ablation studies further confirm that CCAS-filtered counterfactual augmentation improves the downstream classification performance in fundus images, establishing CounterFundus as a clinically-grounded, explainable artificially intelligence (XAI) framework for retinal disease detection.

</details>


### [200] [From Evaluation to Optimisation: Hierarchy-Aware Training Signals for CWE Prediction in Python](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21069)
*Muntasir Adnan,Manile Srun,Carlos C. N. Kuhn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALPHA benchmark penalty can serve as training signal; supervised fine-tuning fails under distribution shift while GRPO reduces penalty by ~27%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original ALPHA benchmark introduced a taxonomy-aware penalty for evaluating CWE-level vulnerability prediction in Python and suggested this penalty could theoretically serve as a training signal, but this needed validation.

Method: Compared three delivery mechanisms: supervised fine-tuning, dual-head classification loss, and reinforcement learning (GRPO) with dense reward derived from normalized ALPHA penalty.

Result: Supervised approaches regress below zero-shot baseline under distribution shift, while GRPO succeeds. Best policy reduces ALPHA penalty of Qwen2.5-Coder-7B on SVEN dataset by 27.9% under greedy decoding and 25.5% under sampled decoding, reaching statistical parity with 4.5x larger zero-shot teacher.

Conclusion: The value of a hierarchical penalty as a training signal depends largely on the directness of its delivery, with GRPO being effective while supervised methods fail under distribution shift.

Abstract: The original ALPHA benchmark introduced a taxonomy-aware penalty for evaluating CWE-level vulnerability prediction in Python and proposed that the penalty could theoretically also serve as a training signal. This paper provides that validation. We compare three delivery mechanisms: supervised fine-tuning, a dual-head classification loss, and reinforcement learning with a dense reward derived from the normalised penalty. We find that supervised approaches consistently regress below the zero-shot baseline under distribution shift, while GRPO succeeds. Our best policy reduces the cumulative ALPHA penalty of Qwen2.5-Coder-7B on Security Hardening and Adversarial Testing (SVEN) dataset by 27.9% under greedy decoding, and by 25.5% under sampled decoding(p = 0.005, Welch's t-test), reaching statistical parity with its 4.5x larger zero-shot teacher. We conclude that the value of a hierarchical penalty as a training signal depends largely on the directness of its delivery.

</details>


### [201] [Spectral Transformation for Layer-wise Global Rank Discovery in Federated LoRA for Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21074)
*Hariharan Ramesh,Jyotikrishna Dass*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SpecTraL is a federated learning method that uses spectral transformation and Householder transformations to aggregate LoRA adapters for Vision Transformers without cross-term errors, dense reconstruction, or auxiliary models, while automatically discovering optimal global ranks using random matrix theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LoRA aggregation strategies for federated fine-tuning of Vision Transformers have fundamental limitations: averaging LoRA factors introduces cross-term aggregation errors, concatenation approaches increase download costs and cause reinitialization lag, and other methods increase server overhead through dense reconstruction or auxiliary models.

Method: SpecTraL stacks local LoRA modules from clients and performs orthonormal Householder Transformation directly in the low-rank latent space, eliminating dense reconstruction. It uses the Spiked Covariance Model from Random Matrix Theory to separate global consensus signals from non-IID noise, automatically discovering optimal layer-wise global ranks without manual tuning. A padding-aware initialization framework allows clients to incorporate residual LoRA dimensions without re-merging into pretrained weights.

Result: Experiments on federated fine-tuning of ViT-B/16 and ViT-L/16 over DomainNet and NICO++ show improved accuracy-communication trade-offs, reduced server computation, and elimination of hyperparameter search for rank selection compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: SpecTraL provides a unified solution to LoRA aggregation challenges in federated learning, offering mathematical consistency, communication efficiency, reduced server overhead, and automated rank discovery while maintaining stable convergence.
