Abstract: Fine-tuning Vision Transformers (ViTs) with low-rank adapters (LoRA) promises better communication efficiency under federated setup, yet existing aggregation strategies face fundamental limitations. Independently averaging these LoRA factors is mathematically inconsistent, introducing cross-term aggregation error. In contrast, approaches that preserve heterogeneous client ranks by concatenating local adapters on the server substantially increase download cost and often require merging global LoRA updates into pretrained weights on the clients, causing reinitialization lag and unstable convergence. Other approaches further increase server-side overhead by reconstructing dense weight updates or training auxiliary models to refine aggregation error. In this work, we propose SpecTraL, spectral transformation for layer-wise global rank discovery, that resolves these challenges within a unified design. SpecTraL stacks local LoRA modules from clients and performs orthonormal Householder Transformation of the stacked adapters directly in the low-rank latent space, eliminating dense reconstruction of the global update and any auxiliary refinement on the server. By leveraging the Spiked Covariance Model from Random Matrix Theory, SpecTraL analytically separates the global consensus signal from non-IID noise, discovering optimal layer-wise global ranks without manual hyperparameter tuning. To match local ranks in subsequent rounds, we introduce a padding-aware initialization framework that lets clients incorporate residual LoRA dimensions without re-merging them into the pre-trained base model. Experiments on federated fine-tuning of ViT-B/16 and ViT-L/16 over DomainNet and NICO++ demonstrate improved accuracy-communication trade-offs, reduced server computation, and elimination of hyperparameter search for rank selection. Our code is available at https://github.com/DASS-Lab-Group/SpecTraL

</details>


### [202] [Nipping the Butterfly Effect in the Bud: Self-Output Fine-Tuning for Autoregressive Weather Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21080)
*Yun-Ye Cai,Hsuan-Tien Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Autoregressive DLWP suffers from error growth due to distribution shift feedback loop; SOFT uses self-output fine-tuning to calibrate biased inputs, achieving SOTA long-horizon weather forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive Deep Learning Weather Prediction (DLWP) has become the primary paradigm for long-horizon weather forecasting, but it suffers from rapid error growth over extended forecasting horizons, which deteriorates forecasting accuracy for atmospheric science applications.

Method: 1) Theoretical and empirical analysis of error growth mechanism in autoregressive DLWP, identifying feedback loop between output errors and input distribution shifts. 2) Proposed Self-Output Fine-Tuning (SOFT) - a plug-and-play strategy that uses the model's own one-step predictions to calibrate biased input distributions encountered at the first autoregressive step.

Result: SOFT achieves state-of-the-art performance on long-horizon forecasting tasks, substantially reducing both prediction errors and distributional discrepancy. The method successfully mitigates the butterfly effect-like error amplification in autoregressive pipelines.

Conclusion: SOFT represents a critical pipeline advance for atmospheric science by addressing fundamental limitations of autoregressive DLWP. The approach highlights the importance of reexamining deep learning weather prediction pipelines and demonstrates that simple distribution calibration at early inference stages can significantly improve long-horizon forecasting accuracy.

Abstract: Long-horizon weather forecasting is a fundamental challenge in atmospheric science, for which autoregressive Deep Learning Weather Prediction (DLWP) has emerged as the primary paradigm. Although the autoregressive pipeline is highly scalable and flexible, its prediction errors grow rapidly over long forecasting horizons. In this work, we study this error growth phenomenon from both theoretical and empirical perspectives. Our analysis reveals that the growth is driven by a feedback loop between output errors and input distribution shifts. Specifically, the autoregressive process amplifies small initial output errors, which progressively corrupt subsequent input distributions, echoing the butterfly effect in atmospheric science and ultimately deteriorating forecasting accuracy over longer horizons. Furthermore, we show that this distributional shift originates at the earliest stage of inference, with out-of-distribution signatures detectable as early as the first autoregressive step. To mitigate this issue, we propose \textbf{Self-Output Fine-Tuning (SOFT)}, a plug-and-play strategy that leverages the model's own one-step predictions to calibrate the biased input distribution encountered at the first step. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, despite its simplicity, SOFT achieves state-of-the-art performance on long-horizon forecasting tasks and substantially reduces both prediction errors and distributional discrepancy. The success of SOFT highlights the importance of reexamining the fundamental pipeline of deep learning weather prediction, representing a critical pipeline advance for atmospheric science.

</details>


### [203] [CASC: Causal Adversarial Subspace Clustering for Multivariate Spatiotemporal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21088)
*Francis Ndikum Nji,Vandana Janeja,Jianwu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CASC is a novel causal adversarial subspace clustering framework that discovers evolving latent regimes in high-dimensional spatiotemporal data by integrating U-Net architecture with causal relationships and temporal dynamics, moving beyond correlation-driven approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep subspace clustering methods rely on geometric self-expressiveness and assume static subspace structures, failing to capture causal dependencies, local spatial interactions, and long-range temporal dynamics inherent in complex spatiotemporal systems like sea ice monitoring, disease spread analysis, and neuro-degeneration tracking.

Method: Proposes Causal Adversarial Subspace Clustering (CASC) framework that integrates: 1) U-Net-inspired deep adversarial clustering architecture with stacked FAConvLSTM layers to preserve spatial and temporal structure; 2) Graph attention transformer-based self-expressive network to model local spatial relationships, global dependencies, and long-range temporal interactions; 3) Two new learning objectives: Causal Subspace Preservation Loss aligning self-expression coefficients with latent causal relationships, and Dynamic Temporal Subspace Evolution Loss capturing evolving subspace structures and temporal regime transitions.

Result: The framework transforms deep subspace clustering from a correlation-driven paradigm into a causal-temporal regime discovery framework that can discover evolving latent regimes in high-dimensional spatiotemporal data, capturing underlying causal processes rather than simple feature similarity.

Conclusion: CASC addresses key limitations of existing subspace clustering methods by introducing causal relationships and temporal dynamics, enabling more accurate discovery of evolving latent regimes in complex spatiotemporal systems for applications requiring understanding of underlying causal processes over time.

Abstract: Deep subspace clustering plays a critical role in applications involving multivariate spatiotemporal data, such as sea ice monitoring, disease spread analysis, and tracking neuro-degeneration over time. Despite recent advances, existing methods primarily rely on geometric self-expressiveness, assume static subspace structures, and often fail to capture causal dependencies, local spatial interactions, and long-range temporal dynamics inherent in complex spatiotemporal systems. To address these limitations, we propose a novel Causal Adversarial Subspace Clustering (CASC) framework for discovering evolving latent regimes in high-dimensional spatiotemporal data. CASC integrates a U-Net-inspired deep adversarial clustering architecture with stacked FAConvLSTM layers to preserve spatial and temporal structure while learning robust latent representations. A graph attention transformer-based self-expressive network is introduced to jointly model local spatial relationships, global dependencies, and long-range temporal interactions. Furthermore, we propose two new learning objectives: (1) a Causal Subspace Preservation Loss that aligns self-expression coefficients with latent causal relationships, encouraging clusters to reflect underlying causal processes rather than simple feature similarity, and (2) a Dynamic Temporal Subspace Evolution Loss that captures evolving subspace structures and temporal regime transitions in nonstationary environments. Together, these components transform deep subspace clustering from a correlation-driven paradigm into a causal-temporal regime discovery framework.

</details>


### [204] [Training Large Language Models for Self-Explanation Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21090)
*Yeoktatt Cheah,María Pérez-Ortiz,Noah Y. Siegel,Oana-Maria Camburu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL fine-tuning method improves LLM self-explanation faithfulness by optimizing model parameters to identify and disclose internal decision-making factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches only evaluate faithfulness or use inference-time prompting to improve self-explanations, but lack methods to directly optimize model parameters for generating faithful self-explanations that accurately reflect internal decision processes.

Method: Convert existing faithfulness metrics into RL training objectives, using per-sample rewards derived from Phi-CCT correlation metric. Apply RL fine-tuning to models like Llama3.1-8B and Qwen3-8B with two intervention types: random-word insertions and user-bias insertions.

Result: RL fine-tuning substantially improves faithfulness scores: in-distribution Phi-CCT rises from near-zero to 0.664, out-of-distribution scores reach 0.691 on tasks like StrategyQA. Cross-intervention generalization is weaker and model-dependent - Llama3.1-8B shows some transfer from random-word to user-bias interventions, but reverse direction and Qwen3.8-B don't replicate this.

Conclusion: Models can be trained to implicitly identify influential factors in their decision-making and disclose them, providing a scalable approach to reducing unfaithful reasoning in LLMs without falling into reward gaming behaviors.

Abstract: We propose a Reinforcement Learning (RL) method to directly optimize the faithfulness of self-explanations - the extent to which a model's generated reasoning accurately reflects its internal decision-making process. While existing work focuses on evaluating faithfulness or using inference-time prompting frameworks to improve an LLM's self-explanation's tractability, these approaches do not provide a mechanism to directly optimize a model's parameters to generate faithful self-explanations. We bridge this gap by modifying existing faithfulness metrics into an RL training objective. We investigate (1) if models can be trained to accurately detect factors that affect their decisions, and (2) whether RL can directly optimize for the disclosure of these factors thereby improving LLM self-explanations' faithfulness. We experiment with two intervention types: random-word insertions and user-bias insertions, using a per-sample reward derived from the Phi-CCT correlation metric. RL fine-tuned Llama3.1-8B and Qwen3-8B show substantial improvements on the Phi-CCT faithfulness metric, with in-distribution scores rising from near-zero to as high as 0.664, and out-of-distribution scores reaching up to 0.691 on held-out tasks such as StrategyQA. Cross-intervention generalization is weaker but more interesting: a priori we would not expect a model trained only on random word insertions to generalize to user-bias phrases, yet Llama3.1-8B shows non-zero transfer in this direction. The reverse direction and Qwen3-8B do not replicate this, indicating model-dependent and setup-dependent effects we cannot yet explain. Lastly we analyze model behavior to rule out reward gaming behaviors that often plague RL training. Ultimately, we show that models can be trained to implicitly identify influential factors and disclose them, offering a scalable path toward reducing unfaithful reasoning in LLMs.

</details>


### [205] [A Polynomial Architecture-Attribution Co-Design Framework for Exact Aumann-Shapley Attribution in GNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21094)
*Bizu Feng,Zhimu Yang,Shuming Wang,Shaode Yu,Yuan Cheng,Xiaojun Qian,Zixin Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: APEX is a model-attribution co-design framework for GNNs that makes Aumann-Shapley attribution integrals exactly computable using polynomial GNN architectures, achieving high attribution fidelity with deterministic path integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current path-integral attribution methods for GNNs (like Integrated Gradients) require finite-sample approximations that force a trade-off between numerical accuracy and computational cost, limiting practical use in deep GNNs.

Method: The authors propose APEX framework with PolyGIN - a GIN-style polynomial graph network that preserves bounded multivariate polynomial form for model scores. This enables exact computation of Aumann-Shapley path integrals using Gauss-Legendre quadrature with deterministic evaluation points (2^(L-1) points for L polynomial blocks).

Result: Experiments show PolyGIN maintains competitive predictive performance on graph benchmarks while APEX achieves higher attribution fidelity than baselines and substantially reduces the number of evaluations needed for path integration (exact computation up to floating-point precision).

Conclusion: The model-attribution co-design approach successfully bridges theoretical attribution requirements with practical GNN architectures, enabling exact attribution computation while preserving both feature-level and node-level explanation completeness and computational efficiency.

Abstract: We study feature-level and node-level explanations for graph neural networks (GNNs) through the lens of Aumann-Shapley attribution. Path-integral methods such as Integrated Gradients provide an axiomatic formulation of attribution, but their practical use in deep GNNs typically relies on finite-sample numerical approximations to the path integral, requiring a trade-off between quadrature error and computational cost. This paper proposes APEX, a model-attribution co-design framework that makes the attribution integral exactly computable under a polynomial GNN architecture. The key component is PolyGIN, a GIN-style graph network whose message-passing, normalization, and transformation operations preserve a bounded multivariate polynomial form for scalar model scores, such as pre-softmax logits. We show that, for a PolyGIN with $L$ polynomial transformation blocks, the derivative along the attribution path has degree at most $2^L-1$. Therefore, Gauss--Legendre quadrature can evaluate the Aumann--Shapley path integral exactly, up to floating-point precision, with $2^{L-1}$ deterministic evaluation points. The resulting attributions can be computed at the feature level and then aggregated into node-level scores while preserving completeness. Experiments on synthetic and real-world graph benchmarks show that PolyGIN maintains competitive predictive performance, while the complete APEX framework achieves higher attribution fidelity than the compared baselines and substantially reduces the number of evaluations required for path integration.

</details>


### [206] [Smooth Neural Point Processes via B-Splines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21098)
*Michele Bellomo,Riccardo Ramaschi,Alberto Dolara,Tomaso Aste*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neural TPP model using B-spline basis functions to parameterize the conditional intensity function, enabling exact NLL evaluation with flexible architecture and parallel training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural TPP models have limitations: they typically model the compensator rather than the CIF directly, impose architectural constraints, require sequential evaluation during training (limiting parallelization), and are computationally expensive.

Method: Proposes a neural TPP that directly parameterizes the conditional intensity function as a non-negative combination of B-spline basis functions. The coefficients of these basis functions are predicted by a neural network, enabling exact evaluation of the negative log-likelihood without numerical integration.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets show the proposed method achieves improved computational efficiency and predictive accuracy compared to reference neural TPP baselines.

Conclusion: The B-spline based approach successfully addresses key limitations of existing neural TPPs by enabling direct CIF modeling, maintaining architectural flexibility, supporting efficient parallel training, and providing natural smoothness regularization while maintaining exact likelihood evaluation.

Abstract: Temporal point processes (TPPs) provide a general and flexible framework for modeling sequences of events in continuous time. Neural networks have been successfully employed to model TPPs in a highly expressive and data-driven way. Neural TPPs are typically trained via Maximum Likelihood Estimation (MLE) by minimizing the negative log-likelihood (NLL), which depends on both the conditional intensity function (CIF) and its integral over time, the compensator. Recent neural TPP approaches enable exact evaluation of the NLL without numerical integration. However, these methods typically model the compensator rather than the CIF directly, impose constraints on the neural network architecture, and are computationally expensive during training, as event contributions to the NLL are evaluated sequentially rather than in parallel. In this work, we propose a novel neural TPP model that directly parametrizes the CIF as a non-negative combination of B-spline basis functions, whose coefficients are predicted by a neural network. This formulation enables exact evaluation of the NLL, preserves full flexibility in the neural architecture, allows efficient parallelization during training, and naturally supports CIF smoothness regularization through the integrated squared second derivative. Experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets show improved computational efficiency and predictive accuracy compared to the reference neural TPP baseline.

</details>


### [207] [TOUR: A Trajectory-Level Unlearning Benchmark for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21111)
*Chaofan Pan,Lingfei Ren,Xiangyu Jiang,Yanhua Li,Xuemei Cao,Xiangkun Wang,Hao Yu,Wei Wei,Xin Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TOUR introduces a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating trajectory-level deletion/unlearning in offline RL, showing that single-score auditing is insufficient and conclusions depend on multiple factors including attack methods and utility retention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline RL agents are trained on fixed trajectories, making trajectory-level deletion important for data removal. However, evaluating such deletion is difficult because lower membership scores can reflect multiple factors: actual trajectory removal, residual memorization still visible to attacks, or policy collapse destroying useful behavior.

Method: TOUR (Trajectory-level memOrization and Unlearning in offline RL) combines trajectory-level partitioning, matched non-member controls, retraining references, retained-performance anchors, and multi-attack privacy auditing. The benchmark evaluates across D4RL locomotion tasks and AntMaze environments, comparing various deletion baselines.

Result: Common deletion baselines show environment-dependent privacy-utility tradeoffs. Retraining and fine-tuning often provide stronger utility retention than uniform GA+Refit. TrajDeleter is a useful comparator but not uniformly stronger under comprehensive auditing. Multi-attack analysis reveals that single likelihood-based membership scores can overstate deletion quality.

Conclusion: Conclusions about offline RL unlearning are not stable under single-score auditing. They depend on matched non-member construction, retraining-relative calibration, attack family selection, retained utility measures, and explicit scope for diagnostic architecture or component-level evidence. Comprehensive benchmarks like TOUR are needed for reliable evaluation.

Abstract: Offline Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents are trained on fixed behavioral trajectories, which makes trajectory-level deletion important when selected data must be removed after training. Evaluating such deletion is difficult because a lower membership score can reflect trajectory removal, residual memorization visible to another attack, or policy collapse that destroys useful behavior. We introduce Trajectory-level memOrization and Unlearning in offline RL (TOUR), a benchmark that combines trajectory-level partitioning, matched non-member controls, retraining references, retained-performance anchors, and multi-attack privacy auditing. Across D4RL locomotion experiments and an exploratory AntMaze extension, TOUR shows that common deletion baselines have environment-dependent privacy-utility behavior. Retraining and fine-tuning often provide stronger retained-utility references than uniform GA+Refit, while TrajDeleter remains a useful comparator but is not uniformly stronger under the same audit. Reference-model, threshold, deviation, equivalence, action-error, representation-based, and query-limited attacks further show that a single likelihood-based membership score can overstate deletion quality. In the evaluated settings, conclusions about offline RL unlearning are therefore not stable under single-score auditing. They depend on matched non-member construction, retraining-relative calibration, attack family, retained utility, and explicit scope for diagnostic architecture or component-level evidence.

</details>


### [208] [GlucoTune: A Unified Framework for Blood Glucose Preprocessing, Forecasting, and Benchmarking in Diabetes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21117)
*Davide Marelli,Giorgia Rigamonti,Mirko Paolo Barbato,Paolo Napoletano*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GlucoTune is a standardized framework for reproducible preprocessing and evaluation of blood glucose time-series data in diabetes management research, addressing issues of data privacy, inconsistent preprocessing, and lack of benchmarking standards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies several key problems in diabetes management research: lack of standardized preprocessing workflows for blood glucose time-series data, absence of consistent evaluation protocols that hinder reproducibility and fair comparison, and data-sharing restrictions due to privacy and licensing constraints that prevent redistribution of preprocessed medical datasets.

Method: The authors present GlucoTune, a comprehensive framework that: 1) Standardizes the entire experimental workflow from preprocessing to model evaluation, 2) Uses configurable YAML pipelines for reproducible preprocessing without distributing sensitive data, 3) Provides a unified interface for implementing, training, and evaluating blood glucose prediction models, 4) Integrates public datasets through standardized wrappers, 5) Includes a curated collection of state-of-the-art models, and 6) Features a benchmarking leaderboard for systematic comparison.

Result: The framework enables reproducible experiments directly from original datasets while maintaining data privacy. It provides extensible infrastructure for additional datasets, preprocessing strategies, and forecasting models. The effectiveness was demonstrated through comprehensive experiments and usability was assessed in a user study.

Conclusion: GlucoTune addresses critical reproducibility and standardization challenges in diabetes management research by providing a framework that enables fair comparison of methods, maintains data privacy, and promotes transparent evaluation through standardized preprocessing and benchmarking capabilities.

Abstract: Preprocessing blood glucose time-series data is a critical yet often overlooked step in developing data-driven methods for diabetes management, particularly for type 1 diabetes. The lack of standardized preprocessing workflows and evaluation protocols hinders reproducibility and complicates fair comparison across studies. These challenges are further exacerbated by data-sharing restrictions, as privacy and licensing constraints often prevent the redistribution of preprocessed medical datasets. To address these limitations, we present GlucoTune, a comprehensive and extensible framework for reproducible experimentation with blood glucose time-series data. The framework standardizes the entire experimental workflow, from preprocessing to model evaluation, enabling reproducible experiments directly from the original datasets. Reproducible preprocessing is achieved through configurable pipelines defined in portable YAML configuration files, ensuring consistent data handling without distributing sensitive preprocessed data. Beyond preprocessing, GlucoTune provides a unified interface for implementing, training, and evaluating blood glucose prediction models. The framework integrates public datasets through standardized wrappers and provides a curated collection of state-of-the-art blood glucose prediction and general time-series forecasting methods, while remaining readily extensible to additional datasets, preprocessing strategies, and forecasting models. To promote transparent and consistent evaluation, GlucoTune includes a benchmarking leaderboard that reports results across datasets, preprocessing configurations, and forecasting methods, enabling systematic comparison of experimental settings. We demonstrate the effectiveness of GlucoTune through comprehensive experiments and assess its usability in a user study.

</details>


### [209] [Relative Value Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21120)
*Marc Höftmann,Jan Robine,Stefan Harmeling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Relative Value Learning (RV), a framework that learns value differences directly rather than absolute state values, and shows it can be effectively integrated with PPO for competitive Atari performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard reinforcement learning critics estimate absolute state values V(s), but only value differences matter for control. Learning these differences directly could be more efficient and theoretically grounded.

Method: Proposes Relative Value Learning using an antisymmetric function Δ(s_i, s_j) = V(s_i) - V(s_j), introduces pairwise Bellman operator, derives 1-step/n-step/λ-return targets, reconstructs generalized advantage estimation from pairwise differences (R-GAE), and integrates with PPO.

Result: Achieves competitive performance on Atari benchmark (49 ALE games) compared to standard PPO, demonstrating relative value estimation is an effective alternative to absolute critics.

Conclusion: Relative value learning provides a theoretically grounded framework for learning value differences directly, offering an effective alternative to traditional absolute value critics with practical performance benefits.

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, critics typically estimate absolute state values $V(s)$, estimating how good a particular situation is in isolation. However, it turns out that only differences in value are relevant for control. Motivated by this, we propose Relative Value Learning (RV), a framework that learns value differences directly via an antisymmetric function $Δ(s_i, s_j) = V(s_i) - V(s_j)$. We introduce a pairwise Bellman operator and prove it is a $γ$-contraction with a unique fixed point equal to the true value differences, derive well-posed $1$-step, $n$-step and $λ$-return targets and reconstruct generalized advantage estimation from pairwise differences to obtain an unbiased policy-gradient estimator (R-GAE). Beyond theoretical results, we integrate RV with PPO and achieve competitive performance on the Atari benchmark (49 ALE games) compared to standard PPO, indicating that relative value estimation is an effective alternative to absolute critics.

</details>


### [210] [Demographically-Informed Heat-Mortality Risk Curves via Risk Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21131)
*Alex O. Davies,Eunice Lo,Rui Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RGNNs use census data and graph neural networks to improve heat-mortality prediction accuracy while maintaining interpretable risk curves, outperforming traditional DLNMs especially during extreme heat events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Distributed Lag Non-linear Models (DLNMs) for estimating heat-related mortality risk ignore important demographic and geographic context, despite these factors being well-established as relevant to heat vulnerability. The models are effective but lack contextual adaptation.

Method: Propose Risk Graph Neural Networks (RGNNs) - a hierarchical GNN encoder that uses granular census features to optimize DLNM coefficient vectors. This approach preserves interpretable risk curve outputs while improving predictive calibration by incorporating demographic and geographic context.

Result: RGNN variants maintain both lower point-errors and near-nominal uncertainty coverage during the 2022 heatwave where baseline DLNM models collapse. Evaluated across 10 regions of England and Wales on two unprecedented heat years, showing substantial improvement in predictive calibration.

Conclusion: RGNNs represent a significant advancement in heat-mortality risk estimation by successfully incorporating demographic and geographic context through GNN architecture while maintaining interpretability, offering more reliable predictions especially during extreme heat events where traditional models fail.

Abstract: Estimating heat-related mortality risk is a core task in environmental epidemiology, typically addressed with Distributed Lag Non-linear Models (DLNMs); interpretable exposure-response surfaces fitted to temperature-mortality time series. DLNMs are effective but ignore demographic and geographic context, despite well-established relevance to heat vulnerability. We propose Risk Graph Neural Networks (RGNNs), a hierarchical GNN encoder that uses granular census features to optimise DLNM coefficient vectors, preserving interpretable risk curve outputs while substantially improving predictive calibration. Evaluated across 10 regions of England and Wales on two unprecedented heat years, RGNN variants maintain both lower point-errors and near-nominal uncertainty coverage during the 2022 heatwave where baselines collapse.

</details>


### [211] [Agree on the Model, Verify the Inference: GKR Protocols for HND-Based Transformer Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21162)
*Xiaolong Liang,Juanjuan Li,Rui Qin,Yisheng Lv*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GKR-HND is a registered-model protocol that verifies polynomial backbone computations in Homomorphic-Nonhomomorphic Decomposition Transformers during outsourced inference, preventing model substitution and incomplete execution without requiring full computational replay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses security risks in outsourced Transformer inference where clients are vulnerable to model substitution attacks (providers using different models) and incomplete execution attacks (providers skipping computations), while direct replay verification would eliminate the computational benefits of delegation.

Method: Proposes GKR-HND protocol that uses registered model weights to verify the polynomial backbone computations of Homomorphic-Nonhomomorphic Decomposition Transformers. The retained verifier checks GKR transcripts and registered-weight openings, while delegating expensive public evaluations to an assigned computation worker under an honest verifier assumption with non-collusion between prover and worker.

Result: Experiments with pretrained HND models demonstrate successful validation of the proof path and delegated public computation without requiring dense-matrix replay. The protocol ensures the verifier accepts only when the worker's signed, request-bound response matches the proof claims.

Conclusion: GKR-HND provides a practical verification solution for outsourced Transformer inference that maintains security against substitution and incomplete execution attacks while preserving the computational benefits of delegation through selective verification of polynomial backbone computations.

Abstract: Outsourced Transformer inference exposes clients to model substitution and incomplete execution, while direct replay removes the computational benefit of delegation. We present GKR-HND, a registered-model protocol for verifying the polynomial backbone of Homomorphic--Nonhomomorphic Decomposition Transformers. The retained verifier checks the GKR transcript and registered-weight openings, but delegates expensive public evaluations to an assigned computation worker. Assuming an honest retained verifier and prover--worker non-collusion, the verifier accepts only when the worker's signed, request-bound response agrees with the proof claims. Experiments with pretrained HND models validate the proof path and the delegated public computation without dense-matrix replay.

</details>


### [212] [Automated Synthesis and Adversarial Validation of Executable Causal Research Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21173)
*Irena Girshovitz,Dan Zeltzer,Ran Gilad-Bachrach*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ARA framework makes failures visible in automated research by encoding causal principles, validating assumptions, and surfacing when causal claims are unwarranted rather than silently returning potentially invalid results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated research systems are prone to silent failures where analysis code executes successfully but relies on invalid causal assumptions, creating misleading results without proper validation.

Method: ARA integrates protocol construction, synthetic data generation via Structural Causal Models with known ground-truth, and adversarial validation under controlled assumption violations. Translates natural language questions to structured causal protocols and executable code.

Result: Protocol construction and adversarial validation didn't consistently improve numerical accuracy compared to standard LLM generation, but changed failure modes: instead of silent failures, ARA surfaces protocol concerns, diagnostic failures, incomplete inference, or downgrades non-causal interpretations.

Conclusion: Validity-first automated science systems should be evaluated not only by answer accuracy but also by their ability to indicate when causal claims are unwarranted, making failures visible rather than silent.

Abstract: While automated research systems promise to accelerate empirical analysis, they are prone to silent failures: instances in which analysis code executes successfully yet relies on invalid causal assumptions. We present the Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based Epidemiology Research Assistant (ARA), a framework that makes these failures visible by explicitly encoding causal design principles, study-specific assumptions, and methodological constraints. ARA integrates protocol construction, synthetic data generation, and adversarial validation into a unified pipeline. The framework translates natural language research questions into structured causal protocols and executable analysis code by first constructing a protocol and then generating synthetic datasets using Structural Causal Models (SCMs) with known ground-truth effects. This synthetic-data step can also support pipeline development when access to confidential data, such as medical data, is restricted. The generated analysis is then evaluated under controlled violations of identification assumptions. We evaluate ARA on the Automated Causal Reasoning Benchmark, assessing recovery of identification strategies, causal quantities, treatment and outcome variables, and consistency between generated code and approved protocol. Protocol construction and adversarial validation did not consistently improve numerical agreement with benchmark estimates compared with standard LLM-based generation. However, they changed the failure mode: instead of silently returning causal estimates, ARA often surfaced protocol concerns, diagnostic failures, incomplete inference, or downgraded non-causal interpretations. These findings suggest that validity-first automated science systems should be evaluated not only by answer accuracy, but also by whether they indicate when causal claims are unwarranted.

</details>


### [213] [Filter Learning for Subgraphs: Algebras and Performance Risk Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21263)
*Purui Zhang,Feng Ji,Yanan Zhao,Bihan Wen,Wee Peng Tay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a subgraph filter learning (SFL) framework where subgraph-supported operators approximate full graph filters under partial graph topology observations, with performance guarantees and empirical superiority over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph signal processing tasks typically assume access to complete graph topology, which is often unavailable in practice. There's a need to handle scenarios where only partial graph observations (subgraphs) are available while still approximating full graph filter operations.

Method: Proposes systematic SFL framework as statistical learning problem. Develops subgraph filter algebra based on distance-aware Laplacian constructions to create structured, controllable filter class for effective approximation. Uses statistical learning formulation where optimal subgraph operators are data-dependent.

Result: Establishes performance risk bounds under least squares loss, quantifying how well learned operators approximate restricted ambient mapping. Experimental results on real-world datasets show proposed algebraic models consistently outperform polynomial filters, distribution-agnostic operators, and direct numerical filter learning baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed SFL framework provides effective approach for graph signal processing under partial topology observations, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation demonstrating superiority over existing methods for approximating full graph filters from subgraph data.

Abstract: Graph signal processing tasks that leverage spectral information typically assume access to the complete graph topology, which is often unavailable in practice. We propose a systematic framework for subgraph filter learning (SFL), where subgraph-supported operators approximate ambient graph filters under partial observations. We formulate SFL as a statistical learning problem in which optimal subgraph operators are inherently data-dependent. To address the difficulty of directly estimating such operators, we develop a subgraph filter algebra based on distance-aware Laplacian constructions, defining a structured and controllable class of filters for effective approximation. We further establish performance risk bounds under the least squares loss, quantifying how well the learned operator approximates the restricted ambient mapping. Experiments real-world datasets show that, for SFL tasks, the proposed algebraic models consistently outperform polynomial filters, distribution-agnostic operators, and direct numerical filter learning baselines that attempt to recover the underlying structure from data.

</details>


### [214] [The Dark Room in the Reward Channel: Dense Prediction Rewards Collapse GRPO-Trained LLM Agents -- and What Actually Works](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21273)
*Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dense prediction rewards under group-normalized RL (GRPO) catastrophically fail LLM agents by driving them to degenerate absorbing states (dark room pathology), but removing std normalization or using auxiliary losses instead fixes the problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to investigate why dense per-step supervision (rewarding agents for predicting next observations) fails for sparse-reward, long-horizon LLM agents under group-normalized RL, causing catastrophic policy collapse instead of improving performance.

Method: Experimental analysis using Qwen3 models on ALFWorld with potential-based prediction rewards under GRPO, ablation studies (removing std normalization), theoretical analysis of z-scored advantage invariance, variance-profile criterion development, and signal-delivery matrix comparisons between reward channels and auxiliary-loss channels.

Result: GRPO with std normalization causes 0% task success and degenerate absorbing states (dark room pathology), but removing std normalization restores baseline performance. The reward channel is at best neutral while auxiliary-loss channel gains ~20 points, with signal variance profile determining amplification safety.

Conclusion: Z-scoring in GRPO amplifies dense signal variance, creating unbounded optimization pressure that drives policies to degenerate states. Signals with decaying variance (as mastery improves) are safer, and auxiliary losses outperform reward channels for dense supervision in LLM agents.

Abstract: Dense per-step supervision is an appealing remedy for sparse-reward, long-horizon LLM agents: reward the agent for predicting its next observation, and memory should follow. We show that under group-normalized RL (GRPO), this recipe does not merely fail -- it destroys the policy. Across Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B on ALFWorld, a potential-based prediction reward drives every run into a degenerate absorbing state (prediction accuracy -> 1.0, task success -> 0,episode length pinned at the horizon): the "dark room" pathology, built automatically by the optimizer. A single-factor ablation localizes the cause -- removing only GRPO's std normalization turns the same reward from catastrophic (0%) into baseline parity -- and a two-line proposition explains why: in all-fail groups the z-scored advantage is invariant to the shaping coefficient, so bounded rewards become unbounded pressure and annealing cannot help. Our central insight generalizes this: what z-scoring amplifies is a dense signal's within-group variance while all-fail groups dominate, so signals whose variance decays by mastery are structurally amplifier-safe.This variance-profile criterion retrodicts our collapses, carries preregistered predictions for arms that had not yet run, and is consistent with published reward-channel successes (a compatibility check, not an independent test). Finally, a controlled signal-delivery matrix (identical signal, varying only the consumption mechanism) shows the reward channel is at best neutral while the auxiliary-loss channel gains ~20 points -- and a shuffled-gold placebo matches the true-gold arm, so the gap survives without correct labels. Endpoints are single-seed; seed replication and group-size controls are preregistered and in progress.

</details>


### [215] [Multi-Task Learning for Heterogeneous Prediction from Video Game State with Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21290)
*Jonas Peché,Aliaksei Tsishurou,Alexander Zap,Günter Wallner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-task learning improves generalization and reduces computational costs for video game prediction tasks compared to single-task models, using multimodal architecture with vision, context, and unit state inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern video game telemetry provides multiple related supervision signals from the same structured observations, making multi-task learning promising for prediction tasks derived from game state data. There's a need to improve generalization while reducing training and inference costs compared to specialized single-task models.

Method: Adapt a multimodal architecture for endpoint prediction to general multi-task setting combining: 1) rasterized vision inputs, 2) global match context, and 3) per-unit state information through an image encoder and attention-based interaction modeling. Use experiments on large proprietary World of Tanks dataset comparing single-task vs multi-task training, evaluating weighting strategies for mixed losses and conflicting gradients, and testing pre-training/fine-tuning under limited target-data regimes.

Result: The paper evaluates multi-task learning performance through comprehensive experiments including: comparison of single-task vs multi-task training, assessment of different weighting strategies for mixed losses and conflicting gradients, evaluation of pre-training/fine-tuning approaches under limited data conditions, and examination of within-game transfer across game maps under structured environment shift.

Conclusion: Multi-task learning shows promise for video game prediction tasks by leveraging shared representations across related tasks, potentially improving generalization while reducing computational costs. The work provides methodological insights for handling multiple supervision signals in gaming applications.

Abstract: Multi-task learning (MTL) is a promising approach for prediction tasks derived from video game state data, as modern game telemetry provides multiple related supervision signals from the same structured observations. We study whether a shared model trained jointly across tasks in team-based multiplayer games can improve generalization while reducing training and inference cost compared to specialized single-task models. We adapt a multimodal architecture for endpoint prediction to a general multi-task setting that combines rasterized vision inputs, global match context, and per-unit state information through an image encoder and attention-based interaction modeling. Experiments on a large proprietary World of Tanks dataset compare single-task and multi-task training, evaluate weighting strategies for mixed losses and conflicting gradients, and test pre-training/fine-tuning under limited target-data regimes. We also examine within-game transfer across game maps under structured environment shift.

</details>


### [216] [AI Assistants Overassist](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21306)
*Verona Teo,Raghav Jain,Tobias Gerstenberg,Max Kleiman-Weiner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Int-Bench is a simulation benchmark that evaluates how LLMs intervene as "teachers" during problem-solving across code debugging, math, and brain teasers, finding they intervene more frequently/earlier than humans and provide complete solutions rather than targeted hints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs are increasingly used as tutors and thought partners, there's poor understanding of how AI systems make intervention decisions during problem-solving. The benefits of AI guidance depend on how they help, as intervening too early or frequently may hinder true learning and cognitive engagement.

Method: Introduced Int-Bench, a simulation-based benchmark where an LLM "teacher" monitors a simulated "student" solving problems and decides whether, when, and how to intervene. Evaluated LLMs across three domains (code debugging, mathematics, and brain teasers) on frequency and timing of interventions, and their impact on immediate task success and generalization to new problems. Compared LLM performance to human teachers.

Result: LLMs intervene more frequently and earlier than humans. Unlike humans who provide targeted hints, LLMs tend to provide complete solutions. LLMs optimize for short-term task success rather than supporting the reasoning processes needed for deeper learning and long-term success.

Conclusion: Current LLM assistants often prioritize immediate problem-solving success over fostering genuine learning and reasoning skills, highlighting a gap in designing AI tutoring systems that better support long-term cognitive development through appropriate intervention strategies.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as tutors and thought partners, helping users reason through problems. While guidance from AI assistants can scaffold thinking and foster learning, such benefits depend on how they help--for instance, intervening too early or too frequently may hinder true learning and cognitive engagement. Yet how AI systems navigate intervention decisions during problem-solving remains poorly understood. Here, we introduce Int-Bench, a simulation-based benchmark for evaluating LLM interventions during learning. Int-Bench simulates a "student" solving a problem while a "teacher" monitors the student's reasoning and decides whether, when, and how to intervene. Across three domains--code debugging, mathematics, and brain teasers--we evaluate LLM teachers on the frequency and timing of interventions, as well as their impact on both immediate task success and generalization to new problems. We also compare LLMs to humans, finding that LLMs intervene more frequently and earlier than humans. Moreover, in contrast to humans, they tend to provide complete solutions rather than targeted hints. These findings suggest that current LLM assistants often optimize for short-term success rather than supporting the reasoning processes needed for deeper learning and long-term success.

</details>


### [217] [M$^3$-Gen: Interpretable Multimodal Generation of Gene Expression Profiles Using Clinical and Imaging Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21343)
*Francesca Pia Panaccione,Carlo Sgaravatti,Marco Venere*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: M³-Gen is a multimodal generative framework that creates realistic gene expression profiles from histopathology images and clinical metadata using GANs and contrastive learning, providing explainable AI through attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gene expression data acquisition is expensive and privacy-sensitive, limiting its use in multimodal biomedical research and AI applications, creating a need for synthetic generation methods.

Method: Uses Generative Adversarial Network conditioned on histopathology images and clinical metadata with contrastive learning to create unified latent representations, then generates gene expression profiles guided by multimodal embeddings with attention-based explainability.

Result: Demonstrated on TCGA dataset that M³-Gen generates realistic and functionally meaningful gene expression data while providing intrinsic explainability through attention mechanisms identifying influential image regions.

Conclusion: The framework successfully addresses gene expression data limitations by generating biologically coherent profiles from multimodal inputs while offering interpretable AI by design through attention-based explainability.

Abstract: Integrating heterogeneous biomedical data, including clinical metadata, histopathology images, and molecular profiles, is crucial for comprehensive disease understanding. However, gene expression data acquisition remains constrained by high costs and privacy concerns, limiting its use in multimodal research and AI-driven applications. We present MultiModal Molecular Generation (M$^3$-Gen), a novel framework for the generation of gene expression profiles by conditioning a Generative Adversarial Network on histopathology images and clinical metadata. M$^3$-Gen learns a unified latent representation from the clinical variables and the images, leveraging contrastive learning, and exploits the embeddings of the two modalities to guide a generative model in producing biologically coherent gene expression profiles. Evaluations on the TCGA dataset demonstrate that M$^3$-Gen generates realistic and functionally meaningful gene expression data. Importantly, by integrating multiple modalities in an attention-based mechanism, M$^3$-Gen provides intrinsic explainability: it allows the identification of which regions of the histopathology images most strongly influenced the generation of specific gene expression profiles, making the model's decisions interpretable by design.

</details>


### [218] [How Many Bits Can an Adapter Write? Measuring the Capacity and Memorization of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21351)
*Kaizhen Tan,Heqing Du,Yang Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that LoRA adapters store surprisingly little information from training data (just a couple bits per trainable parameter), and the storage capacity depends more on where parameters are placed than how many there are. Adapters trained with supervised fine-tuning leak more privacy than those trained with reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the common assumption that LoRA adapters are treated as mere "skills" rather than records of training data. There's a need to measure exactly how much information adapters store about training data, especially for privacy implications and understanding the difference between different training methods like supervised vs reinforcement learning.

Method: The authors extend compression-based memorization analysis to the frozen-base setting. They develop a method to directly measure, in bits, how much information a low-rank adapter writes into a model's frozen base. They manipulate parameter placement (attention vs MLP layers) and study different training methods (supervised fine-tuning vs reinforcement learning).

Result: Adapters store only 2 bits per trainable parameter, far less than full fine-tuning. Storage capacity depends more on parameter location than count: MLP-located parameters hold nearly twice as much as attention-located ones. When trained on verifiable rewards (RL), adapters store far less sensitive information than supervised fine-tuning, which copies data verbatim. Privacy leakage correlates with bits written rather than parameter count.

Conclusion: By measuring what fine-tuning actually writes rather than attacking models after training, the paper provides quantifiable metrics that can guide privacy-aware model design. The findings distinguish between supervised and reinforcement learning approaches in terms of privacy leakage and provide a basis for designing adapters with specific privacy guarantees.

Abstract: A LoRA adapter is a few megabytes that almost everyone treats as a skill rather than a record of the data behind it. We put that assumption on a scale. Extending compression-based memorization analysis to the frozen-base setting, we measure directly, in bits, how much a low-rank adapter writes into a model it never changes. The answer is both smaller than full fine-tuning and less lawful than parameter counting would predict. Adapters store a couple of bits per trainable parameter, well short of a full model's budget, but that figure turns less on how many parameters an adapter carries than on where they sit. Move the same parameter budget from attention into the MLP and it holds nearly twice as much; strip the frozen base of its structure and the capacity all but disappears. Applied to realistic fine-tunes of Qwen2.5, the same instrument shows privacy leakage rising with the bits an adapter writes rather than the parameters it nominally has, and it draws a clean line between supervised and reinforcement learning: the secrets that supervised fine-tuning copies down verbatim, an adapter trained on verifiable rewards never records. Measuring what fine-tuning writes, rather than attacking it after the fact, turns a piece of folklore into a quantity one can design against.

</details>


### [219] [Gradient Concentration, Not Weight Saliency, Explains Representation-Level Class Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21353)
*Billel Habbati,Alessio Merlo,Luca Verderame,Meriem Guerar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Saliency-based weight masking in machine unlearning doesn't significantly improve representation-level forgetting compared to random masks; gradient concentration and representation geometry are the primary drivers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether saliency-based weight selection in machine unlearning methods like SalUn actually contributes to representation-level forgetting, or if other factors are more important.

Method: Controlled ablation study comparing saliency-based masking against random masks and unconstrained updates with matched compute budget on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 using ResNet-18, measuring representation-level metrics like linear probing, prototype recovery, and CKA.

Result: All three masking strategies (saliency-based, random masks, unconstrained) show statistically equivalent representation-level recoverability. Forget gradients concentrate heavily (92%) in final network layers, causing all masks to operate in same representational subspace. Saliency masks show limited class specificity (0.09-0.11).

Conclusion: Representation-level forgetting is driven by gradient concentration and representation geometry, not by specific saliency-selected weights. Effective unlearning requires objectives that act directly on latent representations rather than sophisticated weight-selection strategies.

Abstract: Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of specific training data while preserving model utility. Many state-of-the-art approaches pursue this goal by restricting the forgetting update to a subset of parameters selected through gradient-based saliency. Although such methods are widely adopted, the actual contribution of saliency-based weight selection to representation-level forgetting remains unclear. In this work, we perform the first controlled ablation of the saliency masking mechanism used by SalUn. Using a matched-compute experimental design on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with ResNet-18, we compare saliency-based masking against random masks of equal sparsity and unconstrained updates, while keeping the unlearning objective, optimization schedule, and computational budget fixed. Across multiple representation-level evaluations, including linear probing, prototype recovery, and layer-wise CKA, the three configurations exhibit statistically equivalent representation-level recoverability. We find that forget gradients are strongly concentrated in the final network layers (approximately 92% of the squared gradient energy on CIFAR-10) before any mask is applied, causing all masking strategies to operate within the same representational subspace. Furthermore, saliency masks show limited class specificity (specificity index 0.09-0.11), selecting highly overlapping parameter subsets across different forget classes. Our findings suggest that, in the studied setting, representation-level forgetting is primarily governed by gradient concentration and representation geometry rather than by the specific identity of saliency-selected weights. More broadly, the results support a growing body of evidence indicating that effective representation-level unlearning requires objectives that act directly on latent representations rather than on increasingly sophisticated weight-selection strategies.

</details>


### [220] [Emergent Misalignment Recruits a Pre-existing Persona Subspace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21356)
*Mohammed Suhail B Nadaf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fine-tuning language models on narrow bad advice causes broad misalignment through pre-existing persona subspaces that can be identified and manipulated to control this effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why narrow fine-tuning on bad advice causes broad misalignment across unrelated domains, a phenomenon called emergent misalignment, and to investigate the underlying mechanisms that allow this generalization to occur.

Method: Extracted per-domain persona subspaces from a frozen instruction-tuned model using contrastive teacher forcing; identified shared low-rank core structure across domains; conducted experiments with subspace projection during fine-tuning, subspace injection, gradient analysis, and post-hoc weight edits; tested misalignment with single-domain vs multi-domain fine-tuning.

Result: Found that 4 unrelated domains share a low-rank persona core (657x above random null); projecting out this subspace prevents broad misalignment (27.7% to 0.0%) while leaving narrow behavior unaffected; injecting the subspace induces misalignment (up to 45.4%); weight edits suppress but don't remove the behavior; multi-domain fine-tuning produces more misalignment than superposition can explain.

Conclusion: Emergent misalignment occurs when narrow fine-tuning recruits pre-existing persona structures rather than creating new ones; these structures can be identified, manipulated, and projected out to prevent broad misalignment while still learning narrow tasks, suggesting persona-aware interventions for alignment safety.

Abstract: Fine-tuning an aligned language model on a narrow stream of bad advice can make it broadly misaligned on questions unrelated to the training data, a phenomenon called emergent misalignment. We ask why the narrow lesson generalizes at all, and we find that narrow fine-tuning recruits a persona structure that is present in the model before the fine-tune exists. From a frozen instruction-tuned model (Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct) we extract per-domain persona subspaces by contrastive teacher forcing and find that 4 unrelated domains share one low-rank core at 657x a random-subspace null, with 82% of that core lying outside a style core built at matched diversity. The literal first optimizer step of fine-tuning on insecure code climbs a broad-misalignment margin harder than the same code framed as educational, and forecasts realized margin movement out to 375 steps. Projecting the subspace out of the residual stream throughout fine-tuning prevents broad misalignment (27.7% to 0.0% of judged generations) while a matched-rank random subspace changes nothing; injecting it into the never-fine-tuned model induces misalignment that grows with dose to 45.4%, past the fine-tuned model it is measured against. The same projection applied to the weight gradient is inert, and three post-hoc weight edits leave the disposition in place: the sharpest edit suppresses the behavior rather than removing it, and the ablated structure re-forms inside the subspace the edit cleared. Spreading a fixed budget of bad data across 4 domains produces more broad misalignment than mechanical weight superposition and matched diversity jointly account for. All measurements come from one model at 14B; the extraction is from an aligned instruction-tuned checkpoint, which leaves the structure's provenance open; and the intervention that prevents misalignment also abolishes the narrow trained behavior.

</details>


### [221] [Hilbert Operator for Progressive Encoding (HOPE): A Mathematical Framework for Deconstructing Learned Representations in Deep Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21366)
*Hossein Mobahi,Peter L. Bartlett*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HOPE (Hilbert Operator for Progressive Encoding) is a mathematical framework that transforms network compression from discrete operations into continuous Hilbert space functions, enabling unbiased analysis and compression of neural network representations without data or hyperparameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While network compression offers a promising way to analyze the internal knowledge encoded in deep neural networks, standard compression methods are limited by scale symmetries and architectural biases. The authors aim to develop a framework that can deconstruct network representations in a more principled, unbiased manner.

Method: HOPE models neural network compression in a Hilbert space of continuous functions. Individual neurons are represented as rank-1 Hilbert-Schmidt operators, allowing pruning and neuron merging to be unified as low-rank subspace projection. The framework extends to macro block eviction for multi-layer structures like residual pathways, providing a unified metric for architectural decisions across different layer types and sizes.

Result: The authors present proof-of-concept experiments in model compression and fine-tuning that demonstrate the practical potential of HOPE. The framework is data-free and hyperparameter-free, enabling unbiased compression decisions.

Conclusion: HOPE provides a unified mathematical foundation for neural network compression that overcomes scale symmetry and architectural bias problems, offering a principled approach to analyzing and deconstructing the representations learned by deep neural networks.

Abstract: Deep neural networks encode complex representations, but deconstructing this internal knowledge remains a challenge. Given the link between learning and compression, network compression offers a promising lens to analyze this knowledge. However, standard compression heuristics often suffer from scale symmetries and architectural biases. To resolve these, we introduce Hilbert Operator for Progressive Encoding (HOPE), a mathematical framework to gradually deconstruct the representations in trained network weights.
  HOPE shifts network compression from the discrete domain into a Hilbert space of continuous functions. By modeling individual neurons as rank-1 Hilbert-Schmidt operators, HOPE unifies pruning and neuron merging as low-rank subspace projection. Extending this formulation, HOPE introduces macro block eviction to encompass multi-layer structures like entire residual pathways under the same unified metric. This unified approach enables unbiased architectural decisions across layers with different types and sizes. HOPE is a data-free and hyperparameter-free framework. We present proof-of-concept experiments in model compression and fine-tuning to highlight the practical potential of our theory.

</details>


### [222] [Mean-to-Score Discrete Diffusion: Posterior-Mean Denoisers for Score Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21372)
*Jingyuan Li,Xiaoyi Jiang,Yixuan Jiang,Wei Liu,Yi Zhu,Zuoqiang Shi,Pipi Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SEDD diffusion models sometimes violate Bayes realizability constraints, causing sampling issues. The paper introduces Mean-to-Score (M2S) which ensures valid posteriors by predicting clean-token posterior means and mapping them to scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Score Entropy Discrete Diffusion (SEDD) parameterizes discrete reverse processes with positive score ratios, but these ratios don't guarantee Bayes realizability. Violations can occur where score vectors aren't induced by any valid posterior under the forward kernel, leading to negative weights in sampling and degraded performance.

Method: Introduces Mean-to-Score (M2S) which predicts clean-token posterior means and converts them to scores through an exact kernel-dependent linear map. This ensures score vectors always correspond to valid posteriors. The method works for any coordinate-wise continuous-time Markov chain satisfying mild support conditions.

Result: M2S improves performance across multiple benchmarks: lowers CIFAR-10 test BPD from 3.173 to 3.129, reduces generative perplexity from 203.6 to 175.1, and a 170M-parameter model achieves PPL 143.3 at 128 steps versus 183.6 for SEDD baselines. It outperforms pure-uniform SEDD, GIDD, and Neural CTMC checkpoints at every sampling budget.

Conclusion: Ensuring Bayes realizability through M2S improves discrete diffusion model performance by guaranteeing valid posteriors, addressing fundamental limitations in SEDD's score parameterization while maintaining flexibility across different forward processes.

Abstract: Score Entropy Discrete Diffusion (SEDD) parameterizes discrete reverse processes with unconstrained positive score ratios. While positivity guarantees nonnegative reverse jump rates, it does not ensure Bayes realizability: ratios at a noisy state need not be jointly induced by any clean-token posterior under the forward kernel. The score-entropy loss has the correct population optimum but does not enforce this constraint away from it. In a trained pure-uniform SEDD checkpoint, roughly one quarter of complete score vectors violate the coordinate box, while more than half lie inside it yet remain materially incompatible with any valid posterior. Such violations can produce negative pre-normalization weights in finite-step sampling. Projecting raw scores onto the bridge polytope removes all observed negative weights and improves external generative PPL from $203.6$ to $175.1$ without changing the sampler. We introduce \emph{mean-to-score} (M2S), which predicts a clean-token posterior mean and converts it to the score through an exact kernel-dependent linear map. The construction applies to any known coordinate-wise continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC) satisfying a mild support condition. For uniform corruption, it maps the probability simplex onto the bridge polytope; for absorbing-mask corruption, the resulting objective recovers MD4 exactly. In a controlled 28.4M-parameter CIFAR-10 comparison, M2S lowers test BPD from $3.173$ to $3.129$ and FID-50k from $\CifarSEDDFID$ to $\CifarMtwoSFID$. A 170M-parameter M2S model trained on about 262B OpenWebText token slots outperforms the evaluated pure-uniform SEDD, GIDD, and Neural CTMC checkpoints at every tested sampling budget, reaching generative PPL $143.3$ at 128 steps versus $183.6$ for the strongest pure-uniform baseline.

</details>


### [223] [A Diffusion-Model Subpopulation Digital Twin for Mobile Health Deployment: A Case Study on the HeartSteps Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21403)
*Ziping Xu,Yuyi Chang,Chenshun Ni,Nithin Sugavanam,Asim H. Gazi,Pedja Klasnja,Emre Ertin,Susan A. Murphy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes "JITAI-Twins" - digital twins for simulating mobile health intervention subpopulations to test online learning algorithms before deployment, validated on HeartSteps physical activity studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile health interventions using online learning algorithms can burden participants if poorly designed, but testing algorithms in real deployments is risky. Need realistic simulated users to vet algorithm design decisions before real-life implementation.

Method: Develops JITAI-Twins using conditional time-series diffusion models that maintain temporal consistency. Uses three-step approach: 1) pre-training on large observational data, 2) fine-tuning on small prior interventions in related populations, 3) inference-time calibration to target population using domain expertise.

Result: Method validated on HeartSteps v2-v4 physical activity intervention deployments. JITAI-Twins reproduce target subpopulation's temporal and between-participant structure better than simpler simulators, enabling pre-deployment simulation testing.

Conclusion: JITAI-Twins can effectively simulate target deployments before they run, providing a crucial tool for testing and informing online algorithm design decisions in mobile health interventions while reducing participant burden.

Abstract: Mobile-health interventions increasingly use online learning and decision making algorithms to personalize when to nudge users toward healthier behavior, but a poorly designed algorithm can burden and disengage participants. New algorithm design decisions should therefore be vetted against realistic simulated users before each real-life deployment. We propose a method to develop ``JITAI-Twins'': digital twins of a target subpopulation for comparing candidate online algorithms before a just-in-time adaptive intervention (JITAI) deployment. The method builds on a conditional time-series diffusion model that is temporally consistent (future actions do not affect the generated past), and it supports repeated updating from three sources of information, in three steps: pre-training on a large observational dataset, fine-tuning on small prior intervention deployments in related populations, and inference-time calibration to the next target population from domain-scientist expertise. We validate the twin at each pre-deployment stage of the long-running HeartSteps series (v2 through v4) of physical-activity suggestion intervention deployments, treating each successive deployment as an upcoming study. The proposed method reproduces the target subpopulation's temporal and between-participant structure better than simpler simulators. These results suggest that our twin can be used to simulate a target deployment before it runs, the prerequisite for testing and informing online algorithm design decisions.

</details>


### [224] [Semantic-Aware Task Clustering for Constructive and Cooperative Multi-Tasking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21426)
*Ahmad Halimi Razlighi,Maximilian H. V. Tillmann,Edgar Beck,Bho Matthiesen,Armin Dekorsy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes semantic-aware task clustering for cooperative multi-task semantic communication to ensure constructive cooperation by grouping semantically aligned tasks and training them jointly within clusters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative multi-task semantic communication can improve task performance through shared representations, but cooperation can be destructive if tasks have conflicting semantics. The authors previously demonstrated that multi-tasking can be either constructive or destructive depending on semantic relationships between tasks, creating a need to ensure constructive cooperation.

Method: Proposes a sequential multi-stage optimization: 1) semantic clustering using hierarchical density-based spatial clustering after short initial training, and 2) end-to-end joint training only within discovered semantically-aligned task groups. The problem decomposes into semantic clustering and intra-cluster E2E CMT-SemCom learning.

Result: Simulation results show the framework effectively mitigates destructive cooperation and negative transfer, achieving accuracy gains compared to both unclustered multi-tasking baselines and individual task training baselines.

Conclusion: Semantic-aware task clustering is essential for ensuring constructive cooperation in CMT-SemCom systems, preventing negative transfer between tasks with conflicting semantics while leveraging the benefits of cooperative multi-tasking for semantically aligned tasks.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-task semantic communication (CMT-SemCom) improves task execution performance by leveraging shared representations. However, as we demonstrated in [1], cooperative multi-tasking can be either constructive or destructive, depending on the semantic relationships among tasks. To ensure constructive cooperation, we propose a semantic-aware task clustering method for CMT-SemCom. We have formulated a sequential multi-stage optimization problem in which semantically aligned tasks are clustered once after a short initial training phase, and then end-to-end (E2E) joint training is conducted exclusively within the discovered groups. Specifically, the problem decomposes into two stages: (i) a semantic clustering problem leveraging hierarchical density-based spatial clustering, and (ii) an intra-cluster E2E CMT-SemCom learning problem. Simulation results demonstrate that the proposed framework effectively mitigates destructive cooperation and negative transfer, yielding accuracy gains compared to unclustered multi-tasking and individual training baselines.

</details>


### [225] [Context-weighted Discrete Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21427)
*Daniil Cherniavskii,Daniel Severo,Karen Ullrich*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes context-aware modifications to discrete flow matching that improve training and sampling efficiency by addressing varying token prediction difficulty through local context information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard factorized training in discrete flow matching exposes models to targets with varying difficulty levels, mixing easy predictable tokens with ambiguous, high-entropy ones. This leads to inefficient training and sampling.

Method: Two key modifications: 1) A context-weighted sampler that incorporates local context information into the underlying continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC), and 2) A scaled cross-entropy loss function that reweights training signals from different tokens based on their context density.

Result: Improves generation quality with negligible computational overhead, reduces generative perplexity by up to 63% on OpenWebText, and matches strong semi-autoregressive block diffusion baselines while retaining flexible generation order.

Conclusion: Local context is a crucial factor in discrete generative modeling, and simple context-aware modifications can significantly enhance both sampling and training efficiency in discrete flow matching frameworks.

Abstract: Discrete flow matching provides a flexible framework for generative modeling on discrete structures. However, the standard factorized training objective exposes the model to targets of varying difficulty, mixing well-conditioned, predictable tokens with ambiguous, high-entropy ones. We empirically demonstrate that the uncertainty over the value of each token is closely related to the density of available context in its neighborhood. Motivated by this observation, we propose a simple modification to the underlying continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC) that incorporates local context information. Our context-weighted sampler improves generation quality with negligible computational overhead, while our scaled cross-entropy loss function reweights the training signal from different tokens and reduces generative perplexity by up to 63% on OpenWebText. Moreover, our approach matches a strong semi-autoregressive block diffusion baseline in quality while retaining the ability to perform generation in any order. These results highlight the role of local context as an important factor in discrete generative modeling and show that simple context-aware modifications can significantly improve both sampling and training efficiency.

</details>


### [226] [KroQuant: Kronecker-Structured Block Transforms for Efficient Post-Training Quantization of Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21446)
*Yann Bouquet,Alireza Khodamoradi,Kristof Denolf,Mathieu Salzmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KroQuant: A post-training quantization method using learned Kronecker-structured transforms for efficient W4A4 diffusion transformers, achieving better quality than existing methods with lower computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PTQ of diffusion transformers to W4A4 degrades output quality due to activation outliers. Existing solutions trade quantization quality for inference cost: per-channel scaling harms accuracy, fixed Hadamard transforms have high online cost, and learned full transforms are computationally prohibitive.

Method: Proposes KroQuant which applies learned Kronecker-structured invertible transforms to 32-element activation blocks.
