Uses offline LoRaQ weight calibration to absorb residual quantization errors. The block-local structure enables efficient small tensor-core GEMMs with fewer parameters than per-channel scaling.

Result: KroQuant produces outputs closer to FP reference than SVDQuant and LoRaQ on PixArt-Σ, SANA, and FLUX.1-schnell at W4A4, while preserving/improving image quality on MJHQ-30K and SDCI benchmarks. On MI350 GPU, the quantizer kernel is up to 14% faster than SmoothQuant kernel.

Conclusion: KroQuant achieves a better trade-off between quantization quality and inference cost for W4A4 diffusion transformers through efficient Kronecker-structured transforms, outperforming existing methods in both accuracy and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) of diffusion transformers (DiTs) to W4A4 severely degrades output quality, because activations entering each linear layer contain outliers that 4-bit formats cannot represent. The standard fix applies an invertible linear transform to the activations and its inverse to the weights before quantizing both. Normalization layers between blocks force this transform to run online at every denoising step, making its inference computation cost the binding design constraint. Existing options trade quantization quality for inference cost: per-channel scaling (SmoothQuant) is computationally cheap but impacts the magnitude of the channels, which can harm quantization accuracy; fixed Hadamard transforms yield better quantization accuracy but require large block sizes that incur a high online cost; learned full-$d$ invertible transforms calibrate best but entail a prohibitive dense $d \times d$ matrix multiplication (GEMM) per layer per step.
  We propose KroQuant, a PTQ method that applies a learned Kronecker-structured invertible transform to each 32-element block of the activation, storing less than half the parameters of per-channel scaling. The block-local structure runs as small tensor-core GEMMs, and on an MI350 GPU the KroQuant quantizer kernel is up to $14\%$ faster than the SmoothQuant kernel. Offline LoRaQ weight calibration then absorbs the residual per-weight quantization error. On PixArt-$Σ$, SANA, and FLUX.1-schnell at W4A4 (MXFP4e2), KroQuant produces outputs closer to the FP reference than SVDQuant and LoRaQ on MJHQ-30K and SDCI, while preserving or improving image quality.

</details>


### [227] [Test-Time Scaling via Error Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21453)
*Rajiv Shailesh Chitale,Rahul Madhavan,Taneesh Gupta,Deepanway Ghosal,Aravindan Raghuveer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTEL (Test-Time Scaling via Error Localization) is an inference-time algorithm that uses feedback to perform token-level error localization, allowing efficient reuse of valid reasoning prefixes and establishing Pareto-optimal performance on reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard inference-time scaling approaches like independent sampling and sequential refinement lack token-level credit assignment, leading to computational inefficiency as valid reasoning prefixes are frequently discarded.

Method: TTEL uses fixed or environment feedback to perform token-level error localization by comparing conditional probabilities under informed feedback against a null-context baseline. It isolates error steps, truncates trajectories, and branches new generations while maximally reusing valid prefixes.

Result: TTEL establishes strictly dominating Pareto frontiers across sequential reasoning domains (pass-at-k vs. generated-token cost). On LiveCodeBench with Qwen3-8B: 71.0% pass@64 with ~360.4k tokens (vs 735.0k for independent sampling). Outperforms baselines on AIME-2025 and HMMT-2025 math benchmarks with both Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507.

Conclusion: TTEL provides an effective inference-time scaling method that addresses computational inefficiency in existing approaches through token-level error localization, enabling substantial reductions in token generation while maintaining or improving performance on complex reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Scaling inference-time computation has emerged as a reliable method to improve the performance of large language models on complex reasoning and programming tasks. However, standard approaches such as independent sampling and sequential multi-turn refinement operate without token-level credit assignment, resulting in computational inefficiency, since valid reasoning prefixes are frequently discarded. In this work, we introduce Test-Time Scaling via Error Localization (TTEL), an inference-time algorithm that utilizes fixed or environment feedback to perform token-level error localization. By comparing conditional probabilities under informed feedback against a null-context baseline, TTEL isolates the step at which an error occurred. The algorithm then truncates the trajectory and branches a new generation, maximally reusing the valid prefix. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that TTEL establishes strictly dominating Pareto frontiers across sequential reasoning domains, measured by pass-at-k vs. generated-token cost. With Qwen3-8B on LiveCodeBench, TTEL attains a pass@64 of 71.0% while generating approximately half as many tokens as independent sampling (360.4k vs. 735.0k). Generalizing to math benchmarks AIME-2025 and HMMT-2025, TTEL cleanly outperforms competing test-time baselines across both Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507.

</details>


### [228] [Error Certificates for KV-Cache Eviction via Randomized Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21475)
*Peng Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Randomized KV-cache eviction enables reliable error attribution for attention approximations, separating cache-induced from inherent model failures, unlike deterministic methods which can't guarantee consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic KV-cache eviction (keeping top-k tokens) suffers from unidentifiable error - evicted values can be manipulated to make retained values unchanged while actual attention errors grow arbitrarily. This makes it impossible to create consistent serving-time error estimators for deterministic approaches.

Method: Proposes randomized eviction using Poisson-sampling for tail tokens with known inclusion probabilities. Implements Hájek correction via a single logit offset inside softmax, and uses survey-sampling variance estimator over retained set to create per-step error certificates with empirical coverage around 0.97 at no accuracy cost.

Result: Randomization enables attribution: certificate separates cache-induced from inherent failures (AUC 0.73-0.75 vs. 0.47-0.54 for output confidence), schedules recomputation better than random or confidence gating. Question-aware eviction at 25-50% budgets is nearly free. Output log-probability predicts failure better than certificate; certificate-gated budget escalation adds nothing.

Conclusion: Randomization provides attribution capabilities rather than prediction - it enables reliable identification of cache-induced failures versus inherent model failures, which deterministic methods cannot achieve. This allows better recomputation scheduling and error analysis in KV-cache approximation systems.

Abstract: Deterministic KV-cache eviction keeps the top-$k$ tokens under an importance score and deletes the rest. We prove that this design cannot know what it destroyed: evicted values can be altered so that everything the serving system retains is unchanged while the true attention-output error grows arbitrarily, so no serving-time estimator of that error is consistent. Randomized eviction restores identifiability. With a Poisson-sampled tail at known inclusion probabilities, one logit offset performs the Hájek correction inside the softmax, and a survey-sampling variance estimator over the retained set becomes a per-step error certificate with 0.97 empirical coverage at no accuracy cost. On real workloads we pre-registered seven claims and lost three: question-aware eviction at 25--50\% budgets is nearly free; output log-probability predicts failure better than the certificate; certificate-gated budget escalation adds nothing. What survives is attribution: the certificate separates cache-induced from inherent failures (AUC 0.73--0.75, against 0.47--0.54 for output confidence) and schedules recomputation better than random or confidence gating. Randomization buys attribution, not prediction.

</details>


### [229] [Finite-Sample Coverage Audits for High-Recall Candidate Generation: Certification and Learning-Theoretic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21480)
*Martin Anthony,Kaveh Salehzadeh Nobari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies label complexity for certifying missed relevant mass in empirical pipelines, showing excluded-pool auditing is minimax optimal and developing exact finite-sample certification tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In empirical pipelines, an initial high-recall stage passes items to later review/labelling/modelling, and any relevant items missed at this stage are lost forever to subsequent stages. The problem is certifying with finite-sample validity that this missed relevant mass is small, which requires understanding how many audit labels are needed.

Method: The paper first proves theoretical results about label complexity: 1) no procedure using only labels from inside the candidate set can certify non-trivial bounds on missed mass; 2) matching finite-corpus lower bounds show excluded-pool auditing is minimax rate-optimal. Building on this, the authors develop an exact finite-sample toolkit using binomial and hypergeometric inversion (rather than asymptotic approximation). The approach certifies missed mass, converts it to recall through two-pool design, certifies pre-specified families of nested candidate generators simultaneously, and produces stress-test certificates against declared perturbation mechanisms.

Result: The key theoretical results show that: 1) Auditing must sample the excluded pool to certify missed mass bounds; 2) Any valid audit certifying fewer than m missed relevant items with high probability when none are present must inspect on the order of N₀/m excluded-pool labels (where N₀ is excluded pool size). Excluded-pool auditing is therefore minimax rate-optimal. The practical toolkit provides exact finite-sample certification with observable review burden, allowing selection of the least burdensome candidate generator meeting a missed-mass target.

Conclusion: Excluded-pool auditing is not just convenient but fundamentally necessary for missed-mass certification, being minimax optimal. The developed exact finite-sample certification methods provide rigorous tools for pipeline quality assurance, with all guarantees holding under the discipline that candidate generators and audit rules are fixed before examining certification labels.

Abstract: An initial high-recall stage in an empirical pipeline decides which items pass to later review, labelling, or modelling, and relevant items it misses are lost to every subsequent stage. We study how many audit labels are needed to certify, with finite-sample validity, that this missed relevant mass is small, and our main results characterise the label complexity of this problem. We first show that no procedure using only labels from inside the candidate set can certify any non-trivial bound on the missed mass: the audit must sample the excluded pool, the only region where unrecovered relevant items can lie. We then prove a matching finite-corpus lower bound. Any valid audit that certifies fewer than $m$ missed relevant items with high probability when none are present, even if adaptive and permitted to label the entire included pool, must inspect on the order of $N_0/m$ excluded-pool labels. Excluded-pool auditing is therefore minimax rate-optimal, not merely convenient, for missed-mass certification in the zero-miss regime. Building on this characterisation, we develop an exact finite-sample toolkit, using binomial and hypergeometric inversion rather than asymptotic approximation, that certifies missed mass, converts it to recall through a two-pool design, certifies pre-specified families of nested candidate generators simultaneously, and produces stress-test certificates against declared perturbation mechanisms. These certificates can be paired with observable review burden to select the least burdensome pre-specified candidate generator meeting a missed-mass target. Every guarantee holds under one discipline: the candidate generator, or the pre-specified family from which it is selected, and the audit rule are fixed before the certification labels are examined.

</details>


### [230] [Windowed-MTP: Removing the Full-Context Draft-KV Tax at Million-Token Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21535)
*Alagappan Valliappan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Speculative decoding with Multi-Token-Prediction (MTP) draft heads becomes inefficient at long contexts due to full attention over the entire KV cache. A training-free sliding window approach applied only to the draft (Windowed-MTP) reduces KV working set by ~99% at 1M tokens, improving decoding latency by 28-44% while preserving output quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: At million-token contexts, MTP draft heads in speculative decoding become inefficient because they run full attention over the entire KV cache, making the draft cost dominate the decoding process. This undermines the benefits of speculative decoding precisely where it should be most valuable, especially with hybrid/linear-attention targets where cheaper verification exposes the draft's expensive full-attention reads.

Method: Apply a StreamingLLM-style sliding window plus attention sink to the draft's attention only (Windowed-MTP), leaving full-attention verification intact. This training-free, drop-in approach bounds the draft's KV working set to a constant. The unread draft KV (7.7-11% of total KV at 1M) is reclaimed via a compact ring buffer.

Result: Windowing reduces KV entries by ~99% at 1M context. Across three architecture families (Qwen GDN-MoE 35B/122B and Mamba2-hybrid NoPE 120B), windowing cuts per-decode-step cost by 28-44% over native MTP draft. The improvement is input-invariant and widens with context. End-to-end decode latency improves proportionally, with no quality degradation since the full-attention target still verifies every accepted token.

Conclusion: Windowed-MTP provides an efficient, lossless solution to the scalability problem of speculative decoding at long contexts. By applying sliding window attention only to the draft component, it dramatically reduces KV cache overhead while maintaining verification accuracy, making speculative decoding practical for million-token contexts.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by having a cheap draft propose tokens that a target verifies in parallel. Frontier models increasingly ship a built-in Multi-Token-Prediction (MTP/NEXTN) draft head under the assumption that the draft is negligibly cheap. At million-token context this breaks: an MTP draft head typically runs full attention over the entire KV cache at every draft step, so its read grows linearly with context and comes to dominate the draft cost -- precisely where speculation is most valuable. The effect compounds with draft length (a deep native draft can turn net-negative, slower than no speculation) and sharpens under hybrid/linear-attention targets, where cheaper verification leaves the draft's full-attention read exposed. We apply a StreamingLLM-style sliding window plus attention sink to the draft's attention only (Windowed-MTP), leaving full-attention verification intact. It is training-free, drop-in, and lossless by construction: the full-attention target still decides every accepted token, so windowing changes only which tokens are proposed, never which are accepted. It bounds the draft's KV working set to a constant, dropping ~99% of KV entries at 1M. Across three architecture families (Qwen GDN-MoE 35B/122B and a Mamba2-hybrid NoPE 120B) at 1M context on a single GPU in SGLang, windowing cuts the per-decode-step cost over the shipping native MTP draft by +28% to +44%, an input-invariant margin that widens with context. Since per-token latency is this cost divided by acceptance length, at matched acceptance end-to-end decode latency improves by the same amount, and more where windowing also lifts acceptance, while preserving the target's verified output distribution. Finally, the unread draft KV -- 7.7-11% of total KV at 1M -- is reclaimed via a compact ring buffer at no acceptance or quality cost.

</details>


### [231] [Zero-Flow Two-Sample Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21542)
*Yakun Wang,Leyang Wang,Song Liu,Taiji Suzuki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a novel two-sample test called zero-flow two-sample test (ZF2ST) based on zero-flow discrepancy (ZFD), which learns local misalignment patterns between distributions as evidence of difference, achieving strong power for structured changes with calibrated type-I error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for a two-sample testing approach that can flexibly use neural networks while maintaining valid statistical calibration. Existing methods may struggle with structured distributional changes or face challenges separating witness learning from hypothesis evaluation.

Method: Builds test on zero-flow discrepancy (ZFD) based on zero-flow criterion. Key idea: learn how samples from two distributions are locally misaligned and use resulting directional pattern as evidence. Separates witness learning from hypothesis evaluation to allow flexible neural networks while maintaining calibration. Develops both regression-based and power-maximized approaches for learning the witness.

Result: Proves validity of ZFD and proposes practical ZF2ST testing procedure. Experiments on synthetic and image datasets demonstrate strong testing power for structured distributional changes while maintaining well-calibrated type-I error.

Conclusion: ZF2ST provides an effective two-sample testing framework that combines the flexibility of neural network witness learning with proper statistical calibration, making it suitable for detecting structured distributional changes in practical applications.

Abstract: We propose a new approach to two-sample testing for deciding whether two sets of samples are drawn from the same distribution. The test is built on a statistical discrepancy based on the zero-flow criterion, termed zero-flow discrepancy (ZFD). We prove the validity of ZFD and propose a practical testing procedure, termed the zero-flow two-sample test (ZF2ST). The key idea is to learn how samples from the two distributions are locally misaligned and use the resulting directional pattern as evidence of distributional difference. By separating witness learning from hypothesis evaluation, ZF2ST can use flexible neural networks while maintaining valid statistical calibration. We develop both regression-based and power-maximized approaches for learning the witness. Experiments on synthetic and image datasets demonstrate that ZF2ST can achieve strong testing power for structured distributional changes while maintaining well-calibrated type-I error.

</details>


### [232] [X$^3$-OPD: Distilling Reasoning into Large Audio-Language Models via On-Policy Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21550)
*Dongjie Fu,Di Cao,Xize Cheng,Zihan Zhang,Wenxu Jia,Yifu Chen,Shengpeng Ji,Yu Zhang,Tao Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: X³-OPD is a cross-modal on-policy distillation framework that transfers reasoning capabilities from text-based LLMs to audio-language models using three-tier audio reasoning data and token-level guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio-language models lag behind text-based LLMs in deep logical reasoning due to scarcity of high-quality audio reasoning data, creating a need to bridge this reasoning capability gap.

Method: Proposes X³-OPD framework with on-policy distillation: student generates reasoning trajectories based on acoustic perception while teacher provides token-level guidance using matched textual inputs. Constructs three-tier symmetric corpus covering textual reasoning rendered to speech, audio-event reasoning in complex scenes, and spoken-dialogue reasoning with paralinguistic cues.

Result: Experiments on MMSU, MMAU, BIG Bench Audio, and MMAR show X³-OPD substantially improves audio-grounded reasoning and chain-of-thought quality while preserving existing capabilities under domain shift.

Conclusion: X³-OPD effectively bridges the reasoning gap between audio-language and text-based models by transferring capabilities through cross-modal distillation beyond textually recoverable content to reasoning grounded in non-linguistic events and context.

Abstract: While large audio-language models have achieved remarkable progress in auditory perception, they still lag behind text-based large language models in deep logical reasoning, primarily due to the scarcity of high-quality audio reasoning data. To bridge this gap, we propose X$^3$-OPD, a cross-modal on-policy distillation framework that transfers reasoning capabilities from a powerful text teacher to an audio-language student. During training, the student generates reasoning trajectories conditioned on its own acoustic perception, while the teacher provides token-level guidance using matched textual inputs and verified answers. We further construct a three-tier symmetric corpus covering textual reasoning rendered into speech, audio-event reasoning grounded in complex acoustic scenes, and spoken-dialogue reasoning involving paralinguistic cues. This design extends cross-modal distillation beyond textually recoverable content to reasoning grounded in non-linguistic events, prosody, and conversational context. Experiments on MMSU, MMAU, BIG Bench Audio, and MMAR demonstrate that X$^3$-OPD substantially improves audio-grounded reasoning and chain-of-thought quality while largely preserving the model's existing capabilities under domain shift.

</details>


### [233] [Graph Learning on Ensembles of Cyclic Peptides: An Investigation of Molecular Ensemble Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21561)
*Aaron Feller,Kris Deibler,Maxim Secor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EnsembleEGNN: a foundation model that encodes molecular conformational ensembles using equivariant graph neural networks and set attention, pretrained on cyclic peptide ensembles, achieving better property prediction than sequence-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current molecular property prediction often uses single conformations, but molecules exist as conformational ensembles in solution. Encoding these ensembles could improve prediction accuracy, especially for cyclic peptides where conformational diversity is important.

Method: EnsembleEGNN encodes each conformer with shared EGNN layers, then pools representations using a Set Attention Block. Pretrained on CREMP dataset using multi-task self-supervised objectives: masked token recovery, noisy-coordinate reconstruction, and pairwise distance reconstruction.

Result: Without pretraining, EnsembleEGNN fails (R²=0.005). Pretrained model achieves R²=0.477 and Pearson r=0.699, outperforming BERT baseline (R²=0.439, r=0.667). When co-trained with BERT encoder, hybrid model reaches R²=0.538 and r=0.737.

Conclusion: Encoding conformational ensembles into a single thermodynamically informed embedding improves cyclic-peptide property prediction, demonstrating the value of ensemble-based representations over single-conformation approaches.

Abstract: Molecular property prediction from structure often uses a single representative conformation, even though many molecules exist as conformational ensembles in solution. We introduce EnsembleEGNN, a molecular ensemble foundation model that encodes an ensemble by first encoding each conformer with shared Equivariant Graph Neural Network (EGNN) layers, then pooling the resulting conformer representations with a Set Attention Block. We pretrain the model on CREMP, a cyclic peptide ensemble dataset, using a multi-task self-supervised objective combining masked token recovery, noisy-coordinate reconstruction, and pairwise distance reconstruction. On the CREMP-CycPeptMPDB dataset, training EnsembleEGNN from scratch fails entirely ($R^2=0.005$). However, the pretrained model reaches $R^2=0.477$ and Pearson $r=0.699$, outperforming the sequence-only BERT baseline ($R^2=0.439$, Pearson $r=0.667$). When EnsembleEGNN is co-trained end-to-end with the BERT sequence encoder, the hybrid model improves further to $R^2=0.538$ and Pearson $r=0.737$. These results demonstrate that encoding conformational ensembles into a single thermodynamically informed embedding improves cyclic-peptide property prediction.

</details>


### [234] [Beyond Sufficiency: Time Series Explanation with Counterfactual Necessity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21573)
*Hongnan Ma,Yiwei Shi,Mengyue Yang,Weiru Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TimePNS introduces a necessity-aware framework for explaining time-series classifiers by combining sufficiency-oriented explanations with counterfactual necessity assessment to identify truly decision-critical subsequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time-series explanation methods focus primarily on sufficiency (identifying subsequences that preserve predictions) but fail to capture necessity (whether those subsequences are essential for the model's decision). This allows spurious subsequences to receive high importance scores even when they're not actually necessary for the model's prediction.

Method: TimePNS uses a two-stage framework: Stage I learns an identifiable causal generative process with sufficiency-oriented explanation masks, while Stage II performs counterfactual interventions on temporal factors to derive necessity signals. These signals supervise a temporal gate that refines initial explanations by suppressing non-essential components and emphasizing counterfactually necessary ones, inspired by Pearl's counterfactual notion of necessity.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world time-series benchmarks demonstrate that TimePNS more accurately identifies decision-critical subsequences and consistently improves sufficiency-necessity trade-offs compared to strong baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed TimePNS framework successfully addresses the limitation of purely sufficiency-oriented explanations by incorporating necessity assessment through counterfactual interventions, leading to more faithful explanations that better capture the actual decision-making process of time-series classifiers.

Abstract: Faithful explanations of time-series classifiers should identify subsequences that are not only sufficient to preserve a black-box model's prediction, but also necessary for maintaining it. However, existing sufficiency-oriented methods can assign high importance to spurious subsequences that support the prediction without being essential to the model's decision. We introduce \textbf{TimePNS}, a necessity-aware framework for time-series explanation. Inspired by Pearl's counterfactual notion of necessity, TimePNS assesses whether a temporal factor is necessary by intervening on it and measuring whether the original prediction is disrupted. The framework adopts a two-stage design. Stage I learns an identifiable causal generative process together with a sufficiency-oriented explanation mask. Stage II performs counterfactual interventions on temporal factors to derive necessity signals, which supervise a temporal gate that refines the initial explanation by suppressing non-essential components and emphasizing counterfactually necessary ones. Experiments on synthetic and real-world time-series benchmarks show that TimePNS more accurately identifies decision-critical subsequences and consistently improves sufficiency-necessity trade-offs over strong baselines.

</details>


### [235] [Expanding Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21585)
*Sophia Tang,Pranam Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EFlows (Expanding Generative Flows) define flows between distributions of increasing dimensionality using expanding interpolants, while EFMs (Expanding Flow Maps) distill these into efficient few-step generative models for variable-size generation across continuous and discrete spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing flow-based generative models are constrained to fixed dimensions or fixed sequence lengths, limiting their applicability to problems requiring variable-size outputs like variable-length sequences or graphs of different sizes.

Method: Introduce Expanding Generative Flows (EFlows) that use expanding interpolants to grow state dimensionality by augmenting with conditional noise. Propose Expanding Flow Maps (EFMs) that factor the expansion into two learnable operations: an expand operator (adds new coordinates/tokens) and a transport map (pushes expanded state forward). The framework extends to both continuous spaces and discrete simplex for variable-size graph/sequence generation.

Result: EFlows and EFMs provide a principled framework for settings where output size is a learned, controllable degree of freedom, enabling variable-size generation while recovering existing fixed-size models as special cases.

Conclusion: The proposed framework generalizes flow-based generative models to handle variable dimensions/lengths, offering a unified approach for controllable variable-size generation across both continuous and discrete modalities.

Abstract: Flow-based generative models have enabled remarkable progress in fast and controllable generation across continuous and discrete state spaces, yet existing parameterizations are constrained to fixed dimensions or fixed sequence lengths. Here, we introduce Expanding Generative Flows (EFlows), which define flows between distributions of increasing dimensionality along an expanding interpolant that grows the state by augmenting it with conditional noise. Building on this construction, we propose Expanding Flow Maps (EFMs), a new class of flow maps that distill the expanding interpolant into efficient few-step generative models. Each EFM factors the map between any two timesteps into two learnable operations: an expand operator, which augments the state space with new coordinates or tokens conditioned on the current state, and a transport map, which pushes the expanded state forward along the interpolant. Composing these operators yields a single map that jointly expands and denoises the state, recovering existing fixed-canvas flows and flow maps as the special case in which the expand operator is the identity. We further extend the framework to the discrete simplex, enabling variable-size graph generation and variable-length sequence generation. Across both continuous and discrete modalities, we establish EFlows and EFMs as a principled framework for settings in which output size is itself a learned, controllable degree of freedom.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [236] [pAI-Econ-claude: A Gated Human-in-the-Loop Multi-Agent Architecture for AI-Assisted Economic Theory Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21268)
*Chen Zhu,Xiaolu Wang,Weilong Zhang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Gated human-in-the-loop multi-agent architecture improves reliability of LLM-based economic theory development when no automatic correctness verification exists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents in social science research lack cheap, task-complete, machine-readable correctness signals, creating reliability problems for multi-agent systems in economics where no component can certify final results.

Method: pAI-Econ-claude: gated human-in-the-loop multi-agent architecture with shared workspace for inspectable intermediate records, specialized gates to diagnose targeted failure modes and recommend loopbacks without certifying correctness, and human checkpoints retaining authority over costly-to-reverse decisions.

Result: Evaluation on five economic-theory tasks: two blinded evaluators agreed on all pairwise rankings, preferring gated architecture in 4 tasks and baseline in 1. Mean failure severity fell from 1.58 to 1.16, overall usefulness rose from 2.60 to 3.10. Largest gain when reality check rejected false market-structure premise and proof review prompted revision of false welfare claim.

Conclusion: Gated oversight improves auditability of AI-assisted economic theory without substituting for formal verification, and allocation of irreversible human judgment is more informative design variable than pure agent autonomy. Scaffolding can sometimes compress economically important mechanisms too aggressively.

Abstract: In many social-science research tasks, such as economics, LLM-based agents must produce outputs for which no cheap, task-complete, machine-readable correctness signal exists. This creates a distinctive reliability problem for multi-agent systems: how should generation, critique, coordination, and human judgment be organized when no component can certify the final result? We address this problem through pAI-Econ-claude, a gated, human-in-the-loop multi-agent architecture for AI-assisted economic theory development. Agents coordinate through a shared workspace of inspectable intermediate records; specialized gates diagnose targeted failure modes and recommend loopbacks without certifying correctness; and human checkpoints retain authority over decisions that are costly to reverse. We evaluate the architecture on five matched economic-theory tasks against an ungated baseline. Two evaluators blinded to configuration agreed on all five pairwise rankings, preferring the gated architecture in four tasks and the baseline in one. Mean failure severity fell from 1.58 to 1.16, while overall usefulness rose from 2.60 to 3.10. The largest observed gain occurred when a reality check rejected a false market-structure premise and a proof review prompted revision of a false welfare claim. The negative case shows that scaffolding can also compress an economically important mechanism too aggressively. The results support a bounded claim: gated oversight improves the auditability of AI-assisted economic theory without substituting for formal verification, and the allocation of irreversible human judgment is a more informative design variable than pure agent autonomy. The workflow is publicly available at https://github.com/maxwell2732/pAI-Econ-claude.

</details>


### [237] [FedAgentKE: Federated Semantic Knowledge Evolution for Heterogeneous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21361)
*Weihao Li,Jun Bai,Ziyang Song*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: FedAgentKE is a federated framework that enables heterogeneous LLM-based agents to collaboratively evolve reasoning knowledge across different frameworks without sharing raw data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based agent frameworks operate in isolation, with fragmented local experiences that limit cross-framework knowledge transfer and collaborative reasoning evolution, despite agents increasingly relying on reasoning, tool use, and iterative execution.

Method: A lightweight federated framework that enables distributed agent frameworks to collaboratively evolve transferable reasoning abstractions through iterative semantic knowledge distillation, aggregation, and adaptation without sharing raw reasoning trajectories.

Result: Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements under both cross-framework and cross-task settings, showing the framework's effectiveness for collaborative knowledge evolution.

Conclusion: FedAgentKE highlights the potential of federated semantic knowledge evolution for future collaborative agent ecosystems, enabling better knowledge sharing across heterogeneous agents while maintaining privacy.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents increasingly rely on reasoning, tool use, and iterative execution, yet existing agent frameworks still operate largely in isolation. While recent memory-based agent systems improve individual agents through local retrieval and workflow reuse, local experiences remain fragmented across isolated agent frameworks, limiting cross-framework knowledge transfer and collaborative reasoning evolution. We propose FedAgentKE, a lightweight framework for Federated Semantic Knowledge Evolution across heterogeneous agents. FedAgentKE enables distributed agent frameworks to collaboratively evolve transferable reasoning abstractions through iterative semantic knowledge distillation, aggregation, and adaptation without sharing raw reasoning trajectories. Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements under both cross-framework and cross-task settings, highlighting the potential of federated semantic knowledge evolution for future collaborative agent ecosystems.

</details>
